36-220 Engineering Statistics & Quality Control · Fall 2026

Exam 2, from zero to reject H₀.

Everything from lecture 5W through 9M, taught as if you have never been to class. Every line of the formula sheet is decoded, every homework and worksheet problem is worked, and it ends with a full practice exam.

Exam
Wednesday, Nov 4

Covers lectures 5W through 9M. Covariance, correlation and PCA (9W, 9F) are not on it.

You get
The formula sheet

So the job is reading it fluently, not memorizing it. Module 11 drills every line.

Question mix
A bit more conceptual

The instructor says slightly more conceptual than Exam 1, but you still compute p-values, power, CIs and control limits.

By hand means
Algebraic c.d.f.s

Mostly the exponential, plus test statistics: t, U, Dn, X², control limits, Cpk. No tables needed.

The one idea behind half the exam

Almost every inference question on this exam is the same equation, \(F_Y(y;\theta) - q = 0\), solved for a different unknown. Hold \(\theta\) fixed and solve for \(y\): you get the edge of a rejection region (hypothesis testing). Hold \(y\) fixed and solve for \(\theta\): you get the end of a confidence interval. Module 2 builds this from scratch. Once it clicks, Modules 3 to 5 are bookkeeping.

How to use this guide

  • Go in order. Each module assumes the ones before it. Modules open one at a time; use the list on the left (or "Contents" on a phone).
  • Keep your formula sheet next to you. Every formula here carries a grey Section · line tag that tells you where it sits on the sheet. Find it there every time. On exam day your eyes will already know where to go.
  • Do the "Check yourself" problems before opening the solution. Write on paper. The solutions show every step.
  • Mark each module complete with the button at its bottom. Your progress and the checklist below stay saved in this browser.

Two typos on your formula sheet

Sample mean: the sheet prints \(\overline X = \sum_{i=1}^n X_i\). It should be \(\overline X = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n X_i\).

CUSUM: the sheet says out of control if \(\mathrm{SL}_i < h\sigma\). Since SL is never positive, it should be \(\mathrm{SL}_i < -h\sigma\).

Both come up again in the modules where they matter.

Module 1 · Exam 1 material, kept light · about 25 min

Quick review: distributions, mean and variance, statistics

This is the top of the left column of your formula sheet, from "Probability Mass Functions" down through "Exponential". Exam 2 builds on all of it. Read each line once, make sure every symbol makes sense, and move on. The deep part (c.d.f.s used for inference) is Module 2.

After this module you can

  • Say the difference between a p.m.f. and a p.d.f., and what a c.d.f. is.
  • Read E, V and σ off the sheet, and use E(aX+b) and V(aX+b).
  • Tell a statistic, its sampling distribution and its standard error apart.
  • Use E(X̄) = μ and V(X̄) = σ²/n, and fix the missing 1/n in the sample mean.
  • For Binomial, Poisson, Normal and Exponential: say when each shows up, its parameters, its mean and its variance.

In plain English

A random variable \(X\) is a number you will get from an experiment but do not know yet: the number of heads in 4 flips, the time until a part fails. Its distribution tells you which values are possible and how likely each one is. Capital \(X\) is the random variable. Lower-case \(x\) is a specific value it could take.

Everything in this module is a way to describe a distribution: a formula for the probabilities (p.m.f. or p.d.f.), a running total of probability (c.d.f.), and two summary numbers (mean and variance).

P.m.f. vs p.d.f.

If \(X\) can only take separate, countable values (0, 1, 2, ...), it is discrete and is described by a probability mass function (p.m.f.) \(p_X(x)\). The p.m.f. gives the probability of each exact value: \(p_X(x) = P(X = x)\).

If \(X\) can take any value in an interval (a time, a length, a weight), it is continuous and is described by a probability density function (p.d.f.) \(f_X(x)\). A density is not a probability. The probability of any single exact value is 0. You get probabilities from a p.d.f. by finding the area under it.

The cumulative distribution function (c.d.f.) works the same way for both: \(F_X(x) = P(X \le x)\), the total probability at or below \(x\). It starts at 0 on the far left, ends at 1 on the far right, and never goes down.

Probability Mass Functions · line 1

\[ 0 \le p_X(x) \le 1, \quad \sum_x p_X(x) = 1, \quad F_X(x) = \sum_{y \le x} p_Y(y) \]

"Each probability is between 0 and 1, they add to 1, and the c.d.f. at x is the sum of all the probabilities at or below x."

\(p_X(x)\)
the p.m.f.: \(P(X = x)\), the probability that \(X\) equals exactly \(x\)
\(\sum_x\)
add over every possible value of \(X\)
\(F_X(x)\)
the c.d.f.: \(P(X \le x)\)
\(y\)
a dummy counting variable that runs over all possible values up to \(x\)

Heads up: a subscript slip

The sheet writes \(p_Y(y)\) inside the c.d.f. sum (and \(f_Y(y)\) in the p.d.f. block). Read it as \(p_X(y)\) and \(f_X(y)\): it is the same function as \(p_X\), with \(y\) as the dummy variable. It has nothing to do with the statistic \(Y\) used later on the sheet.

Probability Mass Functions · line 2

\[ F_X^{-1}(q) = \inf\{x : F_X(x) \ge q\} \]

"The inverse c.d.f. at q is the smallest x whose c.d.f. has reached q."

\(q\)
a quantile level, a probability between 0 and 1
\(F_X^{-1}(q)\)
the value of \(x\) with a fraction \(q\) of the probability at or below it. Taught in depth in Module 2.

Probability Mass Functions · line 3

\[ \mathbb{P}(a \le X \le b) = \sum_{x \in [a,b]} p_X(x) \]

"The probability X lands between a and b is the sum of the p.m.f. over the values from a to b."

\(a, b\)
the ends of the range you care about. For a discrete \(X\), include the end points if they are possible values.

Probability Density Functions · line 1

\[ f_X(x) \ge 0, \quad \int_x f_X(x)\,dx = 1, \quad F_X(x) = \int_{y \le x} f_Y(y)\,dy \]

"The density is never negative, its total area is 1, and the c.d.f. at x is the area under the density to the left of x."

\(f_X(x)\)
the p.d.f., a height. It can be bigger than 1. Only areas under it are probabilities.
\(\int_x\)
integrate over every possible value of \(X\)
\(F_X(x)\)
the c.d.f., \(P(X \le x)\)

Probability Density Functions · line 2

\[ F_X^{-1}(q) = x \]

"Given a quantile q, the inverse c.d.f. returns the x where the c.d.f. equals q."

how
set \(F_X(x) = q\) and solve for \(x\). See Module 2.

Probability Density Functions · line 3

\[ f_X(x) = \frac{d}{dx}F_X(x) \qquad \mathbb{P}(a \le X \le b) = \int_a^b f_X(x)\,dx \]

"The density is the slope of the c.d.f. The probability of landing between a and b is the area under the density from a to b."

\(\frac{d}{dx}F_X\)
differentiate the c.d.f. to get the p.d.f. Integrate the p.d.f. to get the c.d.f.
shortcut
\(\int_a^b f_X(x)\,dx = F_X(b) - F_X(a)\). Any interval probability is a difference of two c.d.f. values.

Mean, variance and standard deviation

Characterizing Distributions · Expected Value

\[ \mathbb{E}(X) = \sum_x x\,p_X(x) \ \text{ or } \int_x x f_X(x)\,dx \]

"The expected value is each possible value times its probability, added up (or integrated)."

\(\mathbb{E}(X)\)
the mean \(\mu\) of the distribution: the long-run average of \(X\) over many repeats. It is the balance point of the p.m.f. or p.d.f.

Characterizing Distributions · Variance and Standard Deviation

\[ \mathbb{V}(X) = \sigma^2 = \mathbb{E}(X^2) - (\mathbb{E}(X))^2 \qquad \sigma = \sqrt{\mathbb{V}(X)} \]

"The variance is the mean of X squared minus the square of the mean. The standard deviation is its square root."

\(\mathbb{V}(X)\), \(\sigma^2\)
the variance: the average squared distance of \(X\) from its mean. Units are squared.
\(\sigma\)
the standard deviation: a typical distance from the mean, in the same units as \(X\)
\(\mathbb{E}(X^2)\)
the mean of \(X^2\): \(\sum_x x^2 p_X(x)\) or \(\int x^2 f_X(x)\,dx\)

Characterizing Distributions · last line

\[ \mathbb{E}(aX+b) = a\mathbb{E}(X) + b \qquad \mathbb{V}(aX+b) = a^2\mathbb{V}(X) \]

"Scaling and shifting X scales and shifts the mean. The variance only feels the scaling, squared."

\(a, b\)
fixed numbers (constants), such as a unit conversion
why no \(b\) in \(\mathbb{V}\)
adding \(b\) slides the whole distribution over without changing its spread
why \(a^2\)
variance is in squared units, so a factor of \(a\) in \(X\) becomes \(a^2\). The SD scales by \(|a|\).

Statistics, sampling distributions, standard error

Three words that get mixed up

Statistic. Any number you compute from your data, such as the sample mean \(\overline X\). Before you collect the data it is random, so it is a random variable. The sheet calls a generic statistic \(Y\).

Sampling distribution. The distribution of that statistic: the values \(Y\) would take if you repeated the whole experiment (all \(n\) data) many times. It has its own mean \(\mathbb{E}(Y)\) and variance \(\mathbb{V}(Y)\).

Standard error. The standard deviation of the sampling distribution, \(\mathrm{se}(Y) = \sqrt{\mathbb{V}(Y)}\). It tells you how much the statistic wobbles from one experiment to the next. The SD \(\sigma\) is the spread of single data; the standard error is the spread of the statistic.

Statistics · Sample mean

\[ \overline X = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n X_i \]

"The sample mean is the sum of the n data divided by n."

\(X_i\)
the \(i\)-th datum, for \(i = 1, \dots, n\)
\(n\)
the sample size: how many data you have
\(\overline X\)
the sample mean, a statistic that estimates \(\mu\)

Heads up: typo on the sheet

The sheet prints \(\overline X = \sum_{i=1}^n X_i\). The \(\frac{1}{n}\) is missing. The correct formula is the one above. Without \(\frac1n\) you get the total \(X_+\), a different statistic that the sheet uses for the Binomial and Poisson.

Statistics · Sample standard deviation

\[ S = \left[\frac{1}{n-1}\sum_{i=1}^n (X_i - \overline X)^2\right]^{1/2} \]

"Square each datum's distance from the sample mean, add them, divide by n minus 1, take the square root."

\(S\)
the sample standard deviation, a statistic that estimates \(\sigma\). \(S^2\) is the sample variance.
\(n-1\)
divide by \(n-1\), not \(n\). Using \(\overline X\) in place of the true \(\mu\) makes the squared distances a bit too small, and \(n-1\) corrects for that.

Statistics · lines 3 and 4

\[ Y \text{ is drawn from a sampling distribution with } \mathbb{E}(Y), \mathbb{V}(Y) \qquad \mathrm{se}(Y) = \sqrt{\mathbb{V}(Y)} \]

"A statistic Y has its own distribution with a mean and variance. Its standard error is the square root of that variance."

\(Y\)
any statistic (for example \(\overline X\), \(X_+\), \(T\))
\(\mathrm{se}(Y)\)
standard error: the SD of the statistic

Statistics · last line

\[ E[\overline X] = \mathbb{E}(X) = \mu \qquad \mathbb{V}(\overline X) = \mathbb{V}(X)/n = \sigma^2/n \]

"On average the sample mean equals the true mean. Its variance is the data's variance divided by n."

\(\mu\)
the true (population) mean of one datum, \(\mathbb{E}(X)\)
\(\sigma^2\)
the true variance of one datum
consequence
\(\mathrm{se}(\overline X) = \sigma/\sqrt{n}\). Four times the data halves the standard error.

Worked example · Lecture 5M example (Exam 1 recap)

You collect \(n = 100\) i.i.d. data from an unknown distribution with \(\mu = 20\) and \(\sigma^2 = 4\). Find \(P(19.8 \le \overline X \le 20.2)\). (The normal c.d.f. values you need are \(F(20.2) = 0.841\) and \(F(19.8) = 0.159\).)

  1. Identify. The question is about the statistic \(\overline X\), so you need its sampling distribution, not the distribution of one datum.
  2. Mean and variance of \(\overline X\). \(\mathbb{E}(\overline X) = \mu = 20\). \(\mathbb{V}(\overline X) = \sigma^2/n = 4/100 = 0.04\). So \(\mathrm{se}(\overline X) = \sqrt{0.04} = 0.2\).
  3. Shape. With \(n = 100\), the central limit theorem says \(\overline X\) is approximately normal: \(\overline X \approx N(20, 0.04)\).
  4. Interval probability. \(P(19.8 \le \overline X \le 20.2) = F(20.2) - F(19.8)\). Both ends sit exactly one standard error (0.2) from 20.
  5. Solve. \(0.841 - 0.159 = 0.683\). This is the area in the second diagram above.

Answer: about 0.683. One standard error either side of the mean holds about 68% of the sampling distribution.

The four named distributions

NameTypeShows up whenParametersMeanVariance
Binomial \(\mathrm{Binom}(k,p)\)discretecount of successes in \(k\) independent yes/no trials\(k\) trials, \(p\) success prob.\(kp\)\(kp(1-p)\)
Poisson \(\mathrm{Pois}(\lambda)\)discretecount of events in a fixed time or space, no upper limit\(\lambda\), the expected count\(\lambda\)\(\lambda\)
Normal \(N(\mu,\sigma^2)\)continuousmeasurements that pile up symmetrically around a center; sample means (CLT)\(\mu\) center, \(\sigma^2\) spread\(\mu\)\(\sigma^2\)
Exponential \(\mathrm{Exp}(\theta)\)continuouswaiting times and lifetimes (positive, skewed right)\(\theta\), the mean\(\theta\)\(\theta^2\)

Binomial · lines 1 to 3

\[ \begin{aligned} &X \sim \mathrm{Binom}(k,p) \quad \text{Bernoulli: } k = 1 \\ &p_X(x) = \binom{k}{x} p^x (1-p)^{k-x}, \quad x \in \{0,1,\dots,k\},\ p \in [0,1] \\ &\binom{k}{x} = k!/[x!(k-x)!] \quad \mathbb{E}(X) = kp \quad \mathbb{V}(X) = kp(1-p) \end{aligned} \]

"X counts successes in k trials, each a success with probability p."

\(k\)
number of trials inside ONE observation. A Bernoulli is the one-trial case, \(k = 1\) (X is 0 or 1).
\(p\)
probability of success on each trial
\(\binom{k}{x}\)
"k choose x": the number of orders in which \(x\) successes can fall among \(k\) trials
\(p^x(1-p)^{k-x}\)
the probability of one particular order with \(x\) successes and \(k-x\) failures

Poisson · lines 1 and 2

\[ X \sim \mathrm{Pois}(\lambda) \qquad p_X(x) = \frac{\lambda^x}{x!}e^{-\lambda}, \quad x \in \{0,1,\dots\},\ \lambda > 0 \qquad \mathbb{E}(X) = \mathbb{V}(X) = \lambda \]

"X is a count with no ceiling. Its mean and its variance are both lambda."

\(\lambda\)
the expected count (the rate times the length of the window)
\(x!\)
\(x\) factorial, \(x(x-1)\cdots 1\), with \(0! = 1\)

Normal · lines 1 and 2

\[ X \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^2) \qquad f_X(x) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^2}}e^{-(x-\mu)^2/(2\sigma^2)}, \quad x, \mu \in (-\infty,\infty),\ \sigma > 0 \]
\[ \mathbb{E}(X) = \mu;\ \mathbb{V}(X) = \sigma^2 \]

"The bell curve centered at mu with spread sigma."

\(\mu\)
the center (mean)
\(\sigma^2\)
the variance. Note the second argument of \(\mathcal N\) is the variance, not the SD.
by hand?
No. The normal c.d.f. has no formula you can integrate. On the exam a normal c.d.f. value is given to you.

Exponential · line 1

\[ X \sim \mathrm{Exp}(\theta) \qquad f_X(x) = \frac{1}{\theta}e^{-x/\theta}, \quad x \ge 0,\ \theta > 0 \qquad \mathbb{E}(X) = \theta \quad \mathbb{V}(X) = \theta^2 \]

"A waiting time with mean theta; its SD is also theta."

\(\theta\)
the mean waiting time (a scale parameter, same units as \(X\))
by hand?
Yes. This is the one named distribution whose c.d.f. you can integrate on paper: \(F_X(x) = 1 - e^{-x/\theta}\). It is NOT printed on the sheet. Module 2 derives it.

Heads up: the letter k

In the Binomial, \(k\) is the number of trials in one observation. Later on the sheet, \(k\) means something else: the TOTAL count in the chi-square tests (and the subgroup size in the p-chart), and the CUSUM slack in \(k\sigma\). Always ask which block you are in.

Each distribution block ends with one or two "Sufficient statistic" lines. Those are what every test and confidence interval uses, so they get full treatment in Module 2.

Worked example · HW7 Q3(a)

A process is declared out of control if any point on an \(\overline X\) chart falls outside the 3σ control limits. Each in-control sample has probability \(p = 0.0027\) of falling outside (a normal c.d.f. value, given). Treat each of the next 50 samples as a Binomial trial and let \(Y\) be the number of false alarms. What is the probability of at least one false alarm in the next 50 samples?

  1. Identify. 50 independent yes/no trials with the same success probability: \(Y \sim \mathrm{Binom}(k = 50, p = 0.0027)\). Here "success" means a false alarm.
  2. Use the complement. "At least one" is everything except zero: \(P(Y \ge 1) = 1 - P(Y = 0)\). This avoids adding 50 terms.
  3. Plug into the p.m.f. \(P(Y = 0) = \binom{50}{0}(0.0027)^0(1 - 0.0027)^{50} = 0.9973^{50} = 0.8736\).
  4. Solve. \(P(Y \ge 1) = 1 - 0.8736 = 0.126\).
  5. Sanity check. \(\mathbb{E}(Y) = kp = 50 \times 0.0027 = 0.135\), a bit above the answer, as it should be (it counts multiple alarms too).

Answer: about 0.126, so a 12.6% chance of at least one false alarm in 50 in-control samples.

Check yourself · HW6 Q3(c)

A random variable has c.d.f. \(F_X(x;\theta) = x^\theta\) for \(x \in [0,1]\), \(\theta > 0\). What is its p.d.f.?

Hint

Use the sheet line \(f_X(x) = \frac{d}{dx}F_X(x)\).

Full solution
  1. A p.d.f. is the derivative of the c.d.f.
  2. \(\frac{d}{dx}x^\theta = \theta x^{\theta-1}\) by the power rule.
  3. Outside \([0,1]\) the c.d.f. is flat (0 or 1), so the density is 0 there.

\(f_X(x) = \theta x^{\theta - 1}\) for \(x \in [0,1]\), and 0 otherwise.

Check yourself · Original practice (5F worksheet data)

Ten counts are observed: 3, 4, 3, 3, 4, 3, 3, 4, 3, 10. Compute the sample mean \(\bar x\) and the sample standard deviation \(s\).

Full solution
  1. Sum. Six 3s, three 4s and one 10: \(18 + 12 + 10 = 40\).
  2. Mean. Remember the \(\frac1n\) the sheet forgot: \(\bar x = 40/10 = 4.0\).
  3. Squared deviations. Each 3 gives \((3-4)^2 = 1\), six of them: 6. Each 4 gives 0. The 10 gives \((10-4)^2 = 36\). Total 42.
  4. Divide by \(n - 1 = 9\). \(s^2 = 42/9 = 4.667\).
  5. Square root. \(s = \sqrt{4.667} = 2.16\).

\(\bar x = 4.0\), \(s = 2.16\). Notice how much the single 10 inflates \(s\).

Check yourself · Original practice

A temperature sensor reading \(X\) (°C) has mean 20 and standard deviation 2. (a) You report \(Y = 1.8X + 32\) in °F. Find \(\mathbb{E}(Y)\), \(\mathbb{V}(Y)\) and the SD of \(Y\). (b) You average \(n = 16\) independent readings in °C. Find \(\mathbb{E}(\overline X)\) and \(\mathrm{se}(\overline X)\).

Full solution
  1. (a) Mean. \(\mathbb{E}(1.8X + 32) = 1.8(20) + 32 = 68\) °F.
  2. (a) Variance. \(\mathbb{V}(1.8X + 32) = 1.8^2 \times 2^2 = 3.24 \times 4 = 12.96\). The +32 does not matter.
  3. (a) SD. \(\sqrt{12.96} = 3.6\) °F, which is \(1.8 \times 2\).
  4. (b) Mean of \(\overline X\). \(\mathbb{E}(\overline X) = \mu = 20\) °C.
  5. (b) Standard error. \(\mathbb{V}(\overline X) = \sigma^2/n = 4/16 = 0.25\), so \(\mathrm{se}(\overline X) = 0.5\) °C.

(a) 68, 12.96, 3.6. (b) 20 and 0.5.

Common mistakes

  • Treating a p.d.f. height as a probability. For continuous \(X\), \(P(X = x) = 0\); probabilities are areas.
  • Copying the sheet's sample mean without the \(\frac1n\).
  • Dividing by \(n\) in \(S\). It is \(n - 1\).
  • Writing \(\mathbb{V}(aX + b) = a\mathbb{V}(X) + b\). The shift drops out and the scale is squared.
  • Mixing up \(\sigma\) (spread of one datum) with \(\sigma/\sqrt n\) (standard error of \(\overline X\)).
  • Reading \(\mathcal{N}(\mu, \sigma^2)\) with \(\sigma\) in the second slot. The sheet puts the variance there.

Quick check

A p.d.f. can take values bigger than 1.

True. A density is a height, not a probability. Only its total area must be 1. Example: \(\mathrm{Exp}(\theta = 0.5)\) has \(f_X(0) = 1/0.5 = 2\).

For a continuous X, \(P(X = 3) = f_X(3)\).

False. \(P(X = 3) = 0\) for any continuous \(X\). The area over a single point is zero.

\(\mathbb{V}(2X + 5) = 4\mathbb{V}(X) + 5\).

False. \(\mathbb{V}(2X + 5) = 4\mathbb{V}(X)\). Shifting does not change spread.

The standard error of \(\overline X\) is \(\sigma/\sqrt n\).

True. \(\mathrm{se} = \sqrt{\mathbb{V}(\overline X)} = \sqrt{\sigma^2/n} = \sigma/\sqrt n\).

The c.d.f. of a discrete X is a smooth curve.

False. It is a staircase: flat between possible values, jumping by \(p_X(x)\) at each one.

X ~ Pois(4). What are its mean and variance?

Both 4. For a Poisson, \(\mathbb{E}(X) = \mathbb{V}(X) = \lambda\).

X ~ Exp(θ = 2). What are its mean and standard deviation?

Both 2. \(\mathbb{E}(X) = \theta = 2\) and \(\mathbb{V}(X) = \theta^2 = 4\), so the SD is 2.

In Binom(k, p), k is the number of data in your sample.

False. \(k\) is the number of trials inside one observation. The number of observations is \(n\). This matters for the sufficient statistic \(X_+ \sim \mathrm{Binom}(nk, p)\) in Module 2.

If you see this on the exam

  • "Find the p.d.f." from a c.d.f. → differentiate. "Find the c.d.f." from a p.d.f. → integrate from the left end of the domain up to \(x\).
  • "\(P(a \le X \le b)\)" → \(F(b) - F(a)\) (continuous), or add the p.m.f. from \(a\) to \(b\) (discrete).
  • A unit change \(aX + b\) → mean becomes \(a\mu + b\), variance becomes \(a^2\sigma^2\).
  • Sample mean on the sheet → add the missing \(\frac1n\).
  • Anything about \(\overline X\) → \(\mathbb{E}(\overline X) = \mu\), \(\mathrm{se}(\overline X) = \sigma/\sqrt n\).
  • Counts with a fixed number of trials → Binomial. Counts with no ceiling → Poisson. Waiting times → Exponential. Symmetric measurements or sample means → Normal.

Module 2 · Lectures 5W, 5F (with the 5M recap) · about 30 min

C.d.f.s, inverse c.d.f.s, sufficient statistics and the fundamental equation

Every p-value, rejection region, power calculation and confidence interval on this exam is a c.d.f. read forwards or backwards. This module gives you the one equation behind all of them, \(F_Y(y;\theta) - q = 0\), and the pieces it is built from.

After this module you can

  • Explain a c.d.f. as a "probability so far" curve and derive the exponential c.d.f. \(1 - e^{-x/\theta}\) in ten seconds.
  • Find an inverse c.d.f. (quantile) by solving \(F(x) = q\), including \(x = -\theta\ln(1-q)\) for the exponential.
  • Read both inverse c.d.f. lines on the sheet, and say why the discrete one needs "inf".
  • Say what a statistic, its sampling distribution and a sufficient statistic are, and read all five sufficient-statistic lines.
  • Decode \(F_Y(y;\theta) - q = 0\) and use it two ways: fix \(\theta\) to get a rejection-region boundary, fix \(y\) to get a confidence bound.

In plain English

A c.d.f. turns a value into a probability: "how much probability is at or below this point?" An inverse c.d.f. turns a probability back into a value: "which point has this much probability below it?"

Inference works with one number computed from the data, called a statistic. If you know the c.d.f. of that statistic, you can ask two questions with the same equation. Testing: assume a value of the parameter, and find which values of the statistic would be too extreme to believe. Confidence intervals: take the value of the statistic you actually saw, and find which parameter values would make it unsurprising. The instructor's 5W summary: tests and intervals "both require that we know (or can estimate) a sampling distribution!"

Vocabulary

SymbolNameWhat it means
\(X\), \(x\)random variable, valueone datum before you see it (\(X\)) and a specific number it could equal (\(x\))
\(\theta\)parameterthe unknown number that sets the distribution's shape or location, "the value of which we're trying to infer" (5W)
\(F_X(x)\)c.d.f.\(P(X \le x)\), probability at or below \(x\)
\(q\)quantile levela probability in \([0,1]\) that you choose, such as \(\alpha\), \(1-\alpha\) or \(\alpha/2\)
\(F_X^{-1}(q)\)inverse c.d.f., quantilethe \(x\) with a fraction \(q\) of the probability at or below it
\(Y\), \(y\)statistic, its valueone number computed from all \(n\) data (for example \(Y = \overline X\)), and a numerical value of it
\(y_{\rm obs}\)observed valuethe value of \(Y\) you actually computed from your data
\(F_Y(y;\theta)\)c.d.f. of the statistic\(P(Y \le y)\) when the parameter equals \(\theta\). The semicolon separates the input \(y\) from the parameter.
\(\theta_0\)null valuethe parameter value assumed by the null hypothesis \(H_0\)
\(y_{\rm RR}\)rejection-region boundarythe edge of the set of \(y\) values for which you would reject \(H_0\)
\(\alpha\)levelthe probability of rejecting \(H_0\) when it is true, usually 0.05

The c.d.f. is a "probability so far" curve

Walk along the number line from left to right and keep a running total of the probability you have passed. That running total is the c.d.f., \(F_X(x) = P(X \le x)\). The instructor (5W) lists its three properties:

  • \(F_X(-\infty) = 0\): nothing has been passed yet.
  • \(F_X(\infty) = 1\): everything has been passed.
  • \(F_X(x)\) is non-decreasing: a running total of probabilities can never go down.

For a discrete \(X\) it is a staircase whose step at \(x\) has height \(p_X(x)\). For a continuous \(X\) it is a smooth S-shaped (or rising) curve whose slope is the p.d.f.

Why this matters: a p-value is a tail area of the statistic's distribution, and every tail area is a c.d.f. value. The lower tail at \(y_{\rm obs}\) is \(F_Y(y_{\rm obs};\theta_0)\); the upper tail is \(1 - F_Y(y_{\rm obs};\theta_0)\). The instructor's 5W conclusion: "computing p-values means working with c.d.f.s."

Deriving the exponential c.d.f. (it is not on the sheet)

Of the four named distributions, only the exponential has a c.d.f. you can work out with pencil and paper. The sheet gives its p.d.f. only, so you must be able to integrate it fast.

\[ \begin{aligned} F_X(x;\theta) &= \int_0^x \frac{1}{\theta}e^{-y/\theta}\,dy \\ &= \Big[-e^{-y/\theta}\Big]_0^x \\ &= -e^{-x/\theta} - (-1) = 1 - e^{-x/\theta}, \quad x \ge 0 \end{aligned} \]

Start the integral at 0 because the exponential lives on \(x \ge 0\) (for \(x < 0\), \(F = 0\)). The antiderivative of \(\frac1\theta e^{-y/\theta}\) is \(-e^{-y/\theta}\): differentiate it and the chain rule brings down \(-\frac1\theta\), which cancels the minus sign. Check your answer: \(F(0) = 1 - 1 = 0\) and \(F(\infty) = 1 - 0 = 1\).

\[ 1 - F_X(x;\theta) = e^{-x/\theta} \]

The upper tail, \(P(X > x)\). You will use this for every upper-tailed p-value and power calculation with an exponential datum.

Heads up

\(F(x) = 1 - e^{-x/\theta}\) is not printed anywhere on the formula sheet. Memorize it, and know the three-line integral above in case you blank.

The inverse c.d.f.: read the curve backwards

The c.d.f. takes a value \(x\) and returns a probability. The inverse c.d.f. takes a probability \(q\) and returns the value \(x\) that has exactly that much probability at or below it. \(F^{-1}(0.5)\) is the median; \(F^{-1}(0.95)\) is the 95th percentile. The recipe from HW5 Q2: "one starts with \(F_X(x) = q\) ... and solves for \(x\)."

Probability Density Functions · Inverse c.d.f.

\[ \text{given quantile } q \in [0,1],\ F_X^{-1}(q) = x \]

"For a continuous X, the inverse c.d.f. at q is the x where the c.d.f. equals q."

\(q\)
the probability you start from, between 0 and 1
\(F_X^{-1}\)
the inverse function of the c.d.f. (it does NOT mean \(1/F_X\))
\(x\)
the answer: the unique point with \(F_X(x) = q\). A continuous c.d.f. rises without jumps, so it hits every \(q\) and there is always a solution.

Probability Mass Functions · Inverse c.d.f.

\[ \text{for quantile } q \in [0,1],\ F_X^{-1}(q) = \inf\{x : F_X(x) \ge q\} \]

"For a discrete X, the inverse c.d.f. at q is the smallest x at which the c.d.f. has reached at least q."

\(\{x : F_X(x) \ge q\}\)
the set of all \(x\) whose running total has reached \(q\) or more
\(\inf\)
"infimum": the smallest value of that set (its left end)
why needed
a staircase c.d.f. has two problems: inside a jump, NO \(x\) gives \(F(x) = q\) exactly; on a flat step, MANY \(x\) do. "Smallest \(x\) with \(F(x) \ge q\)" picks one answer in both cases.

The exponential inverse c.d.f.

\[ \begin{aligned} 1 - e^{-x/\theta} &= q \\ e^{-x/\theta} &= 1 - q \\ -x/\theta &= \ln(1-q) \\ x = F^{-1}(q;\theta) &= -\theta\ln(1-q) \end{aligned} \]

Four moves: set the c.d.f. equal to \(q\), isolate the exponential, take the natural log, multiply by \(-\theta\). Since \(1 - q < 1\), \(\ln(1-q)\) is negative, so \(x\) comes out positive.

\(q\)0.0250.050.50.950.975
\(F^{-1}(q;\theta) = -\theta\ln(1-q)\)\(0.0253\,\theta\)\(0.0513\,\theta\)\(0.693\,\theta\)\(2.996\,\theta\)\(3.689\,\theta\)

Note that \(\theta\) just scales every quantile. That is what "scale parameter" means.

A discrete example you will meet again

In the 5F worksheet the statistic is a Poisson total with \(Y \sim \mathrm{Pois}(50)\) under \(H_0\). Its c.d.f. values near the bottom 5% are \(F(38) = 0.047\) and \(F(39) = 0.065\). No integer has \(F = 0.05\) exactly, so \(F^{-1}(0.05) = \inf\{x : F(x) \ge 0.05\} = 39\). With a discrete statistic you usually cannot hit the level \(\alpha\) exactly.

Method: find an inverse c.d.f.

  1. Get the c.d.f. (integrate the p.d.f. from the left end of the domain if it is not given).
  2. Write \(F(x) = q\).
  3. Solve for \(x\) with algebra (logs for exponentials, roots for powers).
  4. Check: plug \(q = 0\) and \(q = 1\) (or a value you know) and confirm you land at the ends of the domain.
  5. Discrete \(X\): no algebra; scan the c.d.f. values and take the first \(x\) where \(F(x) \ge q\).

Worked example · HW5 Q1(a) and Q2(a), (b)

You sample one datum from the p.d.f. \(f_X(x;\theta) = \theta x^{\theta-1}\), \(x \in [0,1]\), \(\theta > 0\). (a) Find the c.d.f. \(F_X(x)\). (b) Find the inverse c.d.f. (c) For an upper-tail test of \(H_0: \theta = 1\) at \(\alpha = 0.05\), find the rejection-region boundary \(y_{\rm RR}\).

  1. c.d.f. Integrate from the left end of the domain, 0: \(F_X(x) = \int_0^x \theta y^{\theta-1}\,dy = \big[y^\theta\big]_0^x = x^\theta\) for \(x \in [0,1]\) (0 below, 1 above). Check: \(F(0) = 0\), \(F(1) = 1\).
  2. Inverse. Set \(x^\theta = q\). Raise both sides to the power \(1/\theta\): \(x = F_X^{-1}(q) = q^{1/\theta}\).
  3. Which \(q\)? An upper-tail test rejects for big values. You want the top 5% of the null distribution, so the boundary has 95% below it: \(q = 1 - \alpha = 0.95\).
  4. Solve. With \(\theta_0 = 1\): \(y_{\rm RR} = 0.95^{1/1} = 0.95\). Reject \(H_0\) if \(x_{\rm obs} > 0.95\).
  5. Sense check. Under \(H_0\), \(P(X > 0.95) = 1 - 0.95 = 0.05 = \alpha\). The boundary was set before seeing any data, as HW5 reminds you.

Answer: \(F_X(x) = x^\theta\); \(F_X^{-1}(q) = q^{1/\theta}\); \(y_{\rm RR} = 0.95\). (HW5's observed \(x_{\rm obs} = 0.98\) is above it, so that test rejects.)

Statistics and their sampling distributions

You rarely test with one datum. You collect \(n\) data and squeeze them into one number, the statistic \(Y\) (a sum, a mean, a t ratio). The test or interval is then about that one number. To say whether the observed \(y_{\rm obs}\) is surprising, you need to know how \(Y\) behaves over repeated experiments. That is the statistic's sampling distribution, and its c.d.f. \(F_Y\) is what goes into every formula on the right side of the sheet.

The distribution of one datum is the wrong thing to use. A mean of 20 data is much less variable than one datum, so a value that is ordinary for a single datum can be very unusual for the mean. The diagram shows this for exponential data.

What "sufficient" means

A sufficient statistic is a summary that keeps all of the information the sample has about \(\theta\). Once you know its value, the individual data tell you nothing more about \(\theta\). Example: for Poisson counts, the total \(X_+\) is sufficient for \(\lambda\). Two samples with the same total give the same evidence about \(\lambda\), no matter how the counts are spread across the observations.

The instructor calls these the "optimal statistics", and the 5F rule for picking a test statistic is: "best to choose the sufficient statistic for the parameter of interest, with known sampling distribution, if possible." The sheet hands you one for each family, together with its sampling distribution.

Binomial · Sufficient statistic

\[ X_+ = \sum_{i=1}^n X_i \sim \mathrm{Binom}(nk, p) \]

"The total number of successes across all n observations is Binomial with nk trials."

\(X_+\)
the sum of the data (the "+" means "add them all up")
\(n\)
number of observations
\(k\)
trials per observation, so \(nk\) trials in total
\(p\)
the parameter being inferred (\(\theta = p\))

Poisson · Sufficient statistic

\[ X_+ = \sum_{i=1}^n X_i \sim \mathrm{Pois}(n\lambda) \]

"The total count over n observations is Poisson with mean n lambda."

\(n\lambda\)
expected counts add: \(n\) windows each expecting \(\lambda\) events expect \(n\lambda\) in total

Normal · Sufficient statistic (σ known)

\[ \overline X \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu, \sigma^2/n) \]

"When sigma is known, the sample mean is normal with the same mean and variance sigma squared over n."

\(\mu\)
the parameter being inferred
\(\sigma^2/n\)
the variance of \(\overline X\); its standard error is \(\sigma/\sqrt n\)
use when
the problem tells you \(\sigma\) (5F: "What would change if we knew that \(\sigma^2 = 400\)?")

Normal · Sufficient statistic (σ unknown)

\[ T = (\overline X - \mu)/(S/\sqrt n) \sim t(n-1) \]

"When sigma is unknown, the standardized mean, using S in place of sigma, has a t distribution with n minus 1 degrees of freedom."

\(S\)
the sample standard deviation, standing in for the unknown \(\sigma\)
\(t(n-1)\)
the t distribution with \(n-1\) degrees of freedom: bell-shaped like \(\mathcal N(0,1)\) with heavier tails, because \(S\) adds extra wobble
\(\mu\)
in a test, plug in the null value \(\mu_0\). In 5F: \(T = (148 - 140)/(20/5) = 2\) with 24 degrees of freedom.

Heads up

Strictly, \(T\) is not a sufficient statistic (it contains the unknown \(\mu\)). It is a standardized version of \(\overline X\) whose distribution you know. The sheet's label is harmless; use it the same way.

Exponential · Sufficient statistic

\[ \overline X \sim \mathrm{Gamma}(n, \theta/n) \ \text{(shape-scale)} \]

"The mean of n exponential data has a Gamma distribution with shape n and scale theta over n."

shape \(n\)
controls the form: the larger \(n\), the more bell-shaped
scale \(\theta/n\)
stretches it. A Gamma(shape \(a\), scale \(s\)) has mean \(as\) and variance \(as^2\), so \(\overline X\) has mean \(n \cdot \theta/n = \theta\) and variance \(n(\theta/n)^2 = \theta^2/n\), which matches \(\mathbb{E}(\overline X) = \mu\) and \(\mathbb{V}(\overline X) = \sigma^2/n\).
\(n = 1\)
Gamma(1, \(\theta\)) is exactly \(\mathrm{Exp}(\theta)\). For one datum the sampling distribution is just the exponential, and its c.d.f. is \(1 - e^{-x/\theta}\). That is why by-hand exam problems use a single datum: the Gamma c.d.f. for \(n > 1\) needs software.
DataParameter \(\theta\)Statistic \(Y\)Sampling distributionc.d.f. by hand?
Binomial\(p\)\(X_+\)\(\mathrm{Binom}(nk,p)\)only tiny cases
Poisson\(\lambda\)\(X_+\)\(\mathrm{Pois}(n\lambda)\)only tiny cases
Normal, \(\sigma\) known\(\mu\)\(\overline X\)\(\mathcal N(\mu,\sigma^2/n)\)no, value given
Normal, \(\sigma\) unknown\(\mu\)\(T\)\(t(n-1)\)no, value given
Exponential\(\theta\)\(\overline X\)\(\mathrm{Gamma}(n,\theta/n)\)yes when \(n = 1\)

The fundamental equation

The 5W lecture is titled "The Fundamental Equation of Statistical Inference." Here it is.

\[ F_Y(y;\theta) - q = 0 \]

"The c.d.f. of my statistic, at the value y, with parameter theta, equals the quantile level q."

\(Y\)
"our chosen statistic (e.g., \(Y = \overline X\))", ideally the sufficient statistic
\(y\)
"a numerical value of that statistic"
\(\theta\)
"a parameter, the value of which we're trying to infer"
\(q\)
"a user-set quantile value": you pick it from \(\alpha\) using the sheet's tables
\(F_Y(\cdot\,;\cdot)\)
"the cumulative distribution function of Y", the c.d.f. of its sampling distribution

One equation, three quantities (\(y\), \(\theta\), \(q\)). You always set \(q\) yourself. Then you fix one of the other two and solve for the last one. The sheet prints both versions.

Hypothesis Testing · line 4

\[ \text{Solve } F_Y(y_{\rm RR};\theta_0) - q = 0 \text{ for } y_{\rm RR} \]

"Fix theta at its null value and solve for the value of the statistic that cuts off the rejection region."

\(\theta_0\)
fixed: the null value. Rejection regions are "defined assuming the null hypothesis is true."
\(y_{\rm RR}\)
the unknown: the rejection-region boundary. Same as \(y_{\rm RR} = F_Y^{-1}(q;\theta_0)\).
\(q\)
\(\alpha\) for lower-tailed, \(1-\alpha\) for upper-tailed, \(\alpha/2\) and \(1-\alpha/2\) for two-tailed (the power table, Module 4)

Confidence Intervals · line 4

\[ \text{Solve } F_Y(y_{\rm obs};\theta) - q = 0 \text{ for } \theta;\ q \text{ via the table} \]

"Fix the statistic at what I observed and solve for the parameter value that puts it at quantile q."

\(y_{\rm obs}\)
fixed: the observed value of the statistic
\(\theta\)
the unknown: a confidence bound
\(q\)
from the CI table: \(1-\alpha\) or \(1-\alpha/2\) for the lower bound, \(\alpha\) or \(\alpha/2\) for the upper bound (Module 5)

Method: use the fundamental equation

  1. Pick the statistic \(Y\) (the sufficient statistic) and write its c.d.f. \(F_Y(y;\theta)\). For one exponential datum: \(1 - e^{-y/\theta}\).
  2. Pick \(q\) from the sheet's table (test: from the tail type; CI: from the bound type).
  3. Rejection region (test): plug in \(\theta = \theta_0\), solve \(F_Y(y;\theta_0) = q\) for \(y\). This happens before you see data.
  4. Confidence bound (CI): plug in \(y = y_{\rm obs}\), solve \(F_Y(y_{\rm obs};\theta) = q\) for \(\theta\).
  5. p-value (no solving): just evaluate \(F_Y(y_{\rm obs};\theta_0)\) or \(1 - F_Y(y_{\rm obs};\theta_0)\).

Worked example · 5W worksheet, then both directions of the equation

(5W worksheet) The exponential p.d.f. is \(f_X(x;\theta) = \frac1\theta e^{-x/\theta}\), \(x \ge 0\), \(\theta > 0\). You sample one datum, \(x_{\rm obs} = 3\). In a hypothesis test that assumes \(\theta = 1\), the p-value is \(1 - F_X(x = 3;\theta = 1)\). Evaluate the p-value (you may leave terms like \(e^a\)). Then, at \(\alpha = 0.05\): (b) find the rejection-region boundary for this upper-tailed test, and (c) find the 95% one-sided lower confidence bound for \(\theta\).

  1. Identify. One datum, so \(Y = X\) and its sampling distribution is the exponential itself. The p-value form \(1 - F\) means upper-tailed: \(H_0: \theta = 1\) vs \(H_A: \theta > 1\).
  2. c.d.f. \(F_X(x;\theta) = 1 - e^{-x/\theta}\), so \(F_X(3;1) = 1 - e^{-3}\).
  3. p-value. \(p = 1 - (1 - e^{-3}) = e^{-3} \approx 0.0498\). Since \(0.0498 < 0.05\), you reject \(H_0\) (just barely) and conclude there is evidence that \(\theta > 1\).
  4. (b) Fix \(\theta\), solve for \(y\). Upper-tailed, so \(q = 1 - \alpha = 0.95\). Solve \(1 - e^{-y_{\rm RR}/1} = 0.95\): \(e^{-y_{\rm RR}} = 0.05\), \(y_{\rm RR} = -\ln 0.05 = \ln 20 \approx 2.996\). Reject if \(y \ge 2.996\). The observed 3 is inside the region: same conclusion as the p-value.
  5. (c) Fix \(y\), solve for \(\theta\). The CI table gives \(q = 1 - \alpha = 0.95\) for a one-sided lower bound. Solve \(1 - e^{-3/\theta} = 0.95\): \(e^{-3/\theta} = 0.05\), \(3/\theta = \ln 20\), \(\hat\theta_L = 3/\ln 20 \approx 1.0014\).
  6. Connect. The bound \([1.0014, \infty)\) just excludes \(\theta_0 = 1\), matching a p-value just under 0.05. The test and the interval are the same equation read two ways, so they always agree like this.

Answer: \(p = e^{-3} \approx 0.0498\), reject \(H_0\). \(y_{\rm RR} = \ln 20 \approx 2.996\). Lower bound \(\hat\theta_L = 3/\ln 20 \approx 1.0014\).

Which way does the curve move?

For the exponential (and for \(x^\theta\)), a bigger \(\theta\) makes big data more likely, so at a fixed \(y\) the c.d.f. drops as \(\theta\) grows (right panel above). This is why the lower bound uses the bigger \(q\). HW5 Q3 and HW6 Q4 use \(F = 1 - 1/x^\theta\), where a bigger \(\theta\) makes data smaller; there the tail and \(q\) assignments flip, and the problem tells you so. Details in Module 4 and Module 5.

Check yourself · HW5 Q3(a), extended

One datum comes from \(f_X(x;\theta) = \theta/x^{\theta+1}\), \(x \ge 1\), \(\theta > 0\). (a) Find the c.d.f. (b) Find the inverse c.d.f. (c) With \(\theta_0 = 2\), find the value \(x\) with 95% of the null probability below it.

Hint

The domain starts at 1, so integrate from 1 to \(x\). Write \(\theta/y^{\theta+1}\) as \(\theta y^{-\theta-1}\).

Full solution
  1. (a) \(F_X(x) = \int_1^x \theta y^{-\theta-1}\,dy = \big[-y^{-\theta}\big]_1^x = -x^{-\theta} + 1 = 1 - 1/x^\theta\). Check: \(F(1) = 0\), \(F(\infty) = 1\).
  2. (b) Set \(1 - x^{-\theta} = q\): \(x^{-\theta} = 1 - q\), so \(x = (1-q)^{-1/\theta}\).
  3. (c) \(q = 0.95\), \(\theta_0 = 2\): \(x = 0.05^{-1/2} = \sqrt{20} \approx 4.47\).
  4. Link to HW5 Q3(b). That test (\(H_A: \theta < 2\)) uses the upper tail, so it would reject for \(x \ge 4.47\). The observed \(x_{\rm obs} = 3\) is below, so fail to reject, which matches the key's \(p = 1/9 = 0.111\).

\(F_X(x) = 1 - 1/x^\theta\); \(F_X^{-1}(q) = (1-q)^{-1/\theta}\); about 4.47.

Check yourself · 5F worksheet

You will run a counting experiment \(n = 10\) times and want to know whether the expected count \(\theta\) is 5 or lower. (a) State \(H_0\) and \(H_A\). (b) Which of the four named distributions would each datum follow? (c) The data are 3, 4, 3, 3, 4, 3, 3, 4, 3, 10. What is the observed value of the sufficient statistic? (d) What is its sampling distribution under \(H_0\), and which c.d.f. value is the p-value?

Full solution
  1. (a) \(H_0: \theta = 5\), \(H_A: \theta < 5\). Lower-tailed.
  2. (b) Poisson, \(X_i \sim \mathrm{Pois}(\theta)\). The data are counts with no fixed upper limit, and \(\theta\) is the expected count. Binomial needs a fixed number of trials; Normal and Exponential are continuous.
  3. (c) Poisson block: \(X_+ = \sum X_i\). Six 3s, three 4s and a 10: \(x_{+,\rm obs} = 18 + 12 + 10 = 40\).
  4. (d) \(X_+ \sim \mathrm{Pois}(n\lambda) = \mathrm{Pois}(10 \times 5) = \mathrm{Pois}(50)\). Lower-tailed, so \(p = F_Y(40;\theta_0) = P(X_+ \le 40)\) under Pois(50). That sum needs software; its value is 0.086, which is above 0.05, so you would fail to reject \(H_0\): an expected count of 5 is plausible.

\(H_0: \theta = 5\) vs \(H_A: \theta < 5\); Poisson; \(x_+ = 40\); \(X_+ \sim \mathrm{Pois}(50)\), \(p = F(40) = 0.086\).

Check yourself · HW6 Q8 (sufficient-statistic step)

Horse-kick deaths were counted in \(n = 200\) corps-years: 109 years had 0 deaths, 65 had 1, 22 had 2, 3 had 3 and 1 had 4. Assume Poisson(\(\lambda\)) counts. What is the observed sufficient statistic, what is its sampling distribution, and what is the natural point estimate of \(\lambda\)?

Full solution
  1. The sufficient statistic is the total count \(X_+ = \sum X_i\), not the number of years.
  2. \(x_+ = 0(109) + 1(65) + 2(22) + 3(3) + 4(1) = 0 + 65 + 44 + 9 + 4 = 122\).
  3. Sampling distribution: \(X_+ \sim \mathrm{Pois}(n\lambda) = \mathrm{Pois}(200\lambda)\).
  4. Point estimate: \(\hat\lambda = x_+/n = 122/200 = 0.61\). (The 95% CI from the key, \([0.507, 0.728]\), comes from solving \(F_Y(122;\lambda) - q = 0\) for \(\lambda\) with software.)

\(x_+ = 122\), \(X_+ \sim \mathrm{Pois}(200\lambda)\), \(\hat\lambda = 0.61\).

Check yourself · Original practice

One exponential datum. You test \(H_0: \theta = 2\) against \(H_A: \theta \ne 2\) at \(\alpha = 0.10\). Find both rejection-region boundaries.

Full solution
  1. Two-tailed, so split \(\alpha\): \(q = \alpha/2 = 0.05\) for the low edge and \(q = 1 - \alpha/2 = 0.95\) for the high edge.
  2. Fix \(\theta = \theta_0 = 2\) and use \(F^{-1}(q;\theta) = -\theta\ln(1-q)\).
  3. Low: \(y_{\rm RR,lo} = -2\ln(0.95) = 2(0.0513) \approx 0.103\).
  4. High: \(y_{\rm RR,hi} = -2\ln(0.05) = 2(2.996) \approx 5.99\).

Reject if \(y \le 0.103\) or \(y \ge 5.99\).

Check yourself · Original practice

One exponential datum, \(y_{\rm obs} = 2\). Find the 90% one-sided upper confidence bound for \(\theta\). (CI table: a one-sided upper bound uses \(q = \alpha\).)

Full solution
  1. 90% means \(\alpha = 0.10\), so \(q = 0.10\).
  2. Fix \(y = 2\) and solve for \(\theta\): \(1 - e^{-2/\theta} = 0.10\).
  3. \(e^{-2/\theta} = 0.90\), so \(-2/\theta = \ln 0.90 = -0.1054\).
  4. \(\hat\theta_U = 2/0.1054 \approx 19.0\).

\(\hat\theta_U \approx 19.0\), so the bound is \((-\infty, 19.0]\) (in practice \((0, 19.0]\) since \(\theta > 0\)). One datum gives a wide bound.

Common mistakes

  • Reading \(F^{-1}\) as \(1/F\). It is the inverse function: solve \(F(x) = q\) for \(x\).
  • Forgetting that the exponential c.d.f. is not on the sheet, or writing it as \(e^{-x/\theta}\) (that is the upper tail).
  • Using the distribution of one datum when the statistic is a sum or mean of \(n\) data.
  • Using \(X_+ \sim \mathrm{Binom}(k, p)\) instead of \(\mathrm{Binom}(nk, p)\), or \(\mathrm{Pois}(\lambda)\) instead of \(\mathrm{Pois}(n\lambda)\).
  • Normal with \(\sigma\) unknown: using \(\overline X \sim \mathcal N\) instead of \(T \sim t(n-1)\).
  • Solving for the wrong unknown: rejection regions fix \(\theta = \theta_0\) and solve for \(y\); confidence bounds fix \(y = y_{\rm obs}\) and solve for \(\theta\).
  • Discrete statistic: expecting an exact solution to \(F(x) = q\). Take the smallest \(x\) with \(F(x) \ge q\).

Quick check

\(F_X(x)\) can decrease as \(x\) increases.

False. A c.d.f. is a running total of probability, so it is non-decreasing, from 0 at \(-\infty\) to 1 at \(+\infty\).

What is the exponential c.d.f., and where is it on the sheet?

\(F(x;\theta) = 1 - e^{-x/\theta}\) for \(x \ge 0\). It is not on the sheet; integrate the p.d.f. from 0 to \(x\).

What is the median of Exp(θ)?

\(F^{-1}(0.5) = -\theta\ln(0.5) = \theta\ln 2 \approx 0.693\theta\). It is below the mean \(\theta\) because the distribution is skewed right.

Binom(4, 0.5) has c.d.f. values 0.0625, 0.3125, 0.6875, 0.9375, 1 at x = 0 to 4. What is \(F^{-1}(0.9)\)?

3. The smallest \(x\) with \(F(x) \ge 0.9\) is 3, since \(F(2) = 0.6875 < 0.9 \le F(3) = 0.9375\).

A sufficient statistic keeps all the information in the sample about θ.

True. Once you know its value, the individual data add nothing more about \(\theta\). That is why tests and intervals are built on it.

For n exponential data, X̄ ~ Gamma(n, θ/n). What is it when n = 1?

Gamma(1, \(\theta\)) is \(\mathrm{Exp}(\theta)\). A single datum's sampling distribution is the exponential itself, which is why by-hand problems use one datum.

To find a rejection-region boundary you fix y and solve for θ.

False. That gives a confidence bound. For a rejection region you fix \(\theta = \theta_0\) and solve \(F_Y(y_{\rm RR};\theta_0) = q\) for \(y_{\rm RR}\).

The rejection region depends on the data you observe.

False. It depends only on \(\theta_0\), \(\alpha\), the tail and the statistic's distribution. HW5: "we define it before data are even collected!"

If you see this on the exam

  • "Find the c.d.f." → integrate the p.d.f. from the left end of the domain to \(x\); check \(F = 0\) at the left end and 1 at the right.
  • Exponential anything → \(F = 1 - e^{-x/\theta}\), upper tail \(e^{-x/\theta}\), inverse \(F^{-1}(q) = -\theta\ln(1-q)\).
  • "Find the inverse c.d.f." → set \(F(x) = q\), solve for \(x\). Discrete: smallest \(x\) with \(F(x) \ge q\).
  • "What is the test statistic / its distribution?" → the family's sufficient-statistic line: \(X_+\) (Binomial, Poisson), \(\overline X\) (Normal with \(\sigma\) known, Exponential), \(T\) (Normal with \(\sigma\) unknown).
  • "Rejection-region boundary" → solve \(F_Y(y_{\rm RR};\theta_0) = q\) for \(y_{\rm RR}\), with \(q\) from the tail type.
  • "Confidence bound" → solve \(F_Y(y_{\rm obs};\theta) = q\) for \(\theta\), with \(q\) from the CI table.
  • "p-value" → no solving: \(F_Y(y_{\rm obs};\theta_0)\), \(1 - F_Y(y_{\rm obs};\theta_0)\), or twice the smaller one.

Module 3 · Lectures 5W, 5F, 6M · about 30 min

Hypothesis testing: the story and the words

Every test on this exam follows the same six steps, and the study guide says you must be able to write the definitions of the p-value, the level (Type I error) and power. This module gives you the story, the words, the exact sentences to memorize, and the only two conclusions you are ever allowed to write.

After this module you can

  • Tell the whole hypothesis-testing story in six steps, the way the instructor does in lecture 5F.
  • Turn a word problem into \(H_0\) and \(H_A\) and say whether the test is lower-tailed, upper-tailed or two-tailed.
  • Write exam-ready definitions of the p-value, the level \(\alpha\) (Type I error) and power, from memory.
  • Read the "Type I error", "Type II error" and "Rejection region" lines of the formula sheet aloud and explain every symbol.
  • Fill in the 2×2 table of decisions versus truth.
  • State a conclusion as "reject \(H_0\)" or "fail to reject \(H_0\)", in the context of the problem.

The story in plain words

In plain English

Someone makes a claim about an unknown number, for example "the average CMU student weighs 140 pounds". You do not try to prove or disprove it directly. You pretend the claim is true and ask one question: if the claim were true, how surprising would my data be?

To measure surprise you boil the whole data set down to one number, the test statistic (for weights, a standardized sample mean). Because you are pretending the claim is true, you know exactly how that number should behave: its null distribution. Then you see where your observed number lands. If it lands far out in a tail, in a place that would happen less than 5% of the time under the claim, you reject the claim. Otherwise you fail to reject it.

The instructor's summary of the whole thing: it is a "framework for decision-making: nothing is proven!" You make a decision with a known error rate. You never prove anything.

The vocabulary

Every word on this list can show up in a conceptual question. Read the "what it means" column carefully: these are the meanings the instructor uses.

Symbol or wordNameWhat it means
\(\theta\)parameterThe unknown number the test is about: a mean \(\mu\), a proportion \(p\), a Poisson rate \(\lambda\), an exponential mean \(\theta\). "The value of which we're trying to infer."
\(\theta_0\)null valueThe one specific value the claim says \(\theta\) equals. In the weights example, \(\theta_0 = \mu_0 = 140\).
\(H_0\)null hypothesis"Our preconceived notion": \(H_0: \theta = \theta_0\). It always contains the equals sign.
\(H_A\)alternative hypothesisWhat you suspect instead. You choose exactly one of \(\theta < \theta_0\), \(\theta > \theta_0\), \(\theta \ne \theta_0\).
\(Y\)test statisticA number computed from the data, used to decide. The instructor's rule: choose "the sufficient statistic for the parameter of interest, with known sampling distribution, if possible" (for example \(\bar X\), \(X_+\), or \(T\)).
\(y_{\rm obs}\)observed valueThe actual number you get when you plug your data into \(Y\).
null distributionsampling distribution under \(H_0\)How \(Y\) would be distributed if \(H_0\) were true. Its c.d.f. is written \(F_Y(y;\theta_0)\): the c.d.f. of \(Y\) with \(\theta\) set to \(\theta_0\).
"extreme"direction of surpriseFar from \(\theta_0\) in the direction \(H_A\) points. Lower-tailed: extreme means smaller. Upper-tailed: larger. Two-tailed: far away on either side.
\(\alpha\)level of the testChosen by you before seeing data, "typically 0.05". The probability of rejecting \(H_0\) when \(H_0\) is true. Also called the Type I error rate.
Type I errorfalse rejectionRejecting \(H_0\) when \(H_0\) is actually true. Its probability is \(\alpha\).
Type II errormissed detectionFailing to reject \(H_0\) when \(H_0\) is actually false. Its probability is \(\beta\).
\(\theta'\)a specific alternativeOne particular value of \(\theta\) other than \(\theta_0\) that you pretend is the truth, so you can compute \(\beta\) and power. In lecture 6M: \(\theta' = 144\) pounds.
\(\beta\)Type II error probabilityThe chance of failing to reject when \(\theta = \theta'\). It changes with \(\theta'\), so always say which \(\theta'\).
powerdetection probabilityThe probability of rejecting \(H_0\) at a given true value of \(\theta\). At \(\theta'\): power \(= 1-\beta\). At \(\theta_0\): power \(= \alpha\).
p-valuesurprise scoreThe probability, assuming \(H_0\) is true, of getting a test statistic at least as extreme as the one you observed. Small p means surprising data.
rejection region (RR)"reject zone"The set of \(y\) values for which you would reject. Its edge is the boundary \(y_{\rm RR}\). It is fixed before any data come in.

Writing \(H_0\) and \(H_A\)

The null is a single value, \(H_0: \theta = \theta_0\). Because it names one exact value, you can compute probabilities under it; that is the whole reason the null is the thing you "pretend is true". The alternative covers a whole range of values. (Some textbooks call a one-value hypothesis "simple" and a range "composite". The course does not use those words, so you only need the idea.)

Three rules:

  • Hypotheses are about the parameter, never about the data. Write \(H_0: \mu = 140\). Never write \(H_0: \bar x = 148\). You already know \(\bar x\); there is nothing to test about it.
  • Choose \(H_0\), \(H_A\) and \(\alpha\) before you collect data. The lecture says that otherwise "any data we observe might bias how we specify our test, and that's bad."
  • Never change \(H_A\) after you see the p-value. The instructor wrote: "To reiterate: we cannot change \(H_A\) once we see the p-value → this is 'p-hacking'."

Which tail? Read the research question

The tail comes from the words of the question, never from where the data happened to land.

The problem says\(H_A\)Test typeReject when \(y\) is
"less than", "lower", "fewer", "decreased", "lighter", "shorter", "whether it is lower"\(\theta < \theta_0\)lower-tailed (lecture: left-tailed)small
"more than", "heavier", "exceeds", "increased", "longer", "improves"\(\theta > \theta_0\)upper-tailed (lecture: right-tailed)large
"differs from", "has changed", "is it 140 or not", "is the claim correct"\(\theta \ne \theta_0\)two-tailed (lecture: two-sided)far from \(\theta_0\) on either side

Two-tailed tests are "more 'conservative': it is harder to reject \(H_0\)" (lecture 5F). For the same data, the two-tailed p-value is twice the one-tailed one in the direction the data went.

The three definitions you must be able to write

Memorize these word for word

p-value. The probability, assuming the null hypothesis is true, of observing a test statistic value as extreme as, or more extreme than, the one actually observed.

Level \(\alpha\) (Type I error rate). The probability of rejecting the null hypothesis when the null hypothesis is actually true: \(\alpha = \mathbb{P}_{\theta_0}(\text{reject } H_0)\). It is chosen before collecting data, typically 0.05.

Power. The probability of rejecting the null hypothesis, given a particular (arbitrary) value of the parameter \(\theta\). At a specific alternative \(\theta'\), power \(= 1-\beta\); at \(\theta = \theta_0\), power \(= \alpha\).

Bonus, Type II error \(\beta\). The probability of failing to reject the null hypothesis when a specific alternative value \(\theta'\) is true.

A memory hook: each definition has the same two parts, "the probability of [an event], given [what is true]".

DefinitionThe eventAssuming this is true
p-valuea statistic at least as extreme as the one observed\(H_0\) (\(\theta = \theta_0\))
\(\alpha\), Type I errorreject \(H_0\)\(H_0\) (\(\theta = \theta_0\))
powerreject \(H_0\)some value of \(\theta\) (usually \(\theta'\))
\(\beta\), Type II errorfail to reject \(H_0\)a specific alternative \(\theta'\)

Notice the difference between the p-value and \(\alpha\). Both are computed under \(H_0\). The p-value depends on your data (it moves when \(y_{\rm obs}\) moves). \(\alpha\) is a fixed setting you chose before the data existed.

Decoding the sheet: the three testing lines

Hypothesis Testing · Type I error

\[ \text{Type I error: } \alpha = \mathbb{P}_{\theta_0}(\text{reject null}) \]

"Alpha equals the probability, computed with the parameter set to its null value theta-naught, that the test rejects the null."

\(\alpha\)
the level of the test, also the Type I error rate. You pick it (0.05 unless told otherwise).
\(\mathbb{P}_{\theta_0}(\cdot)\)
a probability computed from the distribution of the test statistic with \(\theta = \theta_0\) plugged in. The subscript tells you which world you are in: the world where \(H_0\) is true.
reject null
the event that the observed statistic lands in the rejection region (the same event as p < \(\alpha\)).

You build the rejection region so that its probability under the null is exactly \(\alpha\) (Module 4 shows how). That is why the Type I error rate equals the level you chose.

Hypothesis Testing · Type II error

\[ \text{Type II error: } \beta = \mathbb{P}_{\theta'}(\text{fail to reject null};\, \alpha) \]

"Beta equals the probability, computed with the parameter set to a specific alternative theta-prime, that a level-alpha test fails to reject the null."

\(\beta\)
the probability of a Type II error (a missed detection).
\(\theta'\)
"theta prime": one specific value of \(\theta\) other than \(\theta_0\) that you assume is the truth, for example \(\mu = 144\). Change \(\theta'\) and \(\beta\) changes.
fail to reject null
the event that the statistic lands outside the rejection region.
\(;\,\alpha\)
the semicolon reads "for a test of level \(\alpha\)". \(\alpha\) sets where the boundary sits, so it affects \(\beta\): a smaller \(\alpha\) gives a smaller rejection region and a bigger \(\beta\).
\[ \text{power}(\theta') = 1 - \beta = \mathbb{P}_{\theta'}(\text{reject null}), \qquad \text{power}(\theta_0) = \alpha \]

Not printed as its own line on the sheet, but it follows from the two lines above. Power is "the probability of rejecting \(H_0\) given an arbitrary value of \(\theta\)". Module 4 computes it.

Hypothesis Testing · Rejection region

\[ \begin{aligned} &\text{lower-tailed: } \{\,y \le y_{\rm RR}\,\}\\ &\text{upper-tailed: } \{\,y \ge y_{\rm RR}\,\}\\ &\text{two-tailed: } \{\,y \le y_{\rm RR,lo}\,\} \cup \{\,y \ge y_{\rm RR,hi}\,\} \end{aligned} \]

The sheet line in words: "Rejection region: set of values of test statistic for which we would reject the null; defined assuming the null hypothesis is true."

set of values of test statistic
a range of possible \(y\) values, written down before you have data.
for which we would reject
if \(y_{\rm obs}\) falls inside, you reject \(H_0\); if outside, you fail to reject.
defined assuming \(H_0\) is true
the boundary \(y_{\rm RR}\) is computed from the null distribution \(F_Y(y;\theta_0)\), chosen so the region has probability \(\alpha\) under \(H_0\). It never uses \(\theta'\) and never uses the data.
\(y_{\rm RR}\)
the rejection-region boundary. \(y_{\rm RR,lo}\) and \(y_{\rm RR,hi}\) are the two boundaries of a two-tailed test.

The sheet prints only the words. The three set descriptions above are how those words look for each type of test. How to compute \(y_{\rm RR}\) is the next line of the sheet, decoded in Module 4.

Pictures

The 2×2 decision table

The truth is one column; you never know which. Your decision is one row. Each column adds to 1.

Your decisionTruth: \(H_0\) true (\(\theta = \theta_0\))Truth: \(H_0\) false (\(\theta = \theta'\))
Reject \(H_0\)Type I error. Probability \(\alpha\).Correct. Probability = power \(= 1-\beta\).
Fail to reject \(H_0\)Correct. Probability \(1-\alpha\).Type II error. Probability \(\beta\).

In quality control (Module 9) the same two errors get new names: a false alarm is a Type I error (the chart says "out of control" when the process is fine) and a failure to detect is a Type II error.

Conclusions: only two are allowed

The decision rule from lecture 5F: if \(p < \alpha\), reject \(H_0\). Otherwise, fail to reject \(H_0\). Then say what that means in the words of the problem. Copy these templates:

Conclusion templates

  1. Reject: "Since \(p = \_\_ < \alpha = \_\_\), we reject \(H_0\) and conclude there is evidence that [\(H_A\) in context, e.g. the average student weight is greater than 140 lb]."
  2. Fail to reject: "Since \(p = \_\_ \ge \alpha = \_\_\), we fail to reject \(H_0\) and conclude that [\(\theta_0\) in context, e.g. an average weight of 140 lb] is plausible."

Why never "accept \(H_0\)" and never "prove"

  • Failing to reject is weak. It means the data are consistent with \(\theta_0\). They are usually just as consistent with nearby values (141, 139, ...). A test with low power fails to reject false nulls all the time. So the honest statement is "\(\theta_0\) is plausible", never "\(\theta_0\) is true".
  • Rejecting is not proof either. Even when \(H_0\) is true, you reject with probability \(\alpha\). So you say "there is evidence that", never "we proved".
  • The p-value is not the probability that \(H_0\) is true. It is computed assuming \(H_0\) is true, so it cannot also be the chance that \(H_0\) is true.

The instructor's framework, as a recipe

Method: the six steps of every test (lecture 5F)

  1. Null hypothesis. \(H_0: \theta = \theta_0\). Name the parameter and its claimed value.
  2. Alternative hypothesis. Choose one: \(\theta < \theta_0\) (lower-tailed), \(\theta > \theta_0\) (upper-tailed), \(\theta \ne \theta_0\) (two-tailed). Decide from the research question, before seeing data.
  3. Level. Set \(\alpha\) (typically 0.05), also before seeing data.
  4. Test statistic. Pick \(Y\) (the sufficient statistic with a known sampling distribution), write its null distribution, and compute \(y_{\rm obs}\).
  5. p-value. Use the p-value table for your tail (Module 4). If \(p < \alpha\), reject \(H_0\); otherwise fail to reject. State it in context.
  6. Power. Ask how often this test would catch a specific alternative \(\theta'\) (Module 4).

Worked example · Lecture 5F, CMU student weights

"We record the weights of \(n = 25\) CMU students and assume that these data are normally distributed with mean \(\mu\) and variance \(\sigma^2\). Our null hypothesis is that the average student weight is 140 pounds, and our alternative hypothesis is that students are heavier than 140 pounds. We observe that the sample mean is \(\bar x_{\rm obs} = 148\) and that the sample variance is \(s^2_{\rm obs} = 400\). Assuming that the level of our test is \(\alpha = 0.05\), what do we conclude?" Follow-up from the same lecture: what if we only want to know whether the average is 140 or not?

  1. Identify. The parameter is the population mean weight \(\mu\). "Heavier than 140" points up. So \(H_0: \mu = 140\) and \(H_A: \mu > 140\), an upper-tailed test, with \(\alpha = 0.05\).
  2. Choose the statistic. \(\sigma\) is unknown (we only have the sample variance), so use the sheet's Normal line "Sufficient statistic (\(\sigma\) unknown): \(T = (\bar X - \mu)/(S/\sqrt n) \sim t(n-1)\)". Plug in \(\mu = \mu_0 = 140\) because we are assuming \(H_0\). Under \(H_0\), \(T \sim t(24)\).
  3. Compute \(t_{\rm obs}\). \(s = \sqrt{400} = 20\) and \(\sqrt{25} = 5\), so the standard error is \(20/5 = 4\). Then \(t_{\rm obs} = (148 - 140)/4 = 8/4 = 2\).
  4. p-value. Upper-tailed row: \(p = 1 - F_{t(24)}(2)\). The c.d.f. value is \(F_{t(24)}(2) = 0.972\) (given in lecture), so \(p = 0.028\).
  5. Conclude. \(0.028 < 0.05\): "We reject the null hypothesis and conclude there is evidence that average student weight is >140."
  6. Follow-up, two-tailed. Now \(H_A: \mu \ne 140\). Two-tailed row: \(p = 2\times\min[0.972,\ 0.028] = 2(0.028) = 0.056\). Since \(0.056 \ge 0.05\): "We'd fail to reject \(H_0\) and conclude that 140 is plausible." Same data, opposite decision: two-tailed tests are more conservative.

Answer: upper-tailed, \(t_{\rm obs} = 2\), \(p = 0.028\), reject \(H_0\). Two-tailed version: \(p = 0.056\), fail to reject \(H_0\).

Heads up

The instructor wrote 0.056 by doubling the rounded 0.028. Doubling the unrounded value gives 0.057. The conclusion is the same. Also, the sheet's "Sample mean" line prints \(\bar X = \sum_{i=1}^n X_i\) with the \(1/n\) missing: the correct line is \(\bar X = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n X_i\). And notice what you may NOT do: switch to the one-tailed test after seeing that the two-tailed p is 0.056. That is p-hacking.

Worked example · 5F worksheet

"You intend to complete a particular counting experiment \(n = 10\) times, and you are interested in whether the expected count is 5, or whether it is lower. Denote the expected count as \(\theta\)." (a) What are the null and alternative hypotheses? (b) Which of the four named distributions would you assume each datum comes from? (c) The observed data are {3, 4, 3, 3, 4, 3, 3, 4, 3, 10}. What is the observed value of the relevant sufficient statistic? (d) Extension, not on the worksheet: given that \(\mathbb{P}(X_+ \le 40)\) under Pois(50) is 0.086, what do you conclude at \(\alpha = 0.05\)?

  1. (a) Hypotheses. The claim is "expected count is 5", so \(H_0: \theta = 5\). The suspicion is "lower", so \(H_A: \theta < 5\). Lower-tailed.
  2. (b) Distribution. Poisson, \(X_i \sim \text{Pois}(\theta)\). The data are counts (0, 1, 2, ...) with no fixed maximum, and the Poisson mean is \(\lambda = \theta\). Binomial needs a fixed number of trials; normal and exponential are continuous.
  3. (c) Statistic. The sheet's Poisson block: "Sufficient statistic: \(X_+ = \sum_{i=1}^n X_i \sim \text{Pois}(n\lambda)\)". Add the data: \(3+4+3+3+4+3+3+4+3+10 = 40\). So \(x_{+,\rm obs} = 40\).
  4. (d) Null distribution. Under \(H_0\), \(X_+ \sim \text{Pois}(10 \times 5) = \text{Pois}(50)\). Lower-tailed row: \(p = F_{X_+}(40;\,50) = 0.086\) (computer value, given).
  5. Conclude. \(0.086 \ge 0.05\), so we fail to reject \(H_0\) and conclude that an expected count of 5 is plausible.

Answer: \(H_0: \theta = 5\) vs \(H_A: \theta < 5\); Poisson; \(x_{+,\rm obs} = 40\); with \(p = 0.086\), fail to reject \(H_0\).

Check yourself · Original practice

Write \(H_0\), \(H_A\) and the test type for each. (a) A maker claims its bulbs last 1000 hours on average; a consumer group suspects they burn out sooner. (b) A machine should fill bottles with 500 mL on average; engineers want to know if it has drifted in either direction. (c) A new coating is supposed to raise the mean exponential lifetime \(\theta\) of a part above 3 years. (d) A gambler suspects a coin lands heads more than half the time. (e) A student writes "\(H_0: \bar x = 148\)". What is wrong?

Full solution
  1. (a) \(H_0: \mu = 1000\), \(H_A: \mu < 1000\). "Sooner" means lower: lower-tailed.
  2. (b) \(H_0: \mu = 500\), \(H_A: \mu \ne 500\). "Either direction": two-tailed.
  3. (c) \(H_0: \theta = 3\), \(H_A: \theta > 3\). "Raise above": upper-tailed.
  4. (d) \(H_0: p = 0.5\), \(H_A: p > 0.5\). Upper-tailed.
  5. (e) Hypotheses are statements about the unknown parameter (\(\mu\)), never about the sample mean, which you already know exactly.

Lower, two, upper, upper; hypotheses are always about the parameter.

Check yourself · Lecture 5F follow-ups (σ known, and growing n)

Same weights data: \(n = 25\), \(\bar x_{\rm obs} = 148\), \(H_0: \mu = 140\) vs \(H_A: \mu > 140\), \(\alpha = 0.05\). (a) "What would change if we knew that \(\sigma^2 = 400\)?" Use \(1 - F(148;\ \text{mean } 140,\ \text{sd } 4) = 0.023\). (b) Why is this p-value smaller than the 0.028 from the \(t\) test? (c) "What happens to the p-value if \(\bar x_{\rm obs}\) and \(s^2_{\rm obs}\) stay constant but the sample size \(n\) increases?" Compute \(t_{\rm obs}\) for \(n = 100\).

Hint

With \(\sigma\) known, the sheet's line is "Sufficient statistic (\(\sigma\) known): \(\bar X \sim N(\mu, \sigma^2/n)\)".

Full solution
  1. (a) The statistic becomes \(\bar X\) itself, with null distribution \(N(140,\ 400/25) = N(140,\ 16)\), standard deviation 4. "The framework stays the same, but the sampling distribution is now normal, not t-distributed." Upper-tailed: \(p = 1 - F(148; 140, 4) = 0.023 < 0.05\). Reject \(H_0\); there is evidence the true average weight is greater than 140.
  2. (b) The \(t\) distribution has heavier tails than the normal, because estimating \(\sigma\) with \(S\) adds uncertainty. Knowing \(\sigma\) removes that uncertainty, so the same distance (2 standard errors) is a bit more surprising: 0.023 versus 0.028.
  3. (c) \(t_{\rm obs} = \sqrt n\,(\bar x - \mu_0)/s = \sqrt{100}\,(8)/20 = 4\), double the old value of 2. As \(n\) grows, \(t_{\rm obs}\) grows like \(\sqrt n\), so it moves farther into the tail and the p-value goes to 0.

(a) \(p = 0.023\), reject. (b) Normal tails are thinner than \(t\) tails. (c) \(t_{\rm obs} = 4\); the p-value shrinks toward 0 as \(n\) grows.

Check yourself · HW6 Q1 and Q2 (conclusions from given p-values)

(a) HW6 Q1: 32 cards were drawn and a chi-square test checked whether the four suits appear in equal proportion. The p-value is 0.080 at \(\alpha = 0.05\). (b) HW6 Q2: a chi-square test checked whether infant attachment pattern (secure or anxious) is associated with hours per week in child care. The p-value is 0.026 at \(\alpha = 0.05\). Write each conclusion in context. Then say what is wrong with: "we accept that the deck is fair" and "we proved child care causes anxious attachment".

Full solution
  1. (a) \(0.080 \ge 0.05\): we fail to reject \(H_0\) and conclude that it is plausible the deck is fairly constructed (each suit with probability 1/4).
  2. (b) \(0.026 < 0.05\): we reject \(H_0\) and conclude there is evidence of an association between hours in child care and attachment pattern.
  3. "Accept" is never used: failing to reject only says fairness is plausible. "Proved" is never used: rejecting happens with probability \(\alpha\) even when \(H_0\) is true, and the test speaks only about association, the claim in \(H_A\).

(a) Fail to reject; a fair deck is plausible. (b) Reject; evidence of an association.

Check yourself · HW7 Q3 (false alarms are Type I errors)

A process is monitored with a control chart and is declared out of control if a point falls outside the \(3\sigma\) limits. When the process is in control, each point falls outside with probability \(p = 0.0027\) (a normal c.d.f. value, given). (a) Which type of error is a false alarm, and what plays the role of \(\alpha\)? (b) Treating the 50 subgroups as independent trials, what is the probability of at least one false alarm in the next 50 samples? (c) In 100 samples?

Full solution
  1. (a) A false alarm says "out of control" when the process is in control: rejecting a true null, a Type I error. Each plotted point is a little test with \(\alpha = 0.0027\).
  2. (b) Let \(Y\) = number of false alarms in \(k = 50\) trials, \(Y \sim \text{Binom}(50,\ 0.0027)\). Then \(\mathbb{P}(Y \ge 1) = 1 - \mathbb{P}(Y = 0) = 1 - (1-0.0027)^{50} = 1 - 0.9973^{50} = 1 - 0.874 = 0.126\).
  3. (c) \(1 - 0.9973^{100} = 1 - 0.763 = 0.237\). More tests means more chances for a Type I error somewhere.

(a) Type I, \(\alpha = 0.0027\) per point. (b) 0.126. (c) 0.237.

Heads up

HW7 has no official key. These values were computed here. Here \(k\) is the number of Binomial trials, one of the several meanings of \(k\) on the sheet (it is also the total count in the chi-square tests and the p-chart subgroup size, and the CUSUM slack in \(k\sigma\)).

Check yourself · Original practice (conceptual)

A classmate says three things after a test gives \(p = 0.03\) at \(\alpha = 0.05\). Fix each one. (a) "There is a 3% chance that \(H_0\) is true." (b) "There is a 5% chance we just made a Type I error." (c) "If we had used \(\alpha = 0.01\), we would have made a Type II error for sure."

Full solution
  1. (a) The p-value is computed assuming \(H_0\) is true: it is the probability of a statistic at least this extreme, given \(H_0\). It is not a probability about \(H_0\) itself.
  2. (b) \(\alpha = 0.05\) is the long-run rate of rejecting when \(H_0\) is true, a property of the procedure. For this one decision, either \(H_0\) is true (and you made a Type I error) or it is false (and you did not). You do not know which.
  3. (c) With \(\alpha = 0.01\), \(p = 0.03 \ge 0.01\), so you would fail to reject. That is a Type II error only if \(H_0\) is actually false, which you cannot know. What is true: lowering \(\alpha\) raises \(\beta\), the chance of Type II errors in general.

p is computed given \(H_0\); \(\alpha\) is a long-run rate; failing to reject is an error only if \(H_0\) is false.

Common mistakes

  • Writing "accept \(H_0\)" or "the data prove". Only "reject \(H_0\)" or "fail to reject \(H_0\)", then context.
  • Writing hypotheses about \(\bar x\) instead of \(\mu\).
  • Choosing the tail from where the data landed. The tail comes from the research question, set before data.
  • Defining the p-value as "the probability that \(H_0\) is true", or leaving out "assuming \(H_0\) is true" and "or more extreme".
  • Mixing up \(\alpha\) and the p-value. \(\alpha\) is fixed in advance; p comes from the data.
  • Treating \(\beta\) as one fixed number. It depends on which \(\theta'\) you assume.
  • Thinking power is "the probability \(H_A\) is true". Power is the probability the test rejects, at a given \(\theta\).

Quick check

"Fail to reject \(H_0\)" means \(H_0\) is true.

False. It means \(\theta_0\) is plausible given the data. Many other values are plausible too, and the test may simply lack power.

You may pick \(H_A\) after looking at the data, as long as you report it honestly.

False. \(H_0\), \(H_A\) and \(\alpha\) are fixed before the data. Changing \(H_A\) after seeing the p-value is p-hacking.

The p-value is computed assuming \(H_0\) is true.

True. It is the probability of a statistic at least as extreme as observed, under the null distribution \(F_Y(y;\theta_0)\).

\(p = 0.03\), \(\alpha = 0.05\). Decision?

Reject \(H_0\). \(0.03 < 0.05\). Then say "there is evidence that [\(H_A\) in context]".

For the same data, which test is more conservative, one-tailed or two-tailed?

Two-tailed. Its p-value is twice the one-tailed p-value (in the direction of the data), so it is harder to reject.

What is the power of a test when \(\theta = \theta_0\)?

\(\alpha\). Power is the probability of rejecting; when the null is true, that probability is the Type I error rate, \(\alpha\).

\(\beta\) is a single number for a given test.

False. \(\beta = \mathbb{P}_{\theta'}(\text{fail to reject})\) changes with the alternative \(\theta'\) you assume.

Lowering \(\alpha\) from 0.05 to 0.01 (same \(n\)) makes Type II errors more likely.

True. The rejection region shrinks, so the test rejects less often under every \(\theta\), including the alternatives: \(\beta\) goes up.

A control chart false alarm is which type of error?

Type I. The chart rejects "in control" when the process really is in control. A failure to detect is Type II.

"Students are heavier than 140 lb." Which alternative?

\(H_A: \mu > 140\), upper-tailed, with \(H_0: \mu = 140\).

If you see this on the exam

  • "Define the p-value" → "The probability, assuming \(H_0\) is true, of observing a test statistic as extreme as or more extreme than the one observed."
  • "Define the level / Type I error" → "The probability of rejecting \(H_0\) when \(H_0\) is true; \(\alpha = \mathbb{P}_{\theta_0}(\text{reject})\), chosen before data."
  • "Define power" → "The probability of rejecting \(H_0\) given a particular value of \(\theta\); equals \(1-\beta\) at \(\theta'\) and \(\alpha\) at \(\theta_0\)."
  • "State the hypotheses" → \(H_0: \theta = \theta_0\); \(H_A\) from the keywords: less (lower), more (upper), different (two-tailed). Parameter, never \(\bar x\).
  • "What do you conclude?" → compare p with \(\alpha\); "reject \(H_0\) and conclude there is evidence that ..." or "fail to reject \(H_0\); \(\theta_0\) is plausible". Never "accept", never "prove".
  • "Type I or Type II?" → Type I: reject a true null (false alarm). Type II: miss a false null (failure to detect).
  • "What is \(\theta'\)?" → one specific alternative value assumed true when computing \(\beta\) or power.

Module 4 · Lectures 5W, 5F, 6M · about 65 min

Computing p-values, rejection regions and power

The study guide's "Important" line: "be able to compute p-values, rejection-region boundaries, and test power analytically given simple, algebraically manipulable c.d.f.s!" This module decodes both tables on the sheet, row by row, and drills all three calculations by hand with the exponential and the homework c.d.f.s.

After this module you can

  • Say which area under which curve each row of the p-value table and the power table is.
  • Find a rejection-region boundary by solving \(F_Y(y_{\rm RR};\theta_0) - q = 0\), for lower-, upper- and two-tailed tests.
  • Compute a p-value, a boundary and the power at \(\theta'\) by hand for the exponential and the other algebraic c.d.f.s from the homework.
  • Compute normal power from given c.d.f. values (the lecture 6M weights example: 146.58, 0.259, 0.004).
  • Say how power changes with \(n\), with \(\theta'\), with \(\alpha\) and with \(\sigma\), and why power equals \(\alpha\) at \(\theta_0\).
  • Explain the sample-size calculation and why you always round up.

The idea: one picture, three numbers

In plain English

Draw the null distribution of your statistic \(Y\) (the curve you get by assuming \(\theta = \theta_0\)). Everything in this module is an area under a curve, and every area is a c.d.f. value. In the instructor's words: "computing p-values means working with c.d.f.s".

  • p-value: stand at your observed value \(y_{\rm obs}\) and measure the area of the null curve beyond it, in the direction \(H_A\) points.
  • Rejection-region boundary \(y_{\rm RR}\): work backwards. Find the point on the axis that leaves exactly area \(\alpha\) of the null curve beyond it. That is an inverse c.d.f.
  • Power: keep the same \(y_{\rm RR}\), swap in the curve for the true value \(\theta'\), and measure that curve's area beyond \(y_{\rm RR}\).

Two-step summary for power, written by the instructor in 6M: "① Given \(\alpha\) and \(\theta_0\), we can compute a rejection region boundary (middle column). ② Given \(y_{\rm RR}\) and \(\theta\), we can compute power (right column)."

Symbols used in this module

SymbolNameWhat it means
\(Y\)test statisticThe number you compute from the data. With a single observation, \(Y = X\). With normal data and \(\sigma\) known, \(Y = \bar X\).
\(F_Y(y;\theta)\)c.d.f. of \(Y\)\(\mathbb{P}(Y \le y)\) when the parameter equals \(\theta\): the area of \(Y\)'s density to the left of \(y\). The value after the semicolon says which \(\theta\) to plug in.
\(F_Y^{-1}(q;\theta)\)inverse c.d.f. (quantile function)The \(y\) value with area \(q\) to its left. "One where \(\mathbb{P}(X \le x)\) is the input, and the corresponding \(x\) is the output."
\(\theta_0\)null valueThe value in \(H_0: \theta = \theta_0\). Used for p-values and boundaries.
\(\theta\) or \(\theta'\)true / alternative valueThe value you assume is the truth when you compute power. The power table writes \(\theta\); the Type II line writes \(\theta'\). Same thing.
\(y_{\rm obs}\)observed statisticYour data's value of \(Y\).
\(y_{\rm RR}\)rejection-region boundaryThe edge of the reject zone. Two-tailed tests have two: \(y_{\rm RR,lo}\) and \(y_{\rm RR,hi}\).
\(q\)quantile levelA probability between 0 and 1 that you feed into the inverse c.d.f.: \(\alpha\), \(1-\alpha\), \(\alpha/2\) or \(1-\alpha/2\) depending on the tail.
\(\alpha\), \(\beta\)error probabilities\(\alpha\) = level = Type I error rate. \(\beta\) = Type II error probability at \(\theta'\). Power \(= 1-\beta\).

Your by-hand toolkit: c.d.f.s you can do with a pencil

Of the four named distributions, the typed 5W notes say only the exponential "has a c.d.f. that we can easily work with using pencil and paper".
