The normal and \(t\) need software, so on the exam their c.d.f. values would be given. The homework adds a few more algebraic c.d.f.s. Notice that the exponential's sufficient statistic for \(n\) data, \(\bar X \sim \text{Gamma}(n, \theta/n)\), is not hand-friendly. That is why hand problems use one observation: then \(Y = X\) and the c.d.f. of the statistic is the c.d.f. of the data.

\[ \begin{aligned} F(y;\theta) &= \int_0^y \tfrac{1}{\theta} e^{-t/\theta}\,dt = \Big[-e^{-t/\theta}\Big]_0^y = 1 - e^{-y/\theta}, \quad y \ge 0\\ 1 - F(y;\theta) &= e^{-y/\theta}\\ q = 1 - e^{-y/\theta} &\;\Rightarrow\; e^{-y/\theta} = 1-q \;\Rightarrow\; F^{-1}(q;\theta) = -\theta\ln(1-q) \end{aligned} \]

The exponential c.d.f., its upper tail and its inverse. The sheet prints only the p.d.f. \(f_X(x) = \frac1\theta e^{-x/\theta}\), so memorize \(F = 1 - e^{-y/\theta}\) or be ready to integrate. Since \(\ln(1-q) < 0\), the minus sign makes the inverse positive.

Distribution (one observation)\(F(y;\theta)\)Upper tail \(1-F\)\(F^{-1}(q;\theta)\)Where it appears
Exponential, \(y \ge 0\)\(1 - e^{-y/\theta}\)\(e^{-y/\theta}\)\(-\theta\ln(1-q)\)5W notes and worksheet
\(f = \theta y^{\theta-1}\), \(0 \le y \le 1\)\(y^{\theta}\)\(1 - y^{\theta}\)\(q^{1/\theta}\)HW5 Q1, Q2; HW6 Q3
\(f = \theta / y^{\theta+1}\), \(y \ge 1\)\(1 - y^{-\theta}\)\(y^{-\theta}\)\((1-q)^{-1/\theta}\)HW5 Q3; HW6 Q4
Uniform\((0,\theta)\)\(y/\theta\)\(1 - y/\theta\)\(q\theta\)practice problems below

Decoding the p-value table

Hypothesis Testing · p-value table

\[ \begin{array}{ll} \text{lower-tailed} & F_Y(y_{\rm obs};\theta_0)\\ \text{upper-tailed} & 1 - F_Y(y_{\rm obs};\theta_0)\\ \text{two-tailed} & 2\times\min[F_Y(y_{\rm obs};\theta_0),\ 1 - F_Y(y_{\rm obs};\theta_0)] \end{array} \]

"Lower-tailed: the p-value is the null c.d.f. at what I observed. Upper-tailed: one minus that. Two-tailed: twice the smaller of the two."

\(F_Y(y_{\rm obs};\theta_0)\)
\(\mathbb{P}(Y \le y_{\rm obs})\) assuming \(H_0\): the area of the null curve to the left of what you observed. For a lower-tailed test, "more extreme" means smaller, so this left area is the p-value.
\(1 - F_Y(y_{\rm obs};\theta_0)\)
\(\mathbb{P}(Y \ge y_{\rm obs})\) assuming \(H_0\): the area to the right. For an upper-tailed test, "more extreme" means larger.
\(2\times\min[\cdot,\cdot]\)
take whichever tail is smaller (the side your data fell on) and double it. Under \(H_A: \theta \ne \theta_0\), an equally surprising result on the other side would also count as extreme.
\(\theta_0\)
always the null value. A p-value is computed in the world where \(H_0\) is true.

Heads up

All the by-hand statistics in this module are continuous, so \(\mathbb{P}(Y = y_{\rm obs}) = 0\) and it does not matter whether you write \(\le\) or \(<\). For a discrete statistic such as a Poisson \(X_+\), the upper tail "at or above \(y_{\rm obs}\)" is \(1 - F(y_{\rm obs}-1)\); those p-values come from a computer in this course.

Decoding the boundary line: the fundamental equation, solved for \(y\)

Hypothesis Testing · Solve F_Y(y_RR; θ0) − q = 0 for y_RR

\[ \text{Solve } F_Y(y_{\rm RR};\theta_0) - q = 0 \text{ for } y_{\rm RR} \]

"Find the value of the statistic at which the null c.d.f. equals q. That value is the edge of the rejection region."

\(y_{\rm RR}\)
the unknown you solve for: the rejection-region boundary.
\(\theta_0\)
held fixed at the null value. The region is "defined assuming the null hypothesis is true".
\(q\)
how much null area you want to the left of the boundary. Lower-tailed: \(q = \alpha\). Upper-tailed: \(q = 1-\alpha\) (area \(\alpha\) to the right means area \(1-\alpha\) to the left). Two-tailed: solve twice, with \(q = \alpha/2\) and \(q = 1-\alpha/2\).
the solution
\(y_{\rm RR} = F_Y^{-1}(q;\theta_0)\). That is exactly the "RR Boundary" column of the power table.

This is the course's "fundamental equation" \(F_Y(y;\theta) - q = 0\). Hold \(\theta\) fixed at \(\theta_0\) and solve for \(y\): you get a rejection-region boundary. Hold \(y\) fixed at \(y_{\rm obs}\) and solve for \(\theta\): you get a confidence-interval bound (Module 5). The boundary never depends on the data. HW5 reminds you: "the rejection-region boundary does not depend on what we actually observe; we define it before data are even collected!"

Decoding the power table

Hypothesis Testing · Power via the table

\[ \begin{array}{lll} \text{Test type} & \text{RR boundary} & \text{Test power}\\ \hline \text{lower} & y_{\rm RR} = F_Y^{-1}(\alpha;\theta_0) & F_Y(y_{\rm RR};\theta)\\ \text{upper} & y_{\rm RR} = F_Y^{-1}(1-\alpha;\theta_0) & 1 - F_Y(y_{\rm RR};\theta)\\ \text{two} & y_{\rm RR,lo} = F_Y^{-1}(\alpha/2;\theta_0) & F_Y(y_{\rm RR,lo};\theta)\ +\\ & y_{\rm RR,hi} = F_Y^{-1}(1-\alpha/2;\theta_0) & 1 - F_Y(y_{\rm RR,hi};\theta) \end{array} \]

"Middle column: get the boundary from the null with the inverse c.d.f. Right column: plug that boundary into the c.d.f. at the true theta and take the area on the rejecting side."

middle column
uses \(\theta_0\) and \(\alpha\) only. Same \(q\) values as the boundary line above.
right column
uses the boundary and the true \(\theta\) (the alternative \(\theta'\)). Never \(\theta_0\), unless you want to check that you get \(\alpha\).
lower row
reject when \(Y \le y_{\rm RR}\), so power \(= \mathbb{P}_\theta(Y \le y_{\rm RR}) = F_Y(y_{\rm RR};\theta)\).
upper row
reject when \(Y \ge y_{\rm RR}\), so power \(= \mathbb{P}_\theta(Y \ge y_{\rm RR}) = 1 - F_Y(y_{\rm RR};\theta)\).
two-tailed row
reject in either tail, so add the two areas: left of \(y_{\rm RR,lo}\) plus right of \(y_{\rm RR,hi}\), both under the true \(\theta\).
\(\beta\)
\(1 - \text{power}\): the area under the true curve between the boundaries (the fail-to-reject zone).

Heads up

Lecture 6M calls the rows left-tailed, right-tailed and two-sided. The sheet says lower-tailed, upper-tailed and two-tailed. Same rows. The power column writes \(\theta\), and the Type II line writes \(\theta'\): both mean the value you assume is true.

The three recipes

Method A: a p-value by hand

  1. Write \(H_0: \theta = \theta_0\) and \(H_A\); pick the row (lower, upper, two).
  2. Identify the statistic \(Y\) and its c.d.f. \(F_Y(y;\theta)\). With one observation, \(Y = X\) and \(F_Y\) is the c.d.f. of \(X\). Integrate the p.d.f. if you are only given the p.d.f.
  3. Plug in \(y_{\rm obs}\) and \(\theta = \theta_0\): compute \(F_Y(y_{\rm obs};\theta_0)\).
  4. Apply the row: \(F\), \(1-F\), or \(2\times\min(F,\ 1-F)\).
  5. If \(p < \alpha\), reject \(H_0\); otherwise fail to reject. Say it in context.

Method B: a rejection-region boundary by hand

  1. Pick \(q\): \(\alpha\) (lower), \(1-\alpha\) (upper), or both \(\alpha/2\) and \(1-\alpha/2\) (two-tailed).
  2. Set \(F_Y(y_{\rm RR};\theta_0) = q\), with the null value plugged in.
  3. Solve for \(y_{\rm RR}\): isolate the exponential or the power, then take a log or a root. (Exponential: \(y_{\rm RR} = -\theta_0\ln(1-q)\).)
  4. Write the region: \(y \le y_{\rm RR}\) (lower), \(y \ge y_{\rm RR}\) (upper), or \(y \le y_{\rm RR,lo}\) or \(y \ge y_{\rm RR,hi}\) (two-tailed).
  5. Sanity check: an upper-tailed boundary sits above the middle of the null curve, a lower-tailed one below it. And \(y_{\rm obs}\) inside the region must match \(p < \alpha\).

Method C: power at a specific alternative \(\theta'\)

  1. Get \(y_{\rm RR}\) from the null with Method B. This step never uses \(\theta'\).
  2. Switch worlds: plug \(y_{\rm RR}\) into the c.d.f. with \(\theta = \theta'\).
  3. Apply the row: \(F_Y(y_{\rm RR};\theta')\) (lower), \(1 - F_Y(y_{\rm RR};\theta')\) (upper), or \(F_Y(y_{\rm RR,lo};\theta') + 1 - F_Y(y_{\rm RR,hi};\theta')\) (two-tailed).
  4. \(\beta = 1 - \text{power}\). Interpret: "if \(\theta\) really were \(\theta'\), the test would reject in about [power × 100]% of repeated experiments."
  5. Sanity check: plugging \(\theta' = \theta_0\) must give back \(\alpha\).

Exponential shortcuts (use them to check your work)

\[ \begin{aligned} \text{upper: } y_{\rm RR} &= -\theta_0\ln\alpha, & \text{power}(\theta') &= e^{-y_{\rm RR}/\theta'} = e^{(\theta_0/\theta')\ln\alpha} = \alpha^{\theta_0/\theta'}\\ \text{lower: } y_{\rm RR} &= -\theta_0\ln(1-\alpha), & \text{power}(\theta') &= 1 - e^{-y_{\rm RR}/\theta'} = 1 - (1-\alpha)^{\theta_0/\theta'} \end{aligned} \]

Derived from the table with one exponential observation (not on the sheet). Put \(\theta' = \theta_0\) and both give \(\alpha\), as they must. On the exam, show the full steps; use these only to check.

Worked example · 5W worksheet (plus boundary and two-tailed extensions)

An exponential distribution has p.d.f. \(f_X(x;\theta) = \frac1\theta e^{-x/\theta}\), \(x \ge 0\). You sample a single datum, \(x_{\rm obs} = 3\). You test with \(\theta = 1\) under the null, and the p-value is \(1 - F_X(x = 3;\ \theta = 1)\). (a) Evaluate the p-value and conclude at \(\alpha = 0.05\). (b) Extension: find the rejection-region boundary. (c) Extension: redo it as a two-tailed test.

  1. Identify. The p-value has the form \(1 - F\), the upper-tailed row. So \(H_0: \theta = 1\) and \(H_A: \theta > 1\). One observation, so \(Y = X\).
  2. c.d.f. \(F_X(x;\theta) = 1 - e^{-x/\theta}\), so \(F_X(3; 1) = 1 - e^{-3}\).
  3. (a) p-value. \(p = 1 - (1 - e^{-3}) = e^{-3} = 0.0498\). Since \(0.0498 < 0.05\), reject \(H_0\) (just barely): there is evidence that \(\theta > 1\).
  4. (b) Boundary. Upper-tailed, so \(q = 1 - \alpha = 0.95\): \(1 - e^{-y_{\rm RR}/1} = 0.95 \Rightarrow e^{-y_{\rm RR}} = 0.05 \Rightarrow y_{\rm RR} = -\ln 0.05 = 2.996\). Reject if \(x \ge 2.996\). Since \(3 \ge 2.996\), same decision as (a).
  5. (c) Two-tailed p. \(F(3;1) = 0.9502\) and \(1 - F = 0.0498\). \(p = 2\times\min(0.9502,\ 0.0498) = 2e^{-3} = 0.0996\). Since \(0.0996 \ge 0.05\), fail to reject: \(\theta = 1\) is plausible.
  6. (c) Two-tailed boundaries. \(q = 0.025\): \(y_{\rm RR,lo} = -\ln(0.975) = 0.0253\). \(q = 0.975\): \(y_{\rm RR,hi} = -\ln(0.025) = 3.689\). The observed 3 lies between them, so fail to reject. Consistent.

Answer: (a) \(p = e^{-3} = 0.0498\), reject. (b) \(y_{\rm RR} = 2.996\). (c) \(p = 2e^{-3} = 0.0996\), fail to reject; boundaries 0.0253 and 3.689.

Worked example · HW5 Q1 and Q2

You sample one datum from \(f_X(x;\theta) = \theta x^{\theta-1}\), \(x \in [0,1]\), \(\theta > 0\). Q1: (a) Find \(F_X(x)\). (b) Test \(H_0: \theta = 1\) vs \(H_A: \theta > 1\) with \(y_{\rm obs} = x_{\rm obs} = 0.98\) at \(\alpha = 0.05\): p-value and conclusion. (c) The p-value for a two-tailed test. Q2: (a) Find the inverse c.d.f. (b) Find \(y_{\rm RR}\) for the upper-tailed test at \(\alpha = 0.05\). (c) Find the power at \(\theta = 2\) and interpret it.

  1. Q1(a) c.d.f. \(F_X(x) = \int_0^x \theta t^{\theta-1}\,dt = \big[t^\theta\big]_0^x = x^\theta\) for \(x \in [0,1]\) (0 below, 1 above).
  2. Why upper-tailed makes sense. As \(\theta\) grows, \(x^\theta\) gets smaller for each \(x\), so more probability sits near 1. Bigger \(\theta\) means bigger \(X\), so "\(\theta > 1\)" means "reject for large \(x\)".
  3. Q1(b) p-value. Upper row: \(p = 1 - F(0.98; 1) = 1 - 0.98^1 = 0.02 < 0.05\). Reject \(H_0\); there is evidence that \(\theta > 1\).
  4. Q1(c) two-tailed. \(p = 2\times\min(0.98,\ 0.02) = 0.04\).
  5. Q2(a) inverse. \(q = x^\theta \Rightarrow x = F_X^{-1}(q) = q^{1/\theta}\).
  6. Q2(b) boundary. \(y_{\rm RR} = F^{-1}(1-\alpha;\theta_0) = 0.95^{1/1} = 0.95\). Reject if \(x_{\rm obs} > 0.95\).
  7. Q2(c) power. Upper row with the true \(\theta = 2\): \(1 - F(0.95; 2) = 1 - 0.95^2 = 1 - 0.9025 = 0.0975\).
  8. Interpret. From the key: if the null is \(\theta_0 = 1\) but the true \(\theta = 2\), then with a single datum there is a 9.75% chance of rejecting the null (compared to 5% if \(\theta\) were truly 1). One observation gives very little power.

Answer: \(F = x^\theta\); \(p = 0.02\), reject; two-tailed \(p = 0.04\); \(F^{-1}(q) = q^{1/\theta}\); \(y_{\rm RR} = 0.95\); power(2) \(= 0.0975\).

Worked example · Lecture 6M, power for the CMU weights test

"We record the weights of \(n = 25\) CMU students and assume that these data are normally distributed with mean \(\mu\) and known variance \(\sigma^2 = 400\). Our null hypothesis is that the average student weight is 140 pounds, and our alternative hypothesis is that students are heavier than 140 pounds. Assuming that the level of our test is \(\alpha = 0.05\), what is the test power if \(\mu = 144\) pounds? How about \(\mu = 136\) pounds?" Use the c.d.f. values \(F(146.58;\ 144, 4) = 0.741\) and \(F(146.58;\ 136, 4) = 0.996\).

  1. Identify. \(H_0: \mu = 140\), \(H_A: \mu > 140\): "This is a right-tailed test (second row)" of the power table.
  2. Statistic. \(\sigma\) is known, so \(Y = \bar X \sim N(\mu, \sigma^2/n)\). The standard deviation of \(\bar X\) is \(\sigma/\sqrt n = 20/5 = 4\). (Use 4, not 20: the statistic is the mean, not one weight.)
  3. Boundary. \(y_{\rm RR} = F_Y^{-1}(1-\alpha;\theta_0) = F^{-1}(0.95;\ 140, 4) = 146.58\). (Software value. In \(z\) terms: \(140 + 1.645 \times 4 = 146.58\).)
  4. Power at 144. \(1 - F(146.58;\ 144, 4) = 1 - 0.741 = 0.259\). Instructor's interpretation: "If the null is \(\mu_0 = 140\) but \(\mu\) actually equals 144 and we collect \(n = 25\) data, we have a 25.9% chance of rejecting the null hypothesis in any one such experiment." So \(\beta = 0.741\).
  5. Power at 136. \(1 - F(146.58;\ 136, 4) = 1 - 0.996 = 0.004\): "under these conditions we would have a 0.4% chance of rejection."
  6. Why 0.004 is below \(\alpha\). 136 is on the wrong side of 140 for an upper-tailed test. The curve moves away from the rejection region, so the test almost never rejects. A one-tailed test is blind in the other direction.

Answer: \(y_{\rm RR} = 146.58\); power(144) \(= 0.259\) (\(\beta = 0.741\)); power(136) \(= 0.004\).

Worked example · Original practice, exponential power with full algebra

A part's lifetime \(Y\) (years) is exponential with mean \(\theta\). The supplier's current design has \(\theta = 2\). You test one part of a new design to see whether it lasts longer: \(H_0: \theta = 2\) vs \(H_A: \theta > 2\), \(\alpha = 0.05\). (a) Find the rejection region. (b) Find the power and \(\beta\) at \(\theta' = 4\). (c) Find the power at \(\theta' = 2,\ 3,\ 6,\ 10\). (d) Redo (a) and (b) as a two-tailed test. (e) You observe \(y_{\rm obs} = 6.5\). Find the upper-tailed and two-tailed p-values.

  1. (a) Set up the boundary. Upper-tailed, so \(q = 1 - \alpha = 0.95\), with \(\theta_0 = 2\): \(1 - e^{-y_{\rm RR}/2} = 0.95\).
  2. (a) Solve. \(e^{-y_{\rm RR}/2} = 0.05 \Rightarrow -y_{\rm RR}/2 = \ln 0.05 = -2.996 \Rightarrow y_{\rm RR} = 5.991\). (Same as \(2\ln 20\).) Reject \(H_0\) if \(y \ge 5.991\).
  3. (b) Power at 4. Upper row with \(\theta' = 4\): \(1 - F(5.991; 4) = 1 - (1 - e^{-5.991/4}) = e^{-1.498} = 0.224\). So \(\beta = 1 - 0.224 = 0.776\). Check: \(\alpha^{\theta_0/\theta'} = 0.05^{1/2} = 0.2236\).
  4. (c) Other alternatives. \(e^{-5.991/\theta'}\): \(\theta' = 2\): \(e^{-2.996} = 0.050\) (equals \(\alpha\), as it must). \(\theta' = 3\): \(e^{-1.997} = 0.136\). \(\theta' = 6\): \(e^{-0.999} = 0.368\). \(\theta' = 10\): \(e^{-0.599} = 0.549\). Power climbs as \(\theta'\) moves away from \(\theta_0\).
  5. (d) Two-tailed boundaries. \(q = 0.025\): \(1 - e^{-y/2} = 0.025 \Rightarrow y_{\rm RR,lo} = -2\ln 0.975 = 0.0506\). \(q = 0.975\): \(e^{-y/2} = 0.025 \Rightarrow y_{\rm RR,hi} = -2\ln 0.025 = 7.378\). Reject if \(y \le 0.0506\) or \(y \ge 7.378\).
  6. (d) Two-tailed power at 4. \(F(0.0506; 4) + 1 - F(7.378; 4) = (1 - e^{-0.0127}) + e^{-1.844} = 0.0126 + 0.1581 = 0.171\). Lower than the one-tailed 0.224: the upper boundary moved out from 5.991 to 7.378 because only \(\alpha/2\) sits in each tail.
  7. (e) p-values. \(F(6.5; 2) = 1 - e^{-3.25} = 1 - 0.0388 = 0.9612\). Upper-tailed: \(p = 0.0388 < 0.05\), reject. Two-tailed: \(p = 2\times\min(0.9612,\ 0.0388) = 0.0775 \ge 0.05\), fail to reject. Check with the regions: \(6.5 \ge 5.991\) (in the one-tailed region) but \(6.5 < 7.378\) (outside the two-tailed region).

Answer: (a) reject if \(y \ge 5.991\). (b) power 0.224, \(\beta\) 0.776. (c) 0.050, 0.136, 0.368, 0.549. (d) boundaries 0.0506 and 7.378; power 0.171. (e) \(p = 0.0388\) (reject) one-tailed; \(p = 0.0775\) (fail to reject) two-tailed.

What makes power go up or down

The study guide asks you to "know how power relates to, e.g., sample size, changing the specific alternative hypothesis, and the value of the other parameter (if one exists)." Power is high when the null curve and the true curve are easy to tell apart. The lecture's margin note: "(θ, θ₀ further; σ smaller)". The numbers below are for the 6M weights test (upper-tailed, \(\mu_0 = 140\)), with everything else held at \(n = 25\), \(\sigma = 20\), \(\alpha = 0.05\), \(\mu = 144\). They are normal c.d.f. values, stated as given.

Change (others fixed)PowerWhyWeights example
\(n\) upup\(\sigma/\sqrt n\) shrinks, both curves get narrower and overlap less.\(n = 9\): 0.148 · 25: 0.259 · 100: 0.639 · 400: 0.991
\(\theta'\) farther from \(\theta_0\) (in the \(H_A\) direction)upThe true curve slides deeper into the rejection region.\(\mu = 140\): 0.050 · 144: 0.259 · 148: 0.639 · 150: 0.804
\(\theta'\) on the wrong side (one-tailed test)below \(\alpha\)The true curve slides away from the region.\(\mu = 136\): 0.004
\(\alpha\) upupThe boundary moves toward \(\theta_0\), the region grows. Cost: more Type I errors.\(\alpha = 0.01\): 0.092 · 0.05: 0.259 · 0.10: 0.389
\(\sigma\) up (the other, "nuisance", parameter)downBoth curves get wider and overlap more.\(\sigma = 10\): 0.639 · 20: 0.259 · 40: 0.126
\(\theta' = \theta_0\)\(= \alpha\), for every \(n\)The boundary is built so the null area is exactly \(\alpha\).0.050 at \(\mu = 140\)
two-tailed instead of one-taileddown on the \(H_A\) side, up on the other sideOnly \(\alpha/2\) per tail, but both directions are covered.\(\mu = 144\): 0.170 vs 0.259 · \(\mu = 136\): 0.170 vs 0.004

Here \(\sigma\) and \(n\) enter only through \(\sigma/\sqrt n\). That is why \(\sigma = 10\) with \(n = 25\) and \(\sigma = 20\) with \(n = 100\) give the same power, 0.639: halving \(\sigma\) is worth quadrupling \(n\).

Sample size: how many data for a target power?

This is "a very standard exercise in experimental design" (6M): before you collect anything, guess a realistic \(\theta'\), pick a target power (0.8 or 0.9), and find the smallest \(n\) that reaches it. Power rises with \(n\), so you solve \(\text{power}(n) - \text{target} = 0\) for \(n\) and "round up (always!) to the nearest integer". Rounding down would leave the power just below the target.

Lecture 6M: weights test, \(\mu' = 144\), \(\sigma = 20\), \(\alpha = 0.05\), target 0.8. The instructor solved it numerically ("This is a coding exercise") and got \(n = 154.56\), so \(n = 155\). For the normal with \(\sigma\) known there is also a closed form:

\[ n = \left[\frac{(z_{1-\alpha} + z_{\rm target})\,\sigma}{\theta' - \theta_0}\right]^2 = \left[\frac{(1.645 + 0.842)(20)}{144 - 140}\right]^2 = (12.435)^2 = 154.6 \;\to\; 155 \]

Not on the sheet (derived from the upper-tailed power row). \(z_{1-\alpha}\) and \(z_{\rm target}\) are standard normal inverse c.d.f. values, which an exam would give (\(z_{0.95} = 1.645\), \(z_{0.8} = 0.842\)). Read it qualitatively: \(n\) grows with \(\sigma^2\) and shrinks with \((\theta'-\theta_0)^2\). Halve the gap you want to detect and you need four times the data.

HW5 Q8 did the same for an exponential: \(H_0: \theta = 3\) vs the specific alternative \(\theta' = 2.5\), lower-tailed (because \(\theta' < \theta_0\)), target power 0.9, with \(\bar X \sim \text{Gamma}(n, \theta/n)\). Same two-column recipe (boundary from \(F^{-1}(\alpha;\theta_0)\), power \(= F(y_{\rm RR};\theta')\)), but the Gamma c.d.f. needs a computer. The answer was \(n = 260\). On the exam, expect the concept: which row, which way \(n\) moves, round up.

Check yourself · 6M worksheet

"For a left-tailed hypothesis test: (a) how does the power change if \(\theta < \theta_0\) and \(\theta\) gets smaller? (b) how does the power change if \(\theta < \theta_0\) and \(n\) gets smaller? (c) how does the power change if \(\theta = \theta_0\) and \(n\) gets bigger? (increases/decreases/stays the same)"

Hint

Left-tailed: reject when \(Y \le y_{\rm RR}\), with \(y_{\rm RR} = F_Y^{-1}(\alpha;\theta_0)\) fixed. Power \(= F_Y(y_{\rm RR};\theta)\). Picture where the true curve sits relative to that boundary.

Full solution
  1. (a) As the true \(\theta\) moves further below \(\theta_0\), the distribution of \(Y\) slides left, so more of it falls left of the fixed boundary. Increases. (Weights version, \(y_{\rm RR} = 133.42\): power 0.259 at \(\mu = 136\), 0.639 at \(\mu = 132\).)
  2. (b) Smaller \(n\) widens the sampling distribution (\(\sigma/\sqrt n\) grows). The boundary moves farther from \(\theta_0\) and the curves overlap more, so \(\theta\) and \(\theta_0\) are harder to tell apart. Decreases. (\(\mu = 136\): \(n = 25\) gives 0.259, \(n = 9\) gives 0.148.)
  3. (c) At \(\theta = \theta_0\), power is the probability of rejecting a true null, which is \(\alpha\) by construction for every \(n\). Stays the same.

(a) increases, (b) decreases, (c) stays the same (equals \(\alpha\)).

Check yourself · HW5 Q3

You sample one datum from \(f_X(x;\theta) = \theta/x^{\theta+1}\), \(x \ge 1\), \(\theta > 0\). (a) Find \(F_X(x)\). (b) Test \(H_0: \theta = 2\) vs \(H_A: \theta < 2\) with \(x_{\rm obs} = 3\) at \(\alpha = 0.05\). The problem says: use the upper-tail p-value formula here, "because as \(\theta\) gets smaller, the expected value of our statistic \(Y = X\) gets bigger".

Full solution
  1. (a) \(F_X(x) = \int_1^x \theta t^{-\theta-1}\,dt = \big[-t^{-\theta}\big]_1^x = 1 - x^{-\theta} = 1 - \dfrac{1}{x^\theta}\).
  2. (b) Small \(\theta\) produces large \(x\), so evidence for \(\theta < 2\) is a large \(x\): the extreme direction is up. \(p = 1 - F(3; 2) = 1 - (1 - 1/3^2) = 1/9 = 0.111\).
  3. \(0.111 \ge 0.05\): fail to reject \(H_0\); \(\theta = 2\) is plausible.

\(F = 1 - x^{-\theta}\); \(p = 1/9 = 0.111\); fail to reject.

Heads up

This "reversed" direction is a quirk of this one distribution. The instructor's practice-question notes say reversing "is not something you'll have to worry about for the exam itself: no reversing would be necessary there." On the exam, use the table rows as printed.

Check yourself · Original practice, exponential lower-tailed power

One exponential observation. \(H_0: \theta = 2\) vs \(H_A: \theta < 2\), \(\alpha = 0.05\). (a) Find the rejection region. (b) Find the power at \(\theta' = 1\) and at \(\theta' = 0.5\). (c) Find \(\beta\) at \(\theta' = 1\). (d) What is the p-value if \(y_{\rm obs} = 0.08\)?

Full solution
  1. (a) Lower-tailed, \(q = \alpha = 0.05\): \(1 - e^{-y_{\rm RR}/2} = 0.05 \Rightarrow e^{-y_{\rm RR}/2} = 0.95 \Rightarrow y_{\rm RR} = -2\ln 0.95 = 0.1026\). Reject if \(y \le 0.1026\).
  2. (b) Lower row: power \(= F(0.1026;\theta') = 1 - e^{-0.1026/\theta'}\). At \(\theta' = 1\): \(1 - e^{-0.1026} = 1 - 0.9025 = 0.0975\). At \(\theta' = 0.5\): \(1 - e^{-0.2052} = 1 - 0.8145 = 0.1855\). Check: \(1 - 0.95^{2/1} = 0.0975\) and \(1 - 0.95^{4} = 0.1855\).
  3. (c) \(\beta = 1 - 0.0975 = 0.9025\). With one observation, this test misses \(\theta = 1\) about 90% of the time.
  4. (d) Lower row: \(p = F(0.08; 2) = 1 - e^{-0.04} = 0.0392 < 0.05\): reject; evidence that \(\theta < 2\). (And \(0.08 \le 0.1026\), in the region.)

\(y_{\rm RR} = 0.1026\); power 0.0975 at 1 and 0.1855 at 0.5; \(\beta = 0.9025\); \(p = 0.0392\).

Check yourself · Original practice, exponential two-tailed

One exponential observation, \(H_0: \theta = 1\) vs \(H_A: \theta \ne 1\), \(\alpha = 0.05\). (a) Find both boundaries. (b) You observe \(y_{\rm obs} = 3.2\). Find the p-value and conclude. (c) Find the power at \(\theta' = 3\).

Full solution
  1. (a) \(q = 0.025\): \(y_{\rm RR,lo} = -\ln(0.975) = 0.0253\). \(q = 0.975\): \(y_{\rm RR,hi} = -\ln(0.025) = 3.689\).
  2. (b) \(F(3.2; 1) = 1 - e^{-3.2} = 1 - 0.0408 = 0.9592\). \(p = 2\times\min(0.9592,\ 0.0408) = 0.0815 \ge 0.05\): fail to reject; \(\theta = 1\) is plausible. (Check: \(0.0253 < 3.2 < 3.689\), outside both tails.)
  3. (c) Two-tailed row with \(\theta' = 3\): \(F(0.0253; 3) + 1 - F(3.689; 3) = (1 - e^{-0.0084}) + e^{-1.230} = 0.0084 + 0.2924 = 0.301\).

Boundaries 0.0253 and 3.689; \(p = 0.0815\), fail to reject; power(3) \(= 0.301\).

Check yourself · Original practice, uniform c.d.f.

One observation \(Y \sim \text{Uniform}(0,\theta)\), so \(F(y;\theta) = y/\theta\) for \(0 \le y \le \theta\). (a) \(H_0: \theta = 1\) vs \(H_A: \theta > 1\), \(\alpha = 0.05\): find \(y_{\rm RR}\) and the power at \(\theta' = 2\). (b) What is the power at \(\theta' = 1\)? (c) Lower-tailed version, \(H_A: \theta < 1\): find \(y_{\rm RR}\) and the power at \(\theta' = 0.5\).

Full solution
  1. (a) \(y_{\rm RR}/1 = 0.95 \Rightarrow y_{\rm RR} = 0.95\). Power \(= 1 - F(0.95; 2) = 1 - 0.95/2 = 0.525\).
  2. (b) \(1 - 0.95/1 = 0.05 = \alpha\), as always at \(\theta_0\).
  3. (c) \(y_{\rm RR}/1 = 0.05 \Rightarrow y_{\rm RR} = 0.05\). Power \(= F(0.05; 0.5) = 0.05/0.5 = 0.10\).

(a) 0.95 and 0.525; (b) 0.05; (c) 0.05 and 0.10.

Check yourself · Lecture 6M extension, two-tailed normal power

Weights again: \(n = 25\), \(\sigma = 20\) known, \(\alpha = 0.05\), but now \(H_A: \mu \ne 140\). The two-tailed boundaries are \(140 \pm 1.96 \times 4\). Given \(F(132.16;\ 144, 4) = 0.0015\) and \(F(147.84;\ 144, 4) = 0.8315\), find the power at \(\mu = 144\). Compare with the upper-tailed power 0.259 and explain the difference.

Full solution
  1. Boundaries: \(y_{\rm RR,lo} = 140 - 7.84 = 132.16\), \(y_{\rm RR,hi} = 140 + 7.84 = 147.84\).
  2. Two-tailed row: \(F(132.16; 144) + 1 - F(147.84; 144) = 0.0015 + 1 - 0.8315 = 0.170\).
  3. 0.170 is less than 0.259 because only \(\alpha/2 = 0.025\) sits in the upper tail, so the upper boundary moved out from 146.58 to 147.84. The payoff: the two-tailed test also has power 0.170 at \(\mu = 136\), where the upper-tailed test had 0.004.

Power(144) \(= 0.170\), lower than 0.259 because the upper boundary moved out.

Check yourself · Sample size (6M and HW5 Q8)

(a) In the 6M setting (\(\mu' = 144\), \(\mu_0 = 140\), \(\alpha = 0.05\), target power 0.8), find \(n\) if \(\sigma = 10\) instead of 20. Use \(z_{0.95} = 1.645\), \(z_{0.8} = 0.842\). (b) Without computing, what happens to the required \(n\) if \(\sigma = 40\)? (c) In HW5 Q8 (exponential, \(\theta_0 = 3\), \(\theta' = 2.5\), target 0.9, answer \(n = 260\)), does \(n\) go up or down if the target is 0.8? If \(\theta' = 2\)? If \(\alpha = 0.01\)? (d) Why is HW5 Q8 lower-tailed?

Full solution
  1. (a) \(n = [(1.645 + 0.842)(10)/4]^2 = (6.2175)^2 = 38.66\), round up to 39.
  2. (b) \(n\) scales with \(\sigma^2\): doubling \(\sigma\) multiplies \(n\) by 4. \(4 \times 154.56 = 618.3\), so 619.
  3. (c) Lower target: fewer data needed, \(n\) goes down. \(\theta' = 2\) is farther from 3, easier to detect: \(n\) goes down. Smaller \(\alpha\) shrinks the rejection region and lowers power at each \(n\): \(n\) goes up.
  4. (d) The specific alternative 2.5 is below \(\theta_0 = 3\), and for the exponential smaller \(\theta\) gives smaller \(\bar X\). So you reject for small \(\bar X\): the lower-tailed row, \(y_{\rm RR} = F^{-1}(\alpha;\theta_0)\) and power \(= F(y_{\rm RR};\theta')\).

(a) 39. (b) 4 times as many (619). (c) down, down, up. (d) \(\theta' < \theta_0\).

Check yourself · Original practice (conceptual)

(a) Using the exponential shortcut, show algebraically that the upper-tailed power equals \(\alpha\) at \(\theta' = \theta_0\) and goes to 1 as \(\theta' \to \infty\). (b) A classmate computes power by finding the boundary with \(\theta'\) instead of \(\theta_0\). What answer will they always get, and why is it wrong?

Full solution
  1. (a) Power \(= \alpha^{\theta_0/\theta'}\). At \(\theta' = \theta_0\) the exponent is 1, so power \(= \alpha\). As \(\theta' \to \infty\), the exponent \(\theta_0/\theta' \to 0\), and \(\alpha^0 = 1\).
  2. (b) A boundary built from \(\theta'\) cuts off area \(\alpha\) under the \(\theta'\) curve, so their "power" is always \(\alpha\). The rejection region must come from the null (it is "defined assuming the null hypothesis is true"); only the second step uses \(\theta'\).

(a) \(\alpha^1 = \alpha\); \(\alpha^0 = 1\). (b) They always get \(\alpha\); the boundary must use \(\theta_0\).

Common mistakes

  • Finding \(y_{\rm RR}\) with \(\theta'\). The boundary always comes from \(\theta_0\) and \(\alpha\).
  • Computing power with \(\theta_0\) in the second step. The power column uses the true \(\theta\) (\(\theta'\)).
  • Using \(q = \alpha\) for an upper-tailed boundary. Upper needs \(q = 1-\alpha\): area \(\alpha\) on the right means \(1-\alpha\) on the left.
  • Two-tailed slips: using \(\alpha\) instead of \(\alpha/2\) per tail, forgetting to double the p-value, or dropping one of the two power terms.
  • Using the spread of one observation (\(\sigma = 20\)) instead of the statistic's spread (\(\sigma/\sqrt n = 4\)).
  • Sign errors with logs: \(\ln 0.05 = -2.996\) is negative, so \(y_{\rm RR} = -\theta_0\ln 0.05\) is positive.
  • Writing \(1 - F\) for the exponential and not simplifying: \(1 - (1 - e^{-y/\theta}) = e^{-y/\theta}\).
  • Rounding a sample size down. Always round up.
  • Saying power at \(\theta_0\) is 0. It is \(\alpha\).

Quick check

The rejection-region boundary depends on the observed data.

False. It depends only on \(\alpha\), \(\theta_0\) and the null distribution. It is set before data are collected.

Exponential, \(\theta_0 = 1\), \(\alpha = 0.05\), upper-tailed: \(y_{\rm RR} = -\ln 0.95\).

False. That is the lower-tailed boundary (0.051). Upper-tailed uses \(q = 0.95\): \(y_{\rm RR} = -\ln(1-0.95) = -\ln 0.05 = 2.996\).

Power at \(\theta = \theta_0\) equals \(\alpha\), whatever \(n\) is.

True. That is the 6M worksheet (c) answer: stays the same.

If \(\sigma\) doubles (others fixed), power goes up.

False. Larger \(\sigma\) widens both curves; power goes down (0.259 → 0.126 in the weights example).

If \(y_{\rm obs}\) is in the rejection region, then \(p < \alpha\).

True. Both rules compare the same areas under the null curve, so they always agree.

A two-tailed test has the same power at \(\theta'\) as the one-tailed test pointing toward \(\theta'\).

False. It has less there (0.170 vs 0.259), because each tail only gets \(\alpha/2\).

The sample-size answer 154.56 means you collect 154 data.

False. Round up, always: 155.

Upper-tailed exponential test: power\((\theta') = \alpha^{\theta_0/\theta'}\).

True. \(e^{-y_{\rm RR}/\theta'}\) with \(y_{\rm RR} = -\theta_0\ln\alpha\).

As \(n \to \infty\) with \(\theta' \ne \theta_0\) on the \(H_A\) side, power goes to 1.

True. The curves become needle-thin and stop overlapping (0.991 at \(n = 400\) in the weights example).

Raising \(\alpha\) from 0.05 to 0.10 raises power. Any catch?

True, with a cost. Power at 144 goes 0.259 → 0.389, but the Type I error rate doubles.

If you see this on the exam

  • "Find the p-value" → p-value table: lower \(F(y_{\rm obs};\theta_0)\), upper \(1 - F\), two-tailed \(2\times\min(F, 1-F)\). Exponential upper tail: \(e^{-y/\theta_0}\).
  • "Find the rejection-region boundary" → solve \(F_Y(y_{\rm RR};\theta_0) = q\) with \(q = \alpha\) (lower), \(1-\alpha\) (upper), \(\alpha/2\) and \(1-\alpha/2\) (two). Exponential: \(y_{\rm RR} = -\theta_0\ln(1-q)\).
  • "Compute the power at \(\theta'\)" → boundary from \(\theta_0\) first, then plug it into \(F(\cdot;\theta')\): lower \(F\), upper \(1-F\), two-tailed both tails added. Check: \(\theta' = \theta_0\) gives \(\alpha\).
  • "Normal power" → \(Y = \bar X \sim N(\mu, \sigma^2/n)\), use \(\sigma/\sqrt n\); the c.d.f. values will be given.
  • "How does power change if ..." → \(n\) up: up. \(\theta'\) farther: up. \(\alpha\) up: up. \(\sigma\) up: down. \(\theta' = \theta_0\): stays at \(\alpha\).
  • "Interpret power = 0.259" → "If \(\theta\) really equals \(\theta'\), the test rejects \(H_0\) in about 25.9% of experiments like this one."
  • "How many data for power 0.8?" → smallest \(n\) with power ≥ target; round up; \(n\) grows with \(\sigma^2\), shrinks with \((\theta'-\theta_0)^2\).

Module 5 · Lectures 7F, 8M · HW6 · about 40 min

Confidence intervals: what they mean and how to compute them by hand

A confidence interval is a range of plausible values for an unknown parameter, built from your data. On the exam you will compute one from an algebraic c.d.f. (the study guide marks this "Important") and you will be asked what one means. The interpretation question is where most people lose points.

After this module you can

  • Read every line of the Confidence Intervals block on the formula sheet, including the q table, and say what each symbol is.
  • Explain why the lower bound uses \(q = 1-\alpha/2\) and spot when the lower and upper labels swap.
  • Compute two-sided and one-sided bounds by hand for the exponential and other algebraic c.d.f.s, and for a normal mean with \(\sigma\) known.
  • State the correct interpretation of a 95% interval and knock down the wrong one with the instructor's 190% argument.
  • Use the CI/test duality, and handle discrete (binomial, Poisson) intervals with the \(y_{\rm obs}-1\) rule and the divide-by-\(n\) step.

1. The idea

In plain English

You want to know a number you cannot see directly: a mean lifetime \(\theta\), a coin's heads rate \(p\), a hurricane rate \(\lambda\). Your data give you one best guess. A confidence interval adds error bars: it is the range of parameter values that are consistent with what you observed.

"Consistent" has a precise meaning. A candidate value \(\theta\) is in the 95% interval if, assuming that \(\theta\) were true, your observed statistic would not land in the extreme 5% of its sampling distribution (2.5% in each tail). Values of \(\theta\) that would make your data look extreme are thrown out. What is left is the interval.

The "95%" is a promise about the method: if you repeated the whole experiment many times and built an interval each time, about 95% of those intervals would contain the true value. It is a statement about the procedure, made before you see the data.

2. Vocabulary

SymbolNameWhat it means
\(\theta\)parameterThe fixed, unknown number you want to pin down (for example the exponential mean \(\theta\), a normal mean \(\mu\), a binomial \(p\), a Poisson \(\lambda\)). It does not move. It is not random.
\(X_1,\dots,X_n\)dataYour \(n\) observations, assumed to come from a known family with parameter \(\theta\).
\(Y\)statisticOne number computed from the data that carries the information about \(\theta\) (the sufficient statistic): \(X\) itself if \(n=1\), the sum \(X_+\) for binomial or Poisson data, \(\overline X\) for normal data.
\(y_{\rm obs}\)observed valueThe actual number you got for \(Y\) from your data.
\(F_Y(y;\theta)\)c.d.f. of the sampling distribution\(P(Y \le y)\) when the parameter equals \(\theta\). It is a function of two things: the value \(y\) and the parameter \(\theta\).
\(\alpha\)alphaA number you choose before collecting data. \(\alpha = 0.05\) means a 95% interval; \(\alpha = 0.10\) means a 90% interval.
\(1-\alpha\)confidence coefficient (confidence level)The long-run fraction of intervals, built by this method, that should contain \(\theta\).
\(\hat\theta_L,\ \hat\theta_U\)lower and upper boundsThe two ends of the interval. The hats mark them as estimates computed from data, so they are random: new data give new bounds.
\(q\)quantile levelThe target c.d.f. value you plug into the equation. It comes from \(\alpha\) and from which bound you want (the q table).
coveragecoverageThe actual long-run fraction of intervals that contain the true \(\theta\). Ideally equal to \(1-\alpha\).

3. Decoding the Confidence Intervals block on the sheet

The block sits in the upper right of your formula sheet. Find it now. It has four lines and a small table. Here they are one at a time.

Confidence Intervals · line 1

\[ \text{Interval } [\hat\theta_L,\hat\theta_U] \text{ defined such that } P(\hat\theta_L \le \theta \le \hat\theta_U) = 1-\alpha \]

"The interval from theta-hat-L to theta-hat-U is built so that the probability it ends up containing theta is one minus alpha."

\(\hat\theta_L,\ \hat\theta_U\)
the lower and upper bounds. They are computed from the data, so they are the random quantities in this probability.
\(\theta\)
the true parameter. A fixed number. It is the target, and it never moves.
\(P(\cdots)\)
a probability over repeated experiments: before you collect data, the chance that the interval you are about to build will land on \(\theta\). The instructor's words: "long-term probability that \([\hat\theta_L,\hat\theta_U]\) overlaps \(\theta\) is \(1-\alpha\)."
\(1-\alpha\)
the confidence level. \(\alpha=0.05\) gives a "95% confidence interval".

Confidence Intervals · line 2

\[ \text{One-sided bounds: } [\hat\theta_L,\infty) \text{ or } (-\infty,\hat\theta_U] \]

"Sometimes you only want one end: a lower bound with nothing above it, or an upper bound with nothing below it."

\([\hat\theta_L,\infty)\)
one-sided lower bound: "\(\theta\) is at least this big." Use it when only small values worry you (for example a minimum mean lifetime).
\((-\infty,\hat\theta_U]\)
one-sided upper bound: "\(\theta\) is at most this big." When \(\theta\) must be positive (an exponential mean), the interval is really \((0,\hat\theta_U]\). HW6 Q3(b) says it directly: "The lower bound would be, by definition, zero."

Confidence Intervals · line 3

\[ \text{Confidence coefficient: } 1-\alpha \]

"The confidence coefficient is one minus alpha: the 0.95 in a 95% interval."

\(1-\alpha\)
the same \(1-\alpha\) as in line 1. It is the nominal (advertised) coverage. "Confidence coefficient", "confidence level" and "the 95%" all name this one number.

Confidence Intervals · line 4

\[ \text{Solve } F_Y(y_{\rm obs};\theta) - q = 0 \text{ for } \theta;\ q \text{ via the table} \]

"Plug your observed statistic into its c.d.f., set it equal to q, and solve for the parameter. Each q gives one bound."

\(F_Y\)
the c.d.f. of the sampling distribution of your statistic \(Y\). For one datum, it is just the c.d.f. of \(X\).
\(y_{\rm obs}\)
your observed statistic. It is a known number here. That is the difference from hypothesis testing.
\(\theta\)
the unknown you solve for.
\(q\)
a number between 0 and 1 read from the table below. You solve the equation once per bound.

This is the "fundamental equation of statistical inference" from lecture 5W (Module 2): \(F_Y(y;\theta) - q = 0\). It has two unknowns, \(y\) and \(\theta\). Fix one and solve for the other:

\[ \begin{aligned} &\text{fix } \theta=\theta_0,\ \text{solve for } y &&\Rightarrow\ \text{rejection-region boundary } y_{RR} \\ &\text{fix } y=y_{\rm obs},\ \text{solve for } \theta &&\Rightarrow\ \text{confidence bound } \hat\theta \end{aligned} \]

The Hypothesis Testing block on the sheet says "Solve \(F_Y(y_{RR};\theta_0)-q=0\) for \(y_{RR}\)". The CI block says "Solve \(F_Y(y_{\rm obs};\theta)-q=0\) for \(\theta\)". Same equation, different unknown.

Confidence Intervals · q table

Interval Typeq for Lower Boundq for Upper BoundWhat the row means
one-sided lower\(1-\alpha\)\(-\)Only a lower bound. Solve \(F_Y(y_{\rm obs};\theta) = 1-\alpha\). All of \(\alpha\) goes in one tail. The "\(-\)" means there is no upper bound to compute (it is \(\infty\)).
one-sided upper\(-\)\(\alpha\)Only an upper bound. Solve \(F_Y(y_{\rm obs};\theta) = \alpha\). The lower end is \(-\infty\), or the bottom of the parameter range (0 for a positive \(\theta\)).
two-sided\(1-\alpha/2\)\(\alpha/2\)Both bounds. \(\alpha\) is split in half, \(\alpha/2\) per tail. For 95%: lower uses 0.975, upper uses 0.025. Solve the equation twice.

The pattern to remember: the lower bound always uses the big q (close to 1), and the upper bound uses the small q (close to 0). The instructor repeated "q = 0.025 (upper bound), 0.975 (lower bound)" in every 8M example. The next section shows why, and when it flips.

4. Why the lower bound uses \(q = 1-\alpha/2\)

Take the lecture 8M example: \(n=10\) normal data with \(\sigma = 1\) known, observed \(\overline x_{\rm obs} = 5\). The sampling distribution is \(\overline X \sim N(\mu, \sigma^2/n)\), a bell curve centered at \(\mu\) with standard deviation \(1/\sqrt{10} = 0.316\). You do not know \(\mu\), so imagine sliding the bell curve left and right while the observed value 5 stays put.

  • Slide \(\mu\) down. The curve moves left. Your observed 5 looks bigger and bigger compared with what the curve expects. Stop when 5 is so big it sits right at the edge of the top 2.5% tail. That \(\mu\) is the smallest value you still accept: the lower bound. At that point 97.5% of the curve is to the left of 5, so \(F_Y(5;\mu) = 0.975 = 1-\alpha/2\).
  • Slide \(\mu\) up. The curve moves right. Stop when 5 sits at the edge of the bottom 2.5% tail. That is the upper bound, and \(F_Y(5;\mu) = 0.025 = \alpha/2\).
  • Any \(\mu\) between the two makes 5 a "not surprising" value at level \(\alpha\). That set is the interval.

Another way to see it: plot \(F_Y(y_{\rm obs};\theta)\) as a function of \(\theta\) and read off where it crosses the two q lines.

Heads up: the labels can swap

The q table assumes that a bigger \(\theta\) shifts \(Y\) to larger values, so \(F_Y(y_{\rm obs};\theta)\) decreases as \(\theta\) increases. That is true for every named case in this course: normal \(\mu\), exponential mean \(\theta\), binomial \(p\), Poisson \(\lambda\).

If a bigger \(\theta\) shifts \(Y\) down (data get smaller as \(\theta\) grows), the c.d.f. at \(y_{\rm obs}\) increases with \(\theta\), and the labels swap: \(q=\alpha/2\) gives the lower bound and \(q=1-\alpha/2\) gives the upper bound. For one-sided bounds in that case, the upper bound uses \(q = 1-\alpha\). HW6 Q4 is built on exactly this "quirk".

Safe habit: solve both equations, then call the smaller root \(\hat\theta_L\) and the larger root \(\hat\theta_U\). For a one-sided bound, ask which way \(F\) moves with \(\theta\) before picking q.

5. The method

Method: a confidence interval by hand

  1. What do we know? (the instructor's first line, underlined in 7F). Name the statistic \(Y\) and write down \(y_{\rm obs}\). One datum: \(Y = X\). Several data: use the sufficient statistic on the sheet (\(X_+\), \(\overline X\)).
  2. Write the sampling distribution and its c.d.f. \(F_Y(y;\theta)\). For the exponential the sheet gives only the p.d.f. \(f_X(x) = \frac1\theta e^{-x/\theta}\). Integrate it: \(F_X(x;\theta) = 1 - e^{-x/\theta}\) for \(x \ge 0\).
  3. Get \(\alpha\) from the confidence level (95% means \(\alpha = 0.05\)).
  4. Pick q from the table for each bound you need.
  5. Set \(F_Y(y_{\rm obs};\theta) = q\) and solve for \(\theta\). For exponentials, isolate the exponential and take \(\ln\). You may leave an unevaluated \(\ln(\cdot)\); the 7F worksheet says that is what they expect on an exam.
  6. Check. Lower bound below upper bound? Both inside the allowed range (for example \(\theta > 0\))? If the bigger-q root came out larger, the labels swap (Section 4).
  7. Discrete \(Y\) (binomial, Poisson): use \(y_{\rm obs}-1\) in the lower-bound equation (Section 9). Poisson: the answer is for \(n\lambda\), so divide by \(n\).

6. Computing by hand

The exponential: solve for \(\theta\) explicitly

Every exponential CI follows from one line of algebra. Set \(1 - e^{-y/\theta} = q\):

\[ \begin{aligned} e^{-y_{\rm obs}/\theta} &= 1-q \\ -\frac{y_{\rm obs}}{\theta} &= \ln(1-q) \\ \hat\theta &= \frac{-y_{\rm obs}}{\ln(1-q)} \end{aligned} \]

Because \(1-q\) is between 0 and 1, \(\ln(1-q)\) is negative, and \(\hat\theta\) comes out positive. Watch the "1 minus": with \(q = 0.025\) you take \(\ln(0.975)\), and with \(q = 0.975\) you take \(\ln(0.025)\).

Worked example · Lecture 7F (two-sided, exponential)

You sample one datum from an exponential distribution with c.d.f. \(F_X(x;\theta) = 1 - e^{-x/\theta}\). You observe \(x_{\rm obs} = 4\). Compute a two-sided 95% confidence interval for \(\theta\).

  1. Identify. One datum, so \(Y = X\) and \(y_{\rm obs} = 4\). 95% means \(\alpha = 0.05\). Two-sided, so the table gives \(q = 1-\alpha/2 = 0.975\) for the lower bound and \(q = \alpha/2 = 0.025\) for the upper bound.
  2. Upper bound. \(1 - e^{-4/\theta} = 0.025\), so \(e^{-4/\theta} = 0.975\). Take logs: \(-4/\theta = \ln(0.975) = -0.025318\). So \(\hat\theta_U = -4/\ln(0.975) = 4/0.025318 = 157.99\).
  3. Lower bound. \(1 - e^{-4/\theta} = 0.975\), so \(e^{-4/\theta} = 0.025\). Then \(-4/\theta = \ln(0.025) = -3.6889\). So \(\hat\theta_L = -4/\ln(0.025) = 4/3.6889 = 1.084\).
  4. Check. \(1.08 < 157.99\) and both are positive. Direction check: at \(\theta = 157.99\) the mean is huge, so seeing only 4 is a surprisingly small value (bottom 2.5%). At \(\theta = 1.08\), seeing 4 is surprisingly large (top 2.5%). Both make sense.
  5. Conclude. The 95% CI is very wide. The instructor's margin note: with only one data point you cannot pin \(\theta\) down precisely.

Answer: \([\hat\theta_L, \hat\theta_U] = [-4/\ln(0.025),\ -4/\ln(0.975)] = [1.08,\ 157.99]\).

Heads up: class slip

The handwritten lecture notes box the upper bound as 158.99. That is an arithmetic slip. \(-4/\ln(0.975) = 157.99\). Check it: plugging 158.99 back in gives \(1-e^{-4/158.99} = 0.0248\), not 0.025. Use 157.99. The lower bound 1.08 in the notes is correct.

Worked example · 7F worksheet (one-sided upper, exponential)

You sample one datum from an exponential distribution with c.d.f. \(F_X(x;\theta) = 1 - e^{-x/\theta}\) and observe \(x_{\rm obs} = 1\). Compute a one-sided 90% upper bound on \(\theta\). You can leave an unevaluated \(\ln(\cdot)\) in your answer.

  1. Identify. \(Y = X\), \(y_{\rm obs} = 1\). 90% means \(\alpha = 0.10\). One-sided upper: the table says \(q = \alpha = 0.10\). (One-sided puts all of \(\alpha\) in one tail, so there is no halving.)
  2. Set up. \(F_Y(1;\theta) - 0.1 = 0\), that is \(1 - e^{-1/\theta} = 0.1\).
  3. Solve. \(e^{-1/\theta} = 0.9\). Take logs: \(-1/\theta = \ln(0.9) = -0.10536\). So \(\hat\theta_U = -1/\ln(0.9) = 1/\ln(10/9) = 9.49\).
  4. Check. A small q means \(y_{\rm obs} = 1\) sits in the far left tail, which happens when \(\theta\) is large. 9.49 is large compared with 1. Good.
  5. Conclude. Since \(\theta > 0\), the one-sided interval is \((0,\ 9.49]\).

Answer: \(\hat\theta_U = -1/\ln(0.9) \approx 9.49\); the 90% one-sided interval is \((-\infty, 9.49]\), effectively \((0, 9.49]\).

Worked example · HW6 Q3 (a power-function c.d.f.)

You sample one datum from a distribution with c.d.f. \(F_X(x;\theta) = x^\theta\), where \(x \in [0,1]\) and \(\theta > 0\). You observe \(x_{\rm obs} = 0.9\). (a) Construct a 95% two-sided confidence interval for \(\theta\), with numbers. (b) Construct a 90% one-sided upper bound on \(\theta\). (c) What is the p.d.f. of \(X\)?

  1. Identify. One datum: \(Y = X\), \(y_{\rm obs} = 0.9\). Direction check: \(F(0.9;\theta) = 0.9^\theta\) gets smaller as \(\theta\) grows, so the table applies as printed (lower bound uses the big q).
  2. (a) Lower bound, \(q = 0.975\). \(0.9^\theta = 0.975\). Take logs: \(\theta\ln(0.9) = \ln(0.975)\). So \(\hat\theta_L = \ln(0.975)/\ln(0.9) = (-0.025318)/(-0.10536) = 0.240\).
  3. (a) Upper bound, \(q = 0.025\). \(0.9^\theta = 0.025\), so \(\hat\theta_U = \ln(0.025)/\ln(0.9) = (-3.6889)/(-0.10536) = 35.0\).
  4. (b) One-sided upper, 90%. \(\alpha = 0.10\) and \(q = \alpha = 0.1\): \(0.9^\theta = 0.1\), so \(\hat\theta_U = \ln(0.1)/\ln(0.9) = (-2.3026)/(-0.10536) = 21.9\). The interval is \((0, 21.9]\).
  5. (c) P.d.f. Differentiate the c.d.f.: \(f_X(x) = \frac{d}{dx}x^\theta = \theta x^{\theta-1}\) for \(x\in[0,1]\). (This is a beta distribution.)

Answer: (a) \([0.240,\ 35.0]\); (b) \(\hat\theta_U = 21.9\); (c) \(f_X(x) = \theta x^{\theta-1}\). HW6 Q7 re-did part (a) with software and got the same \([0.2403,\ 35.012]\).

The normal mean with \(\sigma\) known

Normal · Sufficient statistic (\(\sigma\) known)

\[ \overline X \sim N(\mu, \sigma^2/n) \]

"The sample mean is normally distributed, centered at the true mean, with variance sigma squared over n."

\(\overline X\)
the sample mean \(\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n X_i\). This is your \(Y\).
\(\mu\)
the unknown true mean: the \(\theta\) of this problem.
\(\sigma^2/n\)
the variance of \(\overline X\). Its square root \(\sigma/\sqrt n\) is the standard error.

Heads up: sheet typo

The Statistics block prints the sample mean as \(\overline X = \sum_{i=1}^n X_i\). The \(1/n\) is missing. The correct line is \(\overline X = \frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n X_i\).

Standardize: \(F_Y(\overline x_{\rm obs};\mu) = \Phi\!\left(\frac{\overline x_{\rm obs}-\mu}{\sigma/\sqrt n}\right)\), where \(\Phi\) is the standard normal c.d.f. Setting this equal to q means \(\frac{\overline x_{\rm obs}-\mu}{\sigma/\sqrt n} = z_q\), the standard normal value with area q to its left. Solve for \(\mu\):

\[ \mu = \overline x_{\rm obs} - z_q\,\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt n} \quad\Longrightarrow\quad [\hat\mu_L,\hat\mu_U] = \overline x_{\rm obs} \pm z_{1-\alpha/2}\,\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt n} \]

Lower bound: \(q = 1-\alpha/2\), \(z\) positive, so you subtract. Upper bound: \(q = \alpha/2\), \(z\) negative, so you add. The z value would be given on the exam: \(z_{0.975} = 1.96\) (95%), \(z_{0.95} = 1.645\) (90%). This shortcut is not on the sheet.

Worked example · Lecture 8M (normal, \(\sigma\) known)

You collect \(n = 10\) data from a normal distribution with unknown \(\mu\) and known \(\sigma = 1\). The sample mean is \(\overline x_{\rm obs} = 5\). Find the 95% two-sided confidence interval for \(\mu\). Use \(z_{0.975} = 1.96\).

  1. Identify. The instructor's first handwritten line: "sampling distribution: \(\overline X \sim N(\mu, \sigma^2/n)\)." So \(Y = \overline X\), \(y_{\rm obs} = 5\), standard error \(\sigma/\sqrt n = 1/\sqrt{10} = 0.3162\). \(\alpha = 0.05\): \(q = 0.975\) (lower), \(q = 0.025\) (upper).
  2. Lower bound. \(\Phi\big((5-\mu)/0.3162\big) = 0.975\) means \((5-\mu)/0.3162 = 1.96\). So \(\mu = 5 - 1.96(0.3162) = 5 - 0.620 = 4.380\).
  3. Upper bound. \(\Phi\big((5-\mu)/0.3162\big) = 0.025\) means \((5-\mu)/0.3162 = -1.96\). So \(\mu = 5 + 0.620 = 5.620\).
  4. Conclude. The class solved the same equation on a computer and got \([4.38, 5.62]\), with the handwritten note "same as \(\overline x \pm 1.96\,\sigma/\sqrt n\)".

Answer: \([4.38,\ 5.62]\), which is \(5 \pm 0.62\).

Lecture 8M follow-up: \(\sigma\) unknown. If you do not know \(\sigma\) and only have the sample standard deviation \(S\) (here \(S = 1\)), switch to the sheet line "Sufficient statistic (\(\sigma\) unknown): \(T = (\overline X - \mu)/(S/\sqrt n) \sim t(n-1)\)." Replace \(\sigma\) with \(S\) and the normal with the \(t\) distribution with \(n-1 = 9\) degrees of freedom. The interval becomes \(\overline x \pm t_{0.975,9}\,S/\sqrt n\). With the \(t(9)\) value 2.262 (it would be given), that is \(5 \pm 0.715 = [4.285,\ 5.715]\): wider than the \(\sigma\)-known interval, because estimating \(\sigma\) adds uncertainty and the \(t\) has heavier tails. The class did not compute a number for this one.

7. Coverage and how to interpret a CI

Here is what the instructor drew in 7F: the same experiment repeated many times, one interval per repetition, all lined up against the true value. Below are 20 repetitions of the 8M experiment (\(n = 10\), \(\sigma = 1\), true \(\mu = 5\)), each giving its own \(\overline x \pm 0.62\).

  • Correct interpretation (instructor, handwritten): "over \(\infty\) replications, \(100(1-\alpha)\)% will capture \(\theta\)." Equivalently: the long-term probability that \([\hat\theta_L, \hat\theta_U]\) overlaps \(\theta\) is \(1-\alpha\). The 95% describes the method.
  • Wrong interpretation (the lecture says this is not what it means): "there is a 95% chance that \(\theta\) lies in this particular interval, say \([4.38, 5.62]\)." Once you have computed the numbers, nothing random is left. \(\theta\) is either in \([4.38, 5.62]\) or it is not. You just do not know which.

The 190% argument (lecture 7F)

Pretend the wrong interpretation were right. Look at intervals (a) and (b) above. They come from the same 95% procedure, so each would have a "95% chance of containing \(\theta\)". But they do not overlap, so \(\theta\) cannot be in both. The chance that \(\theta\) is in (a) or (b) would then be \(95\% + 95\% = 190\%\). A probability cannot exceed 100%. Contradiction. So the 95% cannot be a probability about one computed interval. The instructor wrote out this "Pretend" paragraph and struck it through in red.

When is coverage exactly \(1-\alpha\)?

Coverage is the actual long-run fraction of intervals that contain \(\theta\). You check it by repeating the experiment (in practice, by simulation): fix a true \(\theta\), generate many data sets, build an interval from each, count the hits. The \(1-\alpha\) on the sheet is the nominal coverage, the target.

  • Continuous sampling distribution, exactly correct: coverage is exactly \(1-\alpha\). The c.d.f. can hit 0.025 and 0.975 exactly, so the tails are exactly \(\alpha/2\) each. (8M worksheet Q2.)
  • Discrete sampling distribution (binomial, Poisson): coverage is generally not exactly \(1-\alpha\). \(Y\) jumps in whole steps, so the tail probabilities jump too, and the coverage depends on the true \(\theta\). For the 8M coin setup (Binom(100, p), 95%, with the \(-1\) rule) the true coverage is 96.7% at \(p=0.2\), 96.5% at \(p=0.5\), 95.9% at \(p=0.68\): close to 95%, never exactly 95%.
  • Approximate sampling distribution (for example invoking the central limit theorem): coverage is only approximately \(1-\alpha\).

8. Tests and intervals: "two sides of the same coin"

Lecture 7F opens with one picture that contains both a test and a confidence interval. Setup: normal data, \(Y = \overline X\), standard error 1, \(\alpha = 0.10\) (so 5% per tail and \(z = 1.645\)). For any candidate \(\mu\), you fail to reject \(H_0\!: \mu\) when \(y_{\rm obs}\) is within \(\mu \pm 1.645\). Those edges are the two parallel green lines.

The duality rule

A two-sided \(100(1-\alpha)\)% confidence interval contains exactly the values \(\theta_0\) that a two-tailed level-\(\alpha\) test would fail to reject. So:

  • \(\theta_0\) outside the interval: reject \(H_0\!: \theta = \theta_0\) at level \(\alpha\).
  • \(\theta_0\) inside the interval: fail to reject \(H_0\). This does not show \(\theta = \theta_0\). Every other value in the interval is just as consistent with the data.

Lecture 8M used it right away: the 95% interval for a coin's \(p\) was \([0.579, 0.770]\), which excludes 0.5, so the coin is judged unfair (reject \(H_0\!: p = 0.5\) at \(\alpha = 0.05\)). One-sided bounds pair with one-tailed tests the same way.

9. Discrete sampling distributions

For binomial and Poisson data, \(Y\) is a count, and its c.d.f. is a staircase. Two new rules, neither printed on the sheet:

  1. Lower bound: plug in \(y_{\rm obs}-1\). Solve \(F_Y(y_{\rm obs}-1;\theta) = 1-\alpha/2\) (or \(1-\alpha\) for one-sided). The upper bound still uses \(y_{\rm obs}\): \(F_Y(y_{\rm obs};\theta) = \alpha/2\).
  2. Poisson: divide by \(n\). The sufficient statistic is \(X_+ \sim \text{Pois}(n\lambda)\), so the equation gives bounds on \(n\lambda\). Divide both by \(n\) to get bounds on \(\lambda\).

Why the \(-1\)? The lower bound is the \(\theta\) at which \(y_{\rm obs}\) sits in the top \(\alpha/2\) tail: \(P(Y \ge y_{\rm obs};\theta) = \alpha/2\). "At least \(y_{\rm obs}\)" includes \(y_{\rm obs}\) itself. For a count, \(P(Y \ge y_{\rm obs}) = 1 - P(Y \le y_{\rm obs}-1) = 1 - F_Y(y_{\rm obs}-1)\). Setting that equal to \(\alpha/2\) gives \(F_Y(y_{\rm obs}-1;\theta) = 1-\alpha/2\). For a continuous \(Y\), \(P(Y = y_{\rm obs}) = 0\), so no shift is needed. The instructor's handwritten reason: "subtract 1 in lower bound calc. so coverage is closer to \(1-\alpha\), on average." Without it, the lower bound comes out a bit too high and the interval too narrow.

Worked example · Lecture 8M (binomial and Poisson, set up by hand)

(a) Flip a coin \(k = 10\) times and count heads; repeat for \(n = 10\) experiments. The total is \(y_{\rm obs} = \sum x_i = 68\). Find the 95% two-sided CI for \(p\). (b) Count hurricanes striking Florida each year for \(n = 10\) years. The total is \(y_{\rm obs} = 55\). Find the 95% two-sided CI for the yearly rate \(\lambda\).

  1. (a) Sampling distribution. Sheet, Binomial: "Sufficient statistic: \(X_+ = \sum_{i=1}^n X_i \sim \text{Binom}(nk, p)\)", so \(Y \sim \text{Binom}(100, p)\). Here \(k\) is the number of flips per experiment.
  2. (a) Equations. Lower (big q, and \(y_{\rm obs}-1 = 67\)): \(F_{\text{Binom}(100,p)}(67) = 0.975\). Upper: \(F_{\text{Binom}(100,p)}(68) = 0.025\).
  3. (a) Solve. The binomial c.d.f. is a sum of 68 terms, so the class solved it by computer: \(p_L = 0.579\), \(p_U = 0.770\). On the exam you would be asked to set it up or interpret it. (Without the \(-1\) the lower bound would be 0.590, slightly too high.)
  4. (a) Duality. 0.5 is outside \([0.579, 0.770]\): reject \(H_0\!: p = 0.5\) at \(\alpha = 0.05\). The coin is judged unfair.
  5. (b) Sampling distribution. Sheet, Poisson: \(X_+ \sim \text{Pois}(n\lambda) = \text{Pois}(10\lambda)\). Call \(m = 10\lambda\).
  6. (b) Equations. Lower: \(F_{\text{Pois}(m)}(54) = 0.975\). Upper: \(F_{\text{Pois}(m)}(55) = 0.025\). Solved by computer: \(m_L = 41.43\), \(m_U = 71.59\).
  7. (b) Divide by \(n\). The instructor's note: "returning bounds for \(n\lambda\), so, divide bounds by n." \(\lambda_L = 41.43/10 = 4.143\), \(\lambda_U = 71.59/10 = 7.159\).

Answer: (a) \(p \in [0.579,\ 0.770]\); (b) \(\lambda \in [4.143,\ 7.159]\) hurricanes per year.

Heads up: k on the sheet

In the binomial line, \(k\) is the number of trials per experiment, so \(nk\) is the total number of trials. Elsewhere on the sheet \(k\) means something else: the total count in chi-square tests, the subgroup size in the p chart, and the slack in CUSUM's \(k\sigma\). Read \(k\) from context every time.

Lecture 8M last example: when you cannot write \(F_Y\) down. For \(n = 10\) beta data (with \(b = 2\) known, \(\overline x_{\rm obs} = 0.77\)) the sampling distribution of \(\overline X\) has no formula. The fix: for a trial value of the parameter \(a\), simulate many data sets of size 10, compute each one's mean, and use the fraction of simulated means at or below 0.77 as an estimate \(\hat F(y_{\rm obs}; a)\). Then solve the same equation \(\hat F(y_{\rm obs}; a) - q = 0\). The class got \([3.955,\ 10.257]\). The instructor stressed that this "empirical (simulation-based) c.d.f." is not the e.c.d.f. of your original data used in the KS test. Conceptually: same equation, estimated c.d.f.

10. Check yourself

Check yourself · HW6 Q4 (the swapped labels)

You sample one datum from a distribution with c.d.f. \(F_X(x;\theta) = 1 - \dfrac{1}{x^\theta}\), where \(x \ge 1\) and \(\theta > 0\). You observe \(x_{\rm obs} = 3\). (a) Construct a 95% two-sided confidence interval for \(\theta\), with numbers. (b) Construct a 99% one-sided upper bound on \(\theta\). The homework warns: data get smaller as \(\theta\) gets larger, so the mapping of q to lower and upper bounds reverses.

Hint

Check the direction: \(F(3;\theta) = 1 - 3^{-\theta}\). As \(\theta\) grows, \(3^{-\theta}\) shrinks, so \(F\) grows. That is the swapped case. Solve \(1 - 3^{-\theta} = q\) as \(3^\theta = 1/(1-q)\).

Full solution
  1. General solve. \(1 - 3^{-\theta} = q\) gives \(3^{-\theta} = 1-q\), so \(3^\theta = \frac{1}{1-q}\) and \(\theta = \ln\!\big(\tfrac{1}{1-q}\big)/\ln 3\).
  2. (a) Lower bound uses \(q = \alpha/2 = 0.025\) (swapped). \(3^\theta = 1/0.975 = 1.0256\), so \(\hat\theta_L = \ln(1.0256)/\ln 3 = 0.02532/1.0986 = 0.023\).
  3. (a) Upper bound uses \(q = 1-\alpha/2 = 0.975\). \(3^\theta = 1/0.025 = 40\), so \(\hat\theta_U = \ln 40/\ln 3 = 3.6889/1.0986 = 3.358\).
  4. (a) Check. \(0.023 < 3.358\). Had you used the table blindly you would have gotten the same two numbers with the labels backwards, and a "lower" bound bigger than the "upper" one.
  5. (b) 99% one-sided upper. \(\alpha = 0.01\). In the swapped case the upper bound uses \(q = 1-\alpha = 0.99\). \(3^\theta = 1/0.01 = 100\), so \(\hat\theta_U = \ln 100/\ln 3 = 4.6052/1.0986 = 4.192\).

Answer: (a) \([0.023,\ 3.358]\); (b) \(\hat\theta_U = 4.192\), interval \((0, 4.192]\). These match the official key.

Check yourself · 8M worksheet Q1

When we compute confidence intervals, we subtract one from the observed statistic value \(y_{\rm obs}\): (a) when we work with continuous sampling distributions, (b) when we work with discrete sampling distributions, (c) when we work with any distribution, or (d) never.

Full solution
  1. The \(-1\) comes from turning "at least \(y_{\rm obs}\)" into a c.d.f.: \(P(Y \ge y_{\rm obs}) = 1 - F_Y(y_{\rm obs}-1)\). That identity holds only when \(Y\) takes whole-number steps.
  2. For a continuous \(Y\), \(P(Y \ge y_{\rm obs}) = 1 - F_Y(y_{\rm obs})\) already, so no shift.
  3. And only in the lower-bound equation (the one with the big q).

Answer: (b), discrete sampling distributions, and only for the lower bound.

Check yourself · 8M worksheet Q2

Assume that we are working with an exactly correct sampling distribution (as opposed to an approximation, like when we invoke the central limit theorem). The coverage of our intervals will be exactly \(1-\alpha\): (a) when our sampling distribution is continuous, (b) when our sampling distribution is discrete, (c) for all sampling distributions, or (d) never.

Full solution
  1. Coverage is exactly \(1-\alpha\) when each tail can be made exactly \(\alpha/2\).
  2. A continuous c.d.f. passes through every value between 0 and 1, so it can hit \(\alpha/2\) and \(1-\alpha/2\) exactly.
  3. A discrete c.d.f. jumps, so the achievable tail areas come in steps, and the coverage depends on the true \(\theta\). It hovers near \(1-\alpha\) (96% to 97% for the 8M coin setup at 95%), not exactly on it.

Answer: (a), continuous.

Check yourself · Original practice (interpretation)

A lab measures 10 resistors and reports a 95% confidence interval for the true mean resistance: \([98.2,\ 101.4]\) ohms. Mark each statement right or wrong, and say why.

(1) There is a 95% probability that the true mean is between 98.2 and 101.4.
(2) If the lab repeated the whole study many times, about 95% of the intervals it built this way would contain the true mean.
(3) 95% of the individual resistors have resistance between 98.2 and 101.4.
(4) A two-tailed test of \(H_0\!: \mu = 100\) at \(\alpha = 0.05\) would fail to reject.
(5) The interval proves the true mean is about 100.
(6) A 99% interval from the same data would be narrower.

Full solution
  1. (1) Wrong. The true mean is fixed; this interval either contains it or not. The 95% belongs to the procedure. (If it applied to each interval, two non-overlapping 95% intervals would give a 190% chance.)
  2. (2) Right. This is the instructor's interpretation: over many replications, \(100(1-\alpha)\)% of intervals capture \(\theta\).
  3. (3) Wrong. The interval is about the mean, not individual values. Individual resistors vary much more than their average does (the standard error is \(\sigma/\sqrt n\), smaller than \(\sigma\)).
  4. (4) Right. 100 is inside the 95% interval, so by duality the level-0.05 two-tailed test fails to reject \(H_0\!: \mu = 100\).
  5. (5) Wrong. Nothing is proven. The data are consistent with every value from 98.2 to 101.4.
  6. (6) Wrong. More confidence needs a wider interval: \(q\) moves to 0.995 and 0.005, further into the tails.

Answer: right: (2), (4). Wrong: (1), (3), (5), (6).

Check yourself · Original practice (one-sided exponential bounds)

Same setup as the Lecture 7F example: one exponential datum, \(F_X(x;\theta) = 1-e^{-x/\theta}\), \(x_{\rm obs} = 4\). (a) Find a one-sided 95% upper bound on \(\theta\). (b) Find a one-sided 95% lower bound on \(\theta\). (c) Why is the upper bound in (a) smaller than the two-sided upper bound 157.99?

Full solution
  1. Formula. From Section 6, \(\hat\theta = -y_{\rm obs}/\ln(1-q)\). \(\alpha = 0.05\).
  2. (a) One-sided upper: \(q = \alpha = 0.05\). \(\hat\theta_U = -4/\ln(0.95) = 4/0.051293 = 77.98\). Interval \((0,\ 77.98]\).
  3. (b) One-sided lower: \(q = 1-\alpha = 0.95\). \(\hat\theta_L = -4/\ln(0.05) = 4/2.9957 = 1.335\). Interval \([1.335,\ \infty)\).
  4. (c) The one-sided bound puts all 5% in one tail instead of 2.5%. A 5% tail is easier to reach than a 2.5% tail, so you do not have to push \(\theta\) as far before \(y_{\rm obs}=4\) looks extreme. The same reason makes (b) larger than the two-sided lower bound 1.08.

Answer: (a) \(-4/\ln(0.95) \approx 77.98\); (b) \(-4/\ln(0.05) \approx 1.34\).

Check yourself · Original practice (normal CI and duality)

Same data as the Lecture 8M example: \(n = 10\), \(\sigma = 1\) known, \(\overline x_{\rm obs} = 5\). (a) Find the 90% two-sided CI for \(\mu\), using \(z_{0.95} = 1.645\). (b) Without computing a p-value, decide the two-tailed tests of \(H_0\!: \mu = 4.4\) and \(H_0\!: \mu = 5.4\) at \(\alpha = 0.10\).

Full solution
  1. (a) \(\alpha = 0.10\), so the lower bound uses \(q = 0.95\) and \(z = 1.645\). Margin: \(1.645 \times 0.3162 = 0.520\). Interval \(5 \pm 0.520 = [4.48,\ 5.52]\). It is narrower than the 95% interval \([4.38, 5.62]\), as it should be.
  2. (b) \(\mu_0 = 4.4\): outside \([4.48, 5.52]\), so reject \(H_0\) at \(\alpha = 0.10\). (Check: the two-tailed p-value is 0.058, below 0.10.)
  3. (b) \(\mu_0 = 5.4\): inside, so fail to reject \(H_0\). (The p-value is 0.206.)

Answer: (a) \([4.48,\ 5.52]\); (b) reject \(\mu = 4.4\), fail to reject \(\mu = 5.4\).

Check yourself · HW6 Q8 (Poisson, set up)

Deaths from horse kicks in the Prussian army: 200 corps-years of counts, assumed i.i.d. Poisson(\(\lambda\)). There are 109 zeros, 65 ones, 22 twos, 3 threes and 1 four. (a) What is \(y_{\rm obs}\), and what is its sampling distribution? (b) Write the two equations for a 95% two-sided CI. (c) Software solves them and returns 101.31 and 145.67. What is the CI for \(\lambda\)?

Full solution
  1. (a) The sufficient statistic is the sum: \(y_{\rm obs} = 0(109) + 1(65) + 2(22) + 3(3) + 4(1) = 65 + 44 + 9 + 4 = 122\). With \(n = 200\), \(X_+ \sim \text{Pois}(200\lambda)\).
  2. (b) Let \(m = 200\lambda\). Lower (discrete, so \(y_{\rm obs}-1 = 121\)): \(F_{\text{Pois}(m)}(121) = 0.975\). Upper: \(F_{\text{Pois}(m)}(122) = 0.025\).
  3. (c) Those are bounds on \(m = n\lambda\). Divide by \(n = 200\): \(101.31/200 = 0.507\), \(145.67/200 = 0.728\).
  4. Sense check. The point estimate \(122/200 = 0.61\) is inside.

Answer: \(\lambda \in [0.507,\ 0.728]\) deaths per corps-year, matching the official key.

Check yourself · Original practice (discrete by hand: all heads)

You flip a coin 10 times and get 10 heads. \(Y \sim \text{Binom}(10, p)\), \(y_{\rm obs} = 10\). Find the 95% two-sided CI for \(p\). Does it suggest the coin is unfair?

Hint

The upper equation needs \(F(10;p) = 0.025\), but \(F(10;p) = 1\) for every \(p\). For the lower bound, \(F(9;p) = 1 - P(Y = 10) = 1 - p^{10}\).

Full solution
  1. Upper bound. \(F(10;p) = P(Y \le 10) = 1\) no matter what \(p\) is, so it never equals 0.025. No value of \(p\) is too big; the upper bound is the top of the range, \(p_U = 1\).
  2. Lower bound (discrete, use \(y_{\rm obs}-1 = 9\)): \(F(9;p) = 0.975\). \(F(9;p) = 1 - p^{10}\), so \(p^{10} = 0.025\) and \(p_L = 0.025^{1/10} = 0.692\).
  3. Duality. 0.5 is outside \([0.692, 1]\), so reject \(H_0\!: p = 0.5\) at \(\alpha = 0.05\).

Answer: \([0.692,\ 1]\). Yes: the data are evidence that the coin is unfair (reject \(H_0\!: p = 0.5\)).

Check yourself · Original practice (uniform-type c.d.f.)

One datum from Uniform\((0,\theta)\), so \(F(y;\theta) = y/\theta\) for \(0 \le y \le \theta\). You observe \(y_{\rm obs} = 2\). Find the 95% two-sided CI for \(\theta\).

Full solution
  1. Direction. \(F(2;\theta) = 2/\theta\) decreases as \(\theta\) grows. The table applies as printed.
  2. Lower, \(q = 0.975\). \(2/\theta = 0.975\), so \(\hat\theta_L = 2/0.975 = 2.051\).
  3. Upper, \(q = 0.025\). \(2/\theta = 0.025\), so \(\hat\theta_U = 2/0.025 = 80\).
  4. Check. \(\theta\) must be at least the observed 2 (you cannot see a 2 from Uniform(0, 1.5)). The lower bound 2.051 respects that.

Answer: \([2.051,\ 80]\).

Common mistakes

  • Swapping the q values: the lower bound uses the big q (\(1-\alpha/2\) or \(1-\alpha\)). Re-derive it from the sliding picture if you forget.
  • Using \(\alpha/2\) for a one-sided bound. One-sided puts all of \(\alpha\) in one tail: \(q = \alpha\) or \(1-\alpha\).
  • Applying the table blindly when a bigger \(\theta\) makes the data smaller (HW6 Q4). Check the direction of \(F\) in \(\theta\); the smaller root is always \(\hat\theta_L\).
  • Writing \(\ln(q)\) instead of \(\ln(1-q)\) for the exponential. With \(q = 0.025\) the log is \(\ln(0.975)\).
  • Arithmetic slips with logs. The lecture itself boxed 158.99 for \(-4/\ln(0.975) = 157.99\). Plug your bound back into \(F\) and see that you get q.
  • Saying "95% probability that \(\theta\) is in \([a, b]\)". \(\theta\) is fixed; the 95% is the long-run capture rate of the method.
  • Forgetting \(y_{\rm obs}-1\) in a discrete lower bound, or using it in the upper bound or for continuous data.
  • Forgetting to divide Poisson bounds by \(n\): the equation is about \(n\lambda\).
  • Reading "\(\theta_0\) is inside the CI" as "\(\theta_0\) is the true value" or "accept \(H_0\)". The conclusion is only "fail to reject \(H_0\)".

Quick check

In \(P(\hat\theta_L \le \theta \le \hat\theta_U) = 1-\alpha\), the random quantity is \(\theta\).

False. \(\theta\) is a fixed unknown number. The bounds \(\hat\theta_L\) and \(\hat\theta_U\) are computed from random data, so they are the random part.

For a two-sided 90% interval, what q gives the lower bound?

0.95. \(\alpha = 0.10\), and the lower bound uses \(1-\alpha/2 = 0.95\). The upper bound uses \(\alpha/2 = 0.05\).

For a one-sided 95% upper bound, q = 0.025.

False. One-sided means no halving: \(q = \alpha = 0.05\).

Coverage of a 95% binomial interval is exactly 95%.

False. The binomial is discrete, so coverage depends on the true \(p\) and is generally not exactly 95%. Only an exact continuous sampling distribution gives exactly \(1-\alpha\).

A 95% CI for \(\mu\) is \([4.38, 5.62]\). Does a two-tailed test of \(H_0\!: \mu = 4\) at \(\alpha = 0.05\) reject?

Yes, reject. 4 is outside the interval. By duality, values outside a \(100(1-\alpha)\)% interval are exactly the ones a two-tailed level-\(\alpha\) test rejects.

For a Poisson CI from \(n\) years of counts, the equation's roots are already bounds on \(\lambda\).

False. \(X_+ \sim \text{Pois}(n\lambda)\), so the roots are bounds on \(n\lambda\). Divide by \(n\).

What happens to the interval width if \(n\) increases (all else equal)?

It shrinks. The standard error \(\sigma/\sqrt n\) gets smaller, so the sampling distribution is narrower and fewer \(\theta\) values stay "not surprising".

For \(F(x;\theta) = 1 - x^{-\theta}\) (HW6 Q4), the q = 0.025 root is the lower bound.

True. Bigger \(\theta\) makes the data smaller, so \(F(x_{\rm obs};\theta)\) increases with \(\theta\) and the labels swap.

In the beta example of 8M, the simulated c.d.f. is the same thing as the e.c.d.f. used in the KS test.

False. It is a simulation-based estimate of the sampling distribution's c.d.f. at a trial parameter value. The KS e.c.d.f. is built from your original data.

The confidence coefficient of a 99% interval is 0.01.

False. The confidence coefficient is \(1-\alpha = 0.99\). \(\alpha = 0.01\).

If you see this on the exam

  • "Construct a \(100(1-\alpha)\)% CI" with an algebraic c.d.f. → \(Y\) and \(y_{\rm obs}\) first, then \(F(y_{\rm obs};\theta) = 1-\alpha/2\) (lower) and \(= \alpha/2\) (upper); solve each with logs. Exponential: \(\hat\theta = -y_{\rm obs}/\ln(1-q)\).
  • "One-sided upper bound" → \(q = \alpha\). "One-sided lower bound" → \(q = 1-\alpha\).
  • Data get smaller as \(\theta\) grows → labels swap. Always report the smaller root as \(\hat\theta_L\).
  • Normal mean, \(\sigma\) known, z given → \(\overline x \pm z\,\sigma/\sqrt n\) (1.96 for 95%, 1.645 for 90%).
  • Binomial or Poisson → \(y_{\rm obs}-1\) in the lower-bound equation; for Poisson divide by \(n\).
  • "Interpret this interval" → "If we repeated the experiment many times, about \(100(1-\alpha)\)% of intervals built this way would contain \(\theta\)." Never "95% chance \(\theta\) is in this interval" (the 190% argument).
  • "Is \(\theta_0\) plausible?" or "test at level \(\alpha\) using the CI" → outside the interval: reject \(H_0\); inside: fail to reject \(H_0\).
  • "When is coverage exactly \(1-\alpha\)?" → exact, continuous sampling distribution. "When do we subtract one?" → discrete, lower bound only.

Module 6 · Lecture 6F · about 35 min

Distribution tests: Kolmogorov-Smirnov, Shapiro-Wilk and Mann-Whitney U

Every t test assumes your data are normal. These tests check assumptions like that, or compare two samples without assuming any distribution. On the exam you compute two statistics by hand (\(D_n\) and \(U\)) and you pick the right test for a scenario.

After this module you can

  • Build an empirical c.d.f. from data and draw it as a staircase.
  • Read the KS statistic \(D_n\) off a table of empirical and null c.d.f. values.
  • State the KS hypotheses and say why Shapiro-Wilk, not KS, is the test for normality.
  • Rank two samples, compute \(R_1, R_2, U_1, U_2\) and the Mann-Whitney statistic \(U\), with arithmetic checks.
  • Choose KS, Shapiro-Wilk or Mann-Whitney for a word problem (the 6F worksheet).

In plain English

A t test only works if the data are (roughly) normal. The instructor's point in 6F: choosing a distribution is itself a claim about the data, so you can test that claim with another hypothesis test.

Two kinds of question come up:

  • One sample. "Did these data come from this particular distribution?" The Kolmogorov-Smirnov (KS) test answers this for any fully specified continuous distribution. It compares the staircase built from your data with the smooth c.d.f. the null claims. If the two curves are far apart somewhere, the claim looks wrong. If the distribution in question is the normal, use the Shapiro-Wilk test, which is built for that one job and does it better.
  • Two samples. "Did these two groups come from the same distribution?" The Mann-Whitney U test answers this without assuming normality. It pools the data, ranks them, and checks whether one group's ranks are bunched at one end.

KS and Mann-Whitney are non-parametric: they assume no particular distribution for the data. The price is power. They need more data than a t test to catch the same difference.

Vocabulary

SymbolNameWhat it means
\(n\)sample sizeHow many data points you have (one-sample KS).
\(F_{X_n}(x)\), also \(\hat F_n(x)\)empirical c.d.f. (e.c.d.f.)The fraction of your data at or below \(x\). A staircase built from the data. The sheet writes \(F_{X_n}\); the lecture writes \(\hat F_n\). Same thing.
\(F_X(x)\)null c.d.f.The smooth c.d.f. of the distribution named in \(H_0\), with every parameter set to a number.
\(D_n\)KS statisticThe largest vertical gap between the staircase and the smooth null curve. Big \(D_n\) is evidence against \(H_0\).
power(from Module 4)Probability the test rejects \(H_0\) when \(H_0\) is false. "Weak test" = low power.
\(n_1, n_2\)group sizesNumber of data in group 1 and group 2 (Mann-Whitney).
rankposition in pooled dataPool both groups, sort them; the smallest gets rank 1, the largest gets rank \(n_1+n_2\).
\(R_i\)rank sumSum of the ranks belonging to group \(i\).
\(U_i\)U for group \(i\)\(n_1n_2 + n_i(n_i+1)/2 - R_i\). Counts pairs (one value from each group) in which group \(i\)'s value is the smaller one.
\(U\)Mann-Whitney statisticThe smaller of \(U_1\) and \(U_2\). Small \(U\) is evidence against \(H_0\).

The empirical c.d.f.: a staircase made from data

Recall the c.d.f. of a random variable: \(F_X(x) = P(X \le x)\), the probability of landing at or below \(x\). The empirical version replaces "probability" with "fraction of the data you actually saw":

\[ \hat F_n(x) = \frac{\text{number of data} \le x}{n} \]

Not printed on the sheet as a formula. The instructor's words: "What proportion of observed data lie at or below \(x\)?"

How to build it:

Method: build an e.c.d.f.

  1. Sort the data from smallest to largest: \(x_{(1)} \le x_{(2)} \le \dots \le x_{(n)}\).
  2. Left of the smallest datum, \(\hat F_n = 0\). No data are that small.
  3. At each datum the staircase jumps up by \(1/n\). If two data are equal, it jumps \(2/n\) there.
  4. The value at a datum already includes the jump, because "at or below \(x\)" counts that datum. So at the \(i\)-th sorted datum, \(\hat F_n = i/n\). Just before it, \(\hat F_n = (i-1)/n\).
  5. At and right of the largest datum, \(\hat F_n = 1\).

Example (HW5 Q4a): data 0.2, 0.3, 0.4, 0.9, so \(n = 4\) and each step is \(1/4\).

\[ \hat F_4(x) = \begin{cases} 0 & x < 0.2 \\ 0.25 & 0.2 \le x < 0.3 \\ 0.50 & 0.3 \le x < 0.4 \\ 0.75 & 0.4 \le x < 0.9 \\ 1 & x \ge 0.9 \end{cases} \]

Each interval is closed on the left (the jump has happened) and open on the right.

The KS test

Hypotheses (one-sample KS)

  • \(H_0\): the data are sampled from a specified distribution, for example "Uniform(0, 1)" or "Exponential with \(\theta = 2\)".
  • \(H_A\): the data are not sampled from that distribution.

"Specified" means every parameter is a number. You cannot draw the smooth curve \(F_X\) otherwise.

Test Statistics · KS test

\[ D_n = \max|F_{X_n}(x) - F_X(x)| \]

"D-n is the biggest vertical distance, over all x, between the data's staircase and the null hypothesis's smooth c.d.f."

\(D_n\)
the KS test statistic. It is a distance between two c.d.f.s, so it always lies between 0 and 1. The subscript \(n\) reminds you it was built from \(n\) data.
\(F_{X_n}(x)\)
the empirical c.d.f. of your \(n\) data: the fraction of data at or below \(x\). The lecture writes it \(\hat F_n(x)\) and calls it the "step fxn".
\(F_X(x)\)
the c.d.f. of the distribution named in \(H_0\), the "smooth fxn". For Uniform(0, 1), \(F_X(x) = x\). For Exponential(\(\theta\)), \(F_X(x) = 1 - e^{-x/\theta}\).
\(|\cdot|\)
absolute value. A gap counts whether the staircase is above or below the curve.
\(\max\)
the largest such gap over all \(x\). The sheet leaves off the "over \(x\)" subscript.

Why it works: if \(H_0\) is true, the staircase should hug the smooth curve, so every gap is small. A big gap anywhere says the data do not look like the claimed distribution. So large \(D_n\) means reject.

The null distribution: given \(H_0\), \(\sqrt{n}\,D_n\) follows the Kolmogorov distribution (lecture fact, not on the sheet). Its c.d.f. is not something you evaluate by hand. The printed notes say plainly: "We cannot carry out the KS test by hand." So on the exam you compute only the statistic \(D_n\). No critical values. If a decision is needed, you will be given the p-value, and you compare it with \(\alpha\): if \(p < \alpha\), reject \(H_0\); otherwise fail to reject \(H_0\).

Reading \(D_n\) off a table: the course's convention

The study guide says you must "specify the test statistic given a table of null hypothesis and empirical c.d.f. values". The course does this one way, shown in the HW5 Q4 official solution:

Method: \(D_n\) from a table (the exam answer)

  1. Make one row per sorted datum \(x_{(i)}\).
