  4. (c) \(0.035 < 0.05\): reject \(H_0: p_1 = p_2\). The data are evidence that the two lines have different pass rates. (The chi-square approximation gives p = 0.029, the same conclusion.)

Answer: Barnard's exact test; \(X^2 \approx 4.76\) (df 1) as the approximation; reject \(H_0\).

Check yourself · Original practice (classify the design)

For each study, name the test (G.o.F., homogeneity, independence or Barnard) and give the df where a chi-square test applies.

  1. A QA engineer pulls 200 random welds from a week's output and records each weld's shift (day, swing, night) and whether it passed X-ray inspection.
  2. A lab tests 40 specimens from each of three suppliers and grades each A, B or C.
  3. A die is rolled 120 times to check whether it is fair.
  4. 60 patients are randomly assigned to a drug and 60 to a placebo; each is recorded as improved or not improved.
  5. 500 randomly chosen students report their college (5 options) and their favorite study spot (4 options).
  6. Genetics predicts a 9:3:3:1 ratio of four plant types; 160 plants are classified.
  7. 100 chips from Fab A and 100 from Fab B are each classified as no defect, scratch, void or short.
  8. 300 randomly chosen voters say whether they own a home (yes/no) and whether they support a bond measure (yes/no).
Full solution
  1. Independence. One sample, two variables, no margin fixed. 3×2 table, df \(= 2 \times 1 = 2\).
  2. Homogeneity. Supplier sizes fixed at 40, one question. 3×3, df \(= 2 \times 2 = 4\).
  3. G.o.F. One variable, \(H_0\) gives \(p_{i,0} = 1/6\). \(k = 120\), expected 20 each, df \(= 6 - 1 = 5\).
  4. Barnard. 2×2, group sizes 60 and 60 fixed by design, outcome totals random. (Chi-square homogeneity with df 1 would be the by-hand approximation.)
  5. Independence. One sample, two questions. 5×4, df \(= 4 \times 3 = 12\).
  6. G.o.F. \(p_{i,0} = 9/16, 3/16, 3/16, 1/16\); expected 90, 30, 30, 10 (all \(\ge 5\)); df \(= 3\).
  7. Homogeneity. Fab sizes fixed at 100. It is 2×4, not 2×2, so not Barnard. df \(= 1 \times 3 = 3\).
  8. Independence. It is 2×2, but no margin is fixed by design, so the instructor's Barnard rule does not apply. Chi-square test of independence, df = 1. (The course does not discuss this case directly.)

Answer: 1 indep (2), 2 homog (4), 3 G.o.F. (5), 4 Barnard, 5 indep (12), 6 G.o.F. (3), 7 homog (3), 8 indep (1).

Check yourself · Original practice (the k trap and interpretation)

(a) A student redoing the ice-cream test (60, 35, 25 with \(H_0\): equal preference) sets \(k = 3\) "because there are 3 flavors." What expected counts do they get, and how can they tell it is wrong? (b) A chi-square G.o.F. test gives p = 0.046, and the exact multinomial test on the same data gives p = 0.058. What do you conclude at \(\alpha = 0.05\)? (c) A test of homogeneity gives p = 0.31. A classmate writes "we accept \(H_0\); the groups have the same distribution." Fix the sentence.

Full solution
  1. (a) They get \(3 \times 1/3 = 1\) per flavor. The expected counts must add to the total number of people, 120, and these add to 3. \(k\) is the total count, \(60 + 35 + 25 = 120\), so each expected count is 40. The number of categories is \(m = 3\).
  2. (b) The two p-values straddle \(\alpha\), exactly the "\(p \approx \alpha\)" case the instructor warned about. The exact test makes no approximation, so go with it: \(0.058 > 0.05\), fail to reject \(H_0\).
  3. (c) "We fail to reject \(H_0\). It is plausible that the groups share the same distribution." You never "accept" \(H_0\), and a large p-value does not show the distributions are equal.

Answer: (a) expected 1 each, wrong because they must add to 120; use \(k = 120\). (b) Fail to reject, trusting the exact test. (c) "Fail to reject \(H_0\); same distribution is plausible."

Common mistakes

  • Using the number of categories for \(k\). \(k\) is the total count. Expected counts must add up to \(k\).
  • Rounding expected counts to whole numbers. 24.086 stays 24.086.
  • Dividing by the observed count instead of the expected count. The divisor is always \(k\,p_{i,0}\) or \(E_{ij}\).
  • Forgetting to square the gap, or adding the raw gaps. Raw gaps always add to 0.
  • Wrong df: using \(m\) instead of \(m-1\), or \(r \times c\) instead of \((r-1)(c-1)\). Do not count the total row and total column as rows and columns.
  • Treating chi-square as two-tailed. Only large \(X^2\) is evidence against \(H_0\); \(p = 1 - F(X^2_{\rm obs})\).
  • Skipping the "all expected counts \(\ge 5\)" check. It is about expected counts, not observed counts.
  • Calling a test "independence" because the variables seem related, or "homogeneity" because the groups look alike. Decide from the design: were group sizes fixed in advance?
  • Sending a 2×3 table to Barnard. Barnard is 2×2 only, with exactly one margin fixed.
  • Writing "accept \(H_0\)" or "the flavors are proven equally popular."

Quick check

In \(X^2 = \sum (X_i - k\,p_{i,0})^2/(k\,p_{i,0})\), \(k\) is the number of categories. True or false?

False. \(k\) is the total count, the sum of all the \(X_i\). The number of categories is \(m\).

The chi-square G.o.F. test is an approximation to which exact test?

The exact multinomial test. Its statistic is the multinomial p.m.f. of the observed counts under \(H_0\). Its p-value cannot be computed by hand, which is why Pearson's chi-square approximation was invented.

The chi-square approximation needs every observed count to be at least 5. True or false?

False. It needs every expected count (\(k\,p_{i,0}\) or \(E_{ij}\)) to be at least 5. An observed count of 1 is fine if its expected count is 10.

What distribution, with what parameter, is \(X^2\) compared to for a 3×4 contingency table?

\(\chi^2\) with \((3-1)(4-1) = 6\) degrees of freedom. The practice-exam pointers say you may be asked exactly this: which distribution, and its parameter.

Tests of independence and homogeneity use different formulas for \(E_{ij}\). True or false?

False. Both use \(E_{ij} = r_i c_j / k\), the same \(X^2\) and the same df. Only the design and the wording of the conclusion differ.

"We surveyed 400 randomly chosen engineers and recorded their discipline and their preferred CAD package." Homogeneity or independence?

Independence. One sample, two variables per person, neither margin fixed in advance.

Barnard's exact test requires small cell counts. True or false?

False. The course's rule is structural: a 2×2 table with exactly one margin fixed by design. The lecture example had counts from 35 to 58.

Why is Fisher's exact test "not strictly appropriate" for two-group binomial data?

Fisher's test assumes both margins are fixed in advance. Sampling two groups of chosen sizes fixes only one margin. Barnard's test assumes exactly that.

Why does \(H_a\) in a multinomial test say "at least two" probabilities differ?

The \(p_i\) must add to 1. If one moves away from its null value, at least one other must move to compensate.

A G.o.F. test with \(m = 5\) categories gives \(X^2 = 3.9\). Without any p-value, is rejection at \(\alpha = 0.05\) likely?

No. The df is 4 and the mean of \(\chi^2(4)\) is 4. An \(X^2\) of 3.9 is a perfectly ordinary value under \(H_0\), so the p-value is large (about 0.42).

If you see this on the exam

  • "Counts in categories, \(H_0\) gives the probabilities" → G.o.F.: expected \(= k\,p_{i,0}\) with \(k\) = total; \(X^2 = \sum(\text{O}-\text{E})^2/\text{E}\); df \(= m - 1\).
  • "A table of counts, no probabilities given" → contingency table: \(E_{ij} = r_i c_j/k\); sum over all cells; df \(= (r-1)(c-1)\).
  • "Is it valid?" → check that every expected count is \(\ge 5\). If not, use the exact multinomial test.
  • "Why is chi-square an approximation?" → it stands in for the exact multinomial test, whose p-value cannot be done by hand. The gap matters most when \(p \approx \alpha\), and chi-square p-values get biased when \(k\) is small.
  • "Homogeneity or independence?" → group sizes chosen in advance and one question = homogeneity. One sample asked two questions, no margin fixed = independence.
  • "2×2 table, one margin fixed by design" → Barnard's exact test (software). Chi-square homogeneity with df 1 is the by-hand approximation. Not Fisher (needs both margins fixed).
  • "Here is the p-value, interpret it" → \(p \le \alpha\): reject \(H_0\) and say what that means in the story. \(p > \alpha\): fail to reject, "it is plausible that...". Never "accept".
  • "What distribution is the statistic sampled from?" → \(\chi^2\) with df \(m-1\) (G.o.F.) or \((r-1)(c-1)\) (table). p-value \(= 1 - F_{\chi^2}(X^2_{\rm obs})\), upper tail only. For df = 2, \(p = e^{-X^2/2}\).

Module 9 · Lectures 8W, 9M · about 45 min

Control charts: the X̄, S, p and c charts

A control chart is a running check on a production process: plot one number per batch, draw limits at ±3σ, and flag anything outside. The study guide marks one skill as important: given a small snippet of data, compute the control limits for X̄, S, p and c charts. This module teaches exactly that, plus every word of the jargon.

After this module you can

  • Define statistical quality control, control chart, center line, UCL, LCL, in control, out of control, common and special causes, variables, attributes, rational subgroups, false alarm and failure to detect.
  • Compute \(c_4\) by hand for any subgroup size from 2 to 7 using the Gamma values on the sheet.
  • Read every Quality Control line on the sheet for the X̄, S, p and c charts and say what each symbol is.
  • Pick the right chart for a given kind of data.
  • Compute control limits by hand from a few numbers and decide if any point is out of control.

In plain English

A machine makes parts. No two parts are identical: tiny random differences in material, temperature and vibration always exist. That background wobble is fine. Sometimes something real goes wrong: a tool wears out, a new batch of raw material is bad, an operator changes a setting. You want to catch those events quickly and leave the machine alone otherwise.

So every so often you grab a small batch of parts, measure them, and boil the batch down to one number (its mean, its standard deviation, its fraction defective, or its count of flaws). You plot that number against time. If the process is behaving, the number should land inside a band of ±3 standard deviations around its usual value 99.73% of the time. A point outside the band is strong evidence that something changed.

The instructor's own summary: QC is taught right after inference "because it is effectively confidence interval construction." Each chart is a 99.73% interval for where the plotted number should fall. Each new point is a small hypothesis test of "the process is still in control."

The vocabulary

The study guide says to know the jargon. These are the definitions to write on the exam.

TermExam-ready definition
Statistical quality control (QC)The monitoring of a production process to determine whether there are sources of variability (special causes) beyond the expected natural variability (common causes) that need to be identified, investigated and mitigated. (The instructor adds: "or occasionally, replicated", when the special cause makes things better.)
Common causesThe natural, always-present random variation of a process. You cannot remove them without redesigning the process.
Special causesExtra variation from an identifiable event: tool wear, a bad material batch, a setting change. These are what QC hunts for.
Control chartA plot of a statistic computed from each subgroup (X̄, S, a proportion, or a count) against subgroup number, with a center line and lower and upper control limits.
Center line (CL)The estimated typical value of the plotted statistic: \(\overline{\overline X}\) on the X̄ chart, \(\overline S\) on the S chart, \(\overline p\) on the p chart, \(\overline c\) on the c chart.
Control limits (LCL, UCL)The lower and upper limits, 3 standard deviations of the plotted statistic below and above the center line. Computed from the process's own data.
In controlOnly common-cause variation is present: every point is inside the limits and no Western Electric pattern appears (Module 10).
Out of controlThere is evidence of a special cause: a point outside the limits (or a Western Electric pattern).
VariablesContinuously valued data, such as a weight or a length. Charted with the X̄ chart and the S chart.
AttributesDiscrete data, usually categorical, such as "defective" vs "not defective", or a count of defects. Charted with the p chart (proportions) and the c chart (counts).
Rational subgroupA small batch of items sampled together, which gives one point on the chart. Two strategies: sample items made close to the sampling time, or sample at regular intervals at random from everything made since the last sample. For variables the subgroup size is about \(n = 1\) to 10.
False alarmDeciding an in-control process is out of control. The QC version of a Type I error (a false positive). Probability \(\alpha\), which is 0.0027 for 3σ limits.
Failure to detectNot detecting that an out-of-control process is out of control. The QC version of a Type II error (a false negative). Probability \(1 - \text{power}\). A less powerful chart fails to detect more often.

Why the limits sit at 3σ

The goal of an X̄ chart, as written in lecture 8W: find \(a\) and \(b\) so that the subgroup mean lands between them with probability \(1-\alpha\) when the process is in control.

\[ P(a \le \overline X_i \le b) = 1 - \alpha, \qquad \alpha = 0.0027 \]

\(i\) is the rational subgroup index. If \(\overline X_i\) is normal, the interval mean ± 3 SD holds 99.73% of the probability: each tail beyond 3 SD holds 0.00135, and \(2 \times 0.00135 = 0.0027\). So \(a\) = LCL and \(b\) = UCL are the mean ± 3 SD. That is the whole "3σ" idea, and it is why every chart on the sheet has a "± 3 something".

False alarm and failure to detect side by side

Truth about the processChart says in controlChart says out of control
In controlCorrectFalse alarm (Type I, probability \(\alpha = 0.0027\))
Out of controlFailure to detect (Type II, probability \(\beta = 1 - \text{power}\))Correct detection (probability = power)

Compare with Module 3: "in control" plays the role of \(H_0\). A false alarm costs you a pointless investigation. A failure to detect lets bad parts keep coming. How often each happens is the topic of the ARL section in Module 10.

What you do when a point is out

  • X̄ chart: take the most out-of-control point, find its special cause, delete that point and recompute the limits. Repeat until every point is inside. Then fix the special causes and collect new data to confirm the fix worked.
  • S chart, point above the UCL: handle it exactly the same way.
  • S chart, point below the LCL: that subgroup had unusually small variation, which is good. Find the cause and possibly mimic it.

Decoding the Quality Control block: \(c_4\) and the Gamma function

Every variables chart needs \(\sigma\), the standard deviation of individual items. You estimate it from the subgroup standard deviations \(S_i\). The trouble: the sample standard deviation \(S\) is biased low. On average it comes out a bit smaller than \(\sigma\), and the effect is bigger for small subgroups. The constant \(c_4\) is the size of that bias: \(E(S) = c_4\sigma\). So you divide by \(c_4\) to undo it.

Quality Control · line 1

\[ c_4 = \Gamma(n/2)\sqrt{2/(n-1)}\,/\,\Gamma((n-1)/2) \]

"c-four is gamma of n over 2, times root of 2 over n minus 1, divided by gamma of n minus 1 over 2."

\(c_4\)
the de-biasing constant for \(S\). \(E(S) = c_4\sigma\), so \(\overline S / c_4\) is an unbiased estimate of \(\sigma\). The instructor calls it "one of many symbols unique to quality control". It is always less than 1 and climbs toward 1 as \(n\) grows.
\(n\)
the subgroup size: the number of items in ONE rational subgroup. It is not the number of subgroups.
\(\Gamma(\cdot)\)
the Gamma function, a smooth version of the factorial. You only ever need it at whole numbers and halves, and the next sheet line gives you those.

Quality Control · lines 2 and 3

\[ \begin{aligned} &\text{If } n \text{ integer, } \Gamma(n) = (n-1)! \\ &\Gamma(1/2) = \sqrt\pi \quad \Gamma(3/2) = \sqrt\pi/2 \quad \Gamma(5/2) = 3\sqrt\pi/4 \end{aligned} \]

"Gamma of a whole number is the factorial of one less. Here are the three half-integer values you need."

\(\Gamma(n) = (n-1)!\)
so \(\Gamma(1) = 0! = 1\), \(\Gamma(2) = 1! = 1\), \(\Gamma(3) = 2! = 2\), \(\Gamma(4) = 3! = 6\).
\(\sqrt\pi\)
\(\sqrt\pi \approx 1.7725\). So \(\Gamma(3/2) \approx 0.8862\) and \(\Gamma(5/2) \approx 1.3293\).
\[ \Gamma(x+1) = x\,\Gamma(x) \;\Rightarrow\; \Gamma(7/2) = \tfrac52\,\Gamma(5/2) = \tfrac{15\sqrt\pi}{8} \approx 3.3234 \]

Not on the sheet, but it follows from it. You need \(\Gamma(7/2)\) only for \(n = 7\) (where \((n-1)/2 = 3\) and \(n/2 = 7/2\)). The same rule reproduces the sheet's values: \(\Gamma(5/2) = \tfrac32\Gamma(3/2) = \tfrac32 \cdot \tfrac{\sqrt\pi}{2} = \tfrac{3\sqrt\pi}{4}\).

\(c_4\) by hand for \(n = 2\) to 7

For even \(n\), the top Gamma is at a whole number and the bottom one at a half. For odd \(n\) it is the other way round. Either way, one Gamma is a factorial and the other comes from the sheet.

\(n\)Plug inSimplify\(c_4\)
2\(\Gamma(1)\sqrt{2/1}\,/\,\Gamma(1/2)\)\(\sqrt2/\sqrt\pi = 1.4142/1.7725\)0.7979
3\(\Gamma(3/2)\sqrt{2/2}\,/\,\Gamma(1)\)\((\sqrt\pi/2)\cdot 1/1\)0.8862
4\(\Gamma(2)\sqrt{2/3}\,/\,\Gamma(3/2)\)\(1 \cdot 0.8165/0.8862\)0.9213
5\(\Gamma(5/2)\sqrt{2/4}\,/\,\Gamma(2)\)\(1.3293 \cdot 0.7071/1\)0.9400
6\(\Gamma(3)\sqrt{2/5}\,/\,\Gamma(5/2)\)\(2 \cdot 0.6325/1.3293\)0.9515
7\(\Gamma(7/2)\sqrt{2/6}\,/\,\Gamma(3)\)\(3.3234 \cdot 0.5774/2\)0.9594

All the constants in one table

\(B_3\) and \(B_4\) are for the S chart (decoded below). \(A_3 = 3/(c_4\sqrt n)\) is a shortcut the lecture uses for the X̄ chart; it is not on the sheet, so you can always rebuild it.

\(n\)\(c_4\)\(\sqrt{1-c_4^2}\)\(B_3\)\(B_4\)\(A_3 = 3/(c_4\sqrt n)\)
20.79790.602803.2672.659
30.88620.463302.5681.954
40.92130.388802.2661.628
50.94000.341202.0891.427
60.95150.30750.0301.9701.287
70.95940.28220.1181.8821.182

These match the standard QC constants table (the instructor points to "the A.10 table in Navidi"). On the exam you will usually get \(n = 3\) to 6, so expect to compute one of these rows yourself.

The X̄ chart (variables: is the process mean stable?)

Quality Control · X̄-chart

\[ \overline X\text{-chart: } (\mathrm{LCL},\mathrm{UCL}) = \hat\mu \pm 3\hat\sigma = \overline{\overline X} \pm 3\overline S/(c_4\sqrt n) \]

"The control limits are the grand mean plus or minus three times S-bar over c-four root n."

\(\overline X_i\)
the mean of subgroup \(i\). This is the dot you plot.
\(\overline{\overline X}\)
"X double bar", the grand mean: the average of the subgroup means, the "average of averages". It estimates the process mean, so \(\hat\mu = \overline{\overline X}\). It is the center line.
\(S_i\), \(\overline S\)
\(S_i\) is the sample standard deviation of subgroup \(i\) (divide by \(n-1\)). \(\overline S\) is the average of the \(S_i\) over all subgroups.
\(\overline S/c_4\)
the unbiased estimate of \(\sigma\), the SD of one individual item.
\(\hat\sigma = \overline S/(c_4\sqrt n)\)
on this line \(\hat\sigma\) is the SD of a subgroup MEAN, not of one item. You divide by \(\sqrt n\) because \(V(\overline X) = \sigma^2/n\) (Statistics block of the sheet), so \(\mathrm{SD}(\overline X) = \sigma/\sqrt n\).
\(n\)
items per subgroup.
3
three standard deviations, which gives \(\alpha = 0.0027\).

The S chart (variables: is the process spread stable?)

Quality Control · S-chart and the B₃, B₄ line

\[ \begin{aligned} &S\text{-chart: } (\mathrm{LCL},\mathrm{UCL}) = (B_3\overline S,\ B_4\overline S) \\ &B_3 = \max\!\Big(0,\ 1 - (3/c_4)\sqrt{1-c_4^2}\Big) \\ &B_4 = 1 + (3/c_4)\sqrt{1-c_4^2} \end{aligned} \]

"The S chart limits are B-three times S-bar and B-four times S-bar. B-three is one minus a bit, floored at zero; B-four is one plus the same bit."

\(S_i\)
the plotted statistic: each subgroup's standard deviation.
\(\overline S\)
the center line: the average of the \(S_i\).
\((3/c_4)\sqrt{1-c_4^2}\)
the "3 standard deviations of \(S\)" part, written as a fraction of \(\overline S\). It comes from \(V(S) = \sigma^2(1 - c_4^2)\). Compute it once; \(B_3\) subtracts it from 1 and \(B_4\) adds it to 1.
\(\max(0,\cdot)\)
a standard deviation can never be negative, so a negative lower limit is replaced by 0. For \(n \le 5\), \(B_3 = 0\).

The p chart (attributes: proportion defective)

Quality Control · p-chart

\[ p\text{-chart: } (\mathrm{LCL},\mathrm{UCL}) = \overline p \pm 3\sqrt{\overline p(1-\overline p)/k} \]

"p-bar plus or minus three times root of p-bar times one minus p-bar, over k."

Setup
You take \(m\) samples. In each you inspect \(k\) items and count the defectives \(X_i\). If the process is in control, \(X_i \sim \mathrm{Binom}(k, p)\). You plot the sample proportion \(X_i/k\).
\(k\)
the number of items inspected in ONE sample (the subgroup size, typically 100 to 1000). It is the binomial "number of trials".
\(m\)
the number of samples (not on the sheet line, but you need it to get \(\overline p\)).
\(\overline p\)
the center line: total defectives over total items inspected, \(\overline p = \frac{1}{mk}\sum_{i=1}^m X_i\). Equivalently, the average of the \(m\) sample proportions.
\(\sqrt{\overline p(1-\overline p)/k}\)
the SD of one sample proportion. From the binomial line of the sheet, \(V(X) = kp(1-p)\), so \(V(X/k) = p(1-p)/k\).

Heads up: the letter k on your sheet

The sheet uses \(k\) for three different things. In Binom(\(k, p\)) and on the p chart line it is the number of trials, which on the p chart is the subgroup size. In the chi-square lines it is the total count. In the CUSUM lines, \(k\sigma\) is a slack constant, \(k = 1/2\) (Module 10). Read the line you are on before you plug anything in.

Why the normal "± 3" works for counts: the instructor calls this "conventional QC", which leans on large-sample normal approximations because it was designed before modern computing. Exact binomial or Poisson limits are possible but not what this course uses.

The c chart (attributes: count of flaws)

Quality Control · c-chart

\[ c\text{-chart: } (\mathrm{LCL},\mathrm{UCL}) = \overline c \pm 3\sqrt{\overline c} \]

"c-bar plus or minus three root c-bar."

Setup
You inspect \(m\) units (a unit can be one item or a fixed group of items) and count the flaws on each, \(X_i\). In control, \(X_i \sim \mathrm{Pois}(c)\). You plot the counts.
\(\overline c\)
the center line: total flaws divided by the number of units, \(\overline c = \frac1m \sum X_i\).
\(\sqrt{\overline c}\)
the SD of one count. For a Poisson, \(E(X) = V(X) = \lambda\) (sheet, Poisson line), so the SD is the square root of the mean.

Heads up: negative lower limits on p and c charts

The S chart formula floors its LCL at 0 explicitly. The p and c chart lines do not, and both lecture examples happened to give positive LCLs. If your p or c chart LCL comes out negative, write the computed value and then use 0 as the limit, because a proportion or a count can never be negative. This floor is standard practice; it was not stated in lecture.

Which chart for which data

ChartData typePlot each subgroup'sCenter lineLimits
X̄variablesmean \(\overline X_i\)\(\overline{\overline X}\)\(\overline{\overline X} \pm 3\overline S/(c_4\sqrt n)\)
SvariablesSD \(S_i\)\(\overline S\)\((B_3\overline S,\ B_4\overline S)\)
pattributes (defective or not)proportion \(X_i/k\)\(\overline p\)\(\overline p \pm 3\sqrt{\overline p(1-\overline p)/k}\)
cattributes (count of flaws)count \(X_i\)\(\overline c\)\(\overline c \pm 3\sqrt{\overline c}\)

Method: control limits from a small data snippet

  1. Classify the data. Measurements → X̄ and S charts. Defective counts out of \(k\) → p chart. Flaw counts per unit → c chart.
  2. Find the sizes. Write down \(n\) (items per subgroup) for variables, or \(k\) (items per sample) and \(m\) (number of samples) for attributes.
  3. Compute the center line. \(\overline{\overline X}\) and \(\overline S\) are plain averages over subgroups. \(\overline p\) = total defectives / \((mk)\). \(\overline c\) = total flaws / \(m\). If you are given sums, divide by the number of subgroups.
  4. Variables only: get \(c_4\) from the sheet's Gamma values (or the table above), then \(B_3\), \(B_4\).
  5. Compute the half-width (the "3 SD" part) once, then subtract and add.
  6. Floor any negative lower limit at 0.
  7. Compare each plotted point with the limits. Any point outside → out of control at that subgroup. All inside → in control by the limits (then check the Western Electric rules, Module 10).

Worked examples

Worked example · Lecture 8W example (X̄ and S charts)

Three rational subgroups of size \(n = 3\) were measured:

SubgroupObs 1Obs 2Obs 3
12.532.661.88
23.142.843.77
32.562.261.95

Construct (a) an X̄ chart and (b) an S chart. Is the process in control? (Real use needs many more than 3 subgroups; this is for illustration.)

  1. Identify. Continuous measurements, so variables: X̄ and S charts. \(n = 3\) items per subgroup.
  2. Subgroup statistics. Subgroup 1: \(\overline X_1 = (2.53 + 2.66 + 1.88)/3 = 2.357\). Deviations 0.173, 0.303, −0.477; squares 0.0300, 0.0920, 0.2272, sum 0.3493. Divide by \(n - 1 = 2\): 0.1746. Square root: \(S_1 = 0.418\). The same steps give \(\overline X_2 = 3.250\), \(S_2 = 0.475\), \(\overline X_3 = 2.257\), \(S_3 = 0.305\).
  3. Center lines. \(\overline{\overline X} = (2.357 + 3.250 + 2.257)/3 = 2.621\). \(\overline S = (0.418 + 0.475 + 0.305)/3 = 0.399\).
  4. \(c_4\) for \(n = 3\). \(\Gamma(3/2)\sqrt{2/2}/\Gamma(1) = (\sqrt\pi/2)/1 = 0.8862\).
  5. (a) X̄ chart half-width. \(3\overline S/(c_4\sqrt n) = 3(0.399)/(0.8862 \times 1.7321) = 1.198/1.535 = 0.780\). (Same as \(A_3\overline S = 1.954 \times 0.399\).)
  6. (a) Limits. LCL \(= 2.621 - 0.780 = 1.841\). UCL \(= 2.621 + 0.780 = 3.402\). All three means (2.357, 3.250, 2.257) are inside (1.841, 3.402).
  7. (b) S chart constants. \(\sqrt{1 - 0.8862^2} = \sqrt{0.2146} = 0.4633\). \((3/0.8862)(0.4633) = 1.568\). \(B_3 = \max(0, 1 - 1.568) = \max(0, -0.568) = 0\). \(B_4 = 2.568\).
  8. (b) Limits. LCL \(= 0 \times 0.399 = 0\). UCL \(= 2.568 \times 0.399 = 1.026\). All three SDs (0.418, 0.475, 0.305) are inside (0, 1.026).
  9. Conclude. Both charts show the process in control.

Answer: X̄ chart (LCL, UCL) = (1.841, 3.402); S chart (0, 1.026). In control on both.

Worked example · 8W worksheet

Data snippet with \(n = 3\): subgroup 1 is 8, 9, 10 (\(\overline X = 9\), \(S = 1\)); subgroup 2 is 9, 10, 11 (\(\overline X = 10\), \(S = 1\)); subgroup 3 is 6, 8, 10 (\(\overline X = 8\), \(S = 2\)). You are told \(c_4 = 0.886\) and \(A_3 = 3/(\sqrt n\, c_4) = 1.954\). What are the lower and upper control limits for an X̄ chart?

  1. Check one S (good habit). Subgroup 3: mean 8, deviations −2, 0, 2, squares sum to 8, divide by 2 gives 4, root gives 2. Matches.
  2. Grand mean. \(\overline{\overline X} = (9 + 10 + 8)/3 = 9\).
  3. Average SD. \(\overline S = (1 + 1 + 2)/3 = 4/3 = 1.333\).
  4. Half-width. \(3\overline S/(c_4\sqrt n) = 3(1.333)/(0.8862 \times 1.7321) = 4/1.535 = 2.606\). (With the given \(A_3\): \(1.954 \times 1.333 = 2.605\). Same to rounding.)
  5. Limits. LCL \(= 9 - 2.606 = 6.394\). UCL \(= 9 + 2.606 = 11.606\).
  6. Not asked, but check. The means 9, 10, 8 are all inside. S chart bonus: \(B_4 = 2.568\), so (0, \(2.568 \times 1.333\)) = (0, 3.42); SDs 1, 1, 2 are inside.

Answer: (LCL, UCL) ≈ (6.39, 11.61).

Worked example · HW7 Q1 (limits from sums)

A quality-control plan uses rational subgroups of size \(n = 4\). For the last 30 subgroups, \(\sum_{i=1}^{30}\overline X_i = 712.5\) and \(\sum_{i=1}^{30} S_i = 62.5\). (a) Compute 3σ control limits for the S chart. (b) Compute 3σ control limits for the X̄ chart.

  1. Identify. \(n = 4\) items per subgroup, \(m = 30\) subgroups. Do not confuse them: \(n\) goes in \(c_4\) and \(\sqrt n\); 30 only divides the sums.
  2. Center lines. \(\overline{\overline X} = 712.5/30 = 23.75\). \(\overline S = 62.5/30 = 2.0833\).
  3. \(c_4\) for \(n = 4\). \(\Gamma(2)\sqrt{2/3}/\Gamma(3/2) = 1 \times 0.8165/0.8862 = 0.9213\).
  4. (a) S chart. \(\sqrt{1 - 0.9213^2} = \sqrt{0.1512} = 0.3888\). \((3/0.9213)(0.3888) = 1.266\). \(B_3 = \max(0, 1 - 1.266) = 0\), \(B_4 = 2.266\). LCL \(= 0\), UCL \(= 2.266 \times 2.0833 = 4.721\).
  5. (b) X̄ chart. Half-width \(= 3(2.0833)/(0.9213 \times 2) = 6.25/1.8426 = 3.392\). LCL \(= 23.75 - 3.392 = 20.358\). UCL \(= 23.75 + 3.392 = 27.142\).

Answer: S chart (0, 4.72); X̄ chart (20.36, 27.14).

Worked example · Lecture 9M p chart (silicon wafers)

In each of \(m = 30\) samples, \(k = 500\) silicon wafers are examined. In total, 763 defective wafers are found. Find the LCL and UCL for the p chart.

  1. Identify. Each wafer is defective or not: attributes, proportions, so a p chart. \(k = 500\) per sample.
  2. Center line. \(\overline p = 763/(30 \times 500) = 763/15000 = 0.0509\).
  3. SD of one sample proportion. \(\overline p(1 - \overline p) = 0.0509 \times 0.9491 = 0.04828\). Divide by \(k\): \(0.04828/500 = 9.66\times10^{-5}\). Square root: 0.00983.
  4. Half-width. \(3 \times 0.00983 = 0.0295\).
  5. Limits. LCL \(= 0.0509 - 0.0295 = 0.0214\). UCL \(= 0.0509 + 0.0295 = 0.0803\). LCL is positive, so no floor is needed.

Answer: (LCL, UCL) ≈ (0.021, 0.080). The lecture, rounding \(\overline p\) to 0.051 first, writes the same 0.021 and 0.080.

Worked example · Lecture 9M c chart (flaws per unit)

In each of \(m = 40\) samples, one unit is examined and its flaws counted. In total, 491 flaws are found. Find the LCL and UCL for the c chart.

  1. Identify. Counts of flaws per unit: attributes, counts, so a c chart.
  2. Center line. \(\overline c = 491/40 = 12.275\).
  3. Half-width. \(\sqrt{12.275} = 3.5036\); times 3 gives 10.511.
  4. Limits. LCL \(= 12.275 - 10.511 = 1.764\). UCL \(= 12.275 + 10.511 = 22.786\).

Answer: (LCL, UCL) ≈ (1.764, 22.786).

Heads up: slip in the lecture notes

In the handwritten 9M solution the denominator looks like "491/46". It must be 491/40: the problem says \(m = 40\), and only 491/40 gives the 12.275 the notes then use (491/46 would be 10.67). Use \(\overline c = 12.275\).

Check yourself

Check yourself · HW7 Q2 (a) and (b)

20 rational subgroups of size \(n = 5\) gave these subgroup means, in order:

2.49, 2.45, 2.51, 2.53, 2.49, 2.44, 2.44, 2.42, 2.42, 2.47, 2.54, 2.45, 2.54, 2.55, 2.50, 2.53, 2.58, 2.59, 2.60, 2.56.

The overall means are \(\overline{\overline X} = 2.505\) and \(\overline s = 0.057\). (a) Compute the 3σ control limits for the X̄ chart. (b) Based on this chart alone, is the process in control? If not, at what point would you first deem it out of control? (Part (c), the Western Electric rules, is in Module 10.)

Hint

\(n = 5\), so \(c_4 = \Gamma(5/2)\sqrt{2/4}/\Gamma(2)\). Then compare every mean with the limits, in time order.

Full solution
  1. \(c_4\). \(\Gamma(5/2)\sqrt{1/2}/\Gamma(2) = 1.3293 \times 0.7071/1 = 0.9400\).
  2. \(\hat\sigma\) for a mean. \(\overline S/(c_4\sqrt n) = 0.057/(0.9400 \times 2.2361) = 0.057/2.1019 = 0.02712\).
  3. Limits. Half-width \(3 \times 0.02712 = 0.0814\). LCL \(= 2.505 - 0.0814 = 2.4236\). UCL \(= 2.505 + 0.0814 = 2.5864\).
  4. Scan in order. Samples 1 to 7 are inside. Sample 8 is 2.42 < 2.4236. That is the first point outside. (Samples 9, 18 and 19 are also outside: 2.42, 2.59, 2.60.)

Answer: (LCL, UCL) ≈ (2.424, 2.586). Out of control, first at sample 8.

Check yourself · HW7 Q4

Each day a sample of 200 items is inspected and the proportion defective \(p_i\) is recorded. For the last 30 samples, \(\sum p_i = 1.64\). (a) Calculate the 3σ limits for the p chart. (b) Pretend the data are Poisson counts instead, with total \(200 \times 1.64 = 328\). Calculate the 3σ limits for the c chart.

Hint

The center line of a p chart is the average proportion. Which number is \(k\)?

Full solution
  1. (a) Center line. \(\overline p = 1.64/30 = 0.05467\). (Same as \(328/(30 \times 200)\).) \(k = 200\).
  2. (a) Half-width. \(0.05467 \times 0.94533 = 0.05168\); divide by 200: 0.0002584; root: 0.01607; times 3: 0.0482.
  3. (a) Limits. \(0.05467 - 0.0482 = 0.0064\) and \(0.05467 + 0.0482 = 0.1029\).
  4. (b) Center line. \(\overline c = 328/30 = 10.933\).
  5. (b) Limits. \(3\sqrt{10.9333} = 3 \times 3.3066 = 9.9197\). LCL \(= 10.9333 - 9.9197 = 1.0136\), UCL \(= 10.9333 + 9.9197 = 20.8530\).

Answer: p chart (0.0064, 0.1029); c chart (1.01, 20.85).

Check yourself · Original practice (both variables charts)

Four subgroups of size \(n = 4\) have means 10.2, 9.8, 10.0, 10.4 and standard deviations 0.5, 0.7, 0.6, 0.6. Find the X̄ chart and S chart limits. Is the process in control?

Full solution
  1. Center lines. \(\overline{\overline X} = 40.4/4 = 10.1\). \(\overline S = 2.4/4 = 0.6\).
  2. Constants for \(n = 4\). \(c_4 = 0.9213\), \(B_3 = 0\), \(B_4 = 2.266\).
  3. X̄ chart. Half-width \(3(0.6)/(0.9213 \times 2) = 1.8/1.8426 = 0.977\). Limits (9.123, 11.077).
  4. S chart. (0, \(2.266 \times 0.6\)) = (0, 1.360).
  5. Compare. Means 9.8 to 10.4 are inside; SDs 0.5 to 0.7 are inside.

Answer: X̄ chart (9.12, 11.08); S chart (0, 1.36). In control.

Check yourself · Original practice (negative LCL)

25 samples of \(k = 200\) items each contain 150 defectives in total. Find the p chart limits.

Full solution
  1. Center line. \(\overline p = 150/(25 \times 200) = 150/5000 = 0.03\).
  2. Half-width. \(3\sqrt{0.03 \times 0.97/200} = 3\sqrt{0.0001455} = 3 \times 0.01206 = 0.0362\).
  3. Limits. \(0.03 - 0.0362 = -0.0062\), which is impossible for a proportion, so LCL = 0. UCL \(= 0.0662\).

Answer: (0, 0.066). With LCL = 0, only the upper limit can ever flag a point.

Check yourself · Original practice (conceptual)

(a) A plant takes 40 subgroups of 5 parts each. Which number goes inside \(\sqrt n\) and \(c_4\): 5, 40 or 200? (b) A point on the S chart falls below the LCL. Is that bad news? (c) Compute \(c_4\) and \(B_4\) for \(n = 5\) from the sheet alone.

Full solution
  1. (a) 5. \(n\) is the subgroup size. 40 is the number of subgroups; it only appears when you average to get \(\overline{\overline X}\) and \(\overline S\).
  2. (b) No. It means that subgroup had unusually small spread. It still signals something different happened, so investigate, and if you find the cause, possibly mimic it.
  3. (c) \(c_4 = \Gamma(5/2)\sqrt{2/4}/\Gamma(2) = (3\sqrt\pi/4)(0.7071)/1! = 1.3293 \times 0.7071 = 0.9400\). \(\sqrt{1 - 0.9400^2} = \sqrt{0.1164} = 0.3412\). \(B_4 = 1 + (3/0.94)(0.3412) = 1 + 1.089 = 2.089\).

Answer: (a) 5; (b) no, unusually low variation; (c) \(c_4 = 0.940\), \(B_4 = 2.089\).

Common mistakes

  • Using the number of subgroups for \(n\). \(n\) is items per subgroup.
  • Forgetting \(\sqrt n\) on the X̄ chart. The X̄ chart watches means, and means vary less than items.
  • Forgetting \(c_4\), or multiplying by it instead of dividing. \(\overline S\) is too small, so \(\overline S/c_4\) is bigger.
  • Putting the number of samples \(m\) in the p chart's \(\sqrt{\cdot/k}\). The denominator is the items per sample.
  • Using \(\sqrt{\overline c}/\text{something}\) on a c chart. The SD of a Poisson count is just \(\sqrt{\overline c}\).
  • Leaving a negative LCL. S charts floor it with \(\max(0,\cdot)\); for p and c charts use 0 as well.
  • Getting \(\Gamma\) wrong: \(\Gamma(n) = (n-1)!\), not \(n!\). \(\Gamma(3) = 2\), not 6.
  • Calling a point outside the limits "proof" of a problem. It is evidence of a special cause; it can be a false alarm (probability 0.0027 each time).

Quick check

The control limits of an X̄ chart are set by the customer's specifications.

False. Control limits come from the process's own data (\(\overline{\overline X}\), \(\overline S\)). Specification limits come from engineering requirements; they belong to process capability (Module 10).

What is the probability of a false alarm on one point of a 3σ chart that is in control?

0.0027. Each tail beyond 3 SD of a normal has 0.00135, and there are two tails.

A false alarm is the QC version of which error?

Type I. You "reject in control" when the process really is in control. Failure to detect is the Type II analogue.

What does \(c_4\) correct for?

The bias of S. \(E(S) = c_4\sigma\) with \(c_4 < 1\), so \(S\) underestimates \(\sigma\) on average. \(\overline S/c_4\) is unbiased.

For \(n = 4\), \(B_3 = 0\). Why?

The floor. \(1 - (3/c_4)\sqrt{1 - c_4^2} = 1 - 1.266 = -0.266\), and a standard deviation limit cannot be negative, so \(\max(0, -0.266) = 0\). This happens for every \(n \le 5\).

You count scratches on each car door. p chart or c chart?

c chart. You get a count of flaws per unit (Poisson). A p chart is for the fraction of items that are defective.

Weights of bags of flour: which charts?

X̄ and S charts. Weight is continuous, so these are variables data.

What is a rational subgroup?

A small batch sampled together (items made close to the sampling time, or drawn at random from everything made since the last sample). It produces one plotted point.

On the X̄ chart line, \(\hat\sigma\) is the SD of one item.

False. There \(\hat\sigma = \overline S/(c_4\sqrt n)\), the SD of a subgroup mean. The SD of one item is \(\overline S/c_4\).

If you see this on the exam

  • "Compute \(c_4\)" → plug \(n\) into the sheet's \(c_4\) line; whole-number Gammas are factorials of one less; half-integers come from the sheet (\(\Gamma(7/2) = \tfrac52\Gamma(5/2)\)).
  • "Control limits for the X̄ chart" → \(\overline{\overline X} \pm 3\overline S/(c_4\sqrt n)\). Averages first, then one half-width.
  • "Control limits for the S chart" → \((B_3\overline S, B_4\overline S)\); \(B_3 = 0\) for \(n \le 5\).
  • "Proportion defective, k items per sample" → p chart, \(\overline p \pm 3\sqrt{\overline p(1-\overline p)/k}\), \(\overline p\) = total defective / total inspected.
  • "Number of flaws per unit" → c chart, \(\overline c \pm 3\sqrt{\overline c}\), \(\overline c\) = total flaws / number of units.
  • "Given sums over m subgroups" → divide by \(m\) to get the center lines; \(m\) never enters \(c_4\) or \(\sqrt n\).
  • "Is the process in control? When first out?" → compare each point with the limits in time order; the first point outside is the answer by the limits. Then also check the Western Electric rules (Module 10).
  • "Define false alarm / failure to detect" → Type I (call an in-control process out of control) / Type II (miss a real problem).

Module 10 · Lectures 8F, 9M · about 35 min

Western Electric rules, ARL, CUSUM and process capability

A plain 3σ chart is slow to notice small shifts. The Western Electric rules and the CUSUM chart catch them sooner, and the exam asks you to say exactly WHEN a process went out of control. Then a separate question: even when the process is stable, are its parts good enough for the customer? That is process capability, \(C_p\) and \(C_{pk}\).

After this module you can

  • State the four Western Electric rules and apply them to a chart, naming the first sample at which the process is out of control.
  • Explain the average run length (ARL), and show where 370.4 (in control) and about 44 (after a 1σ shift) come from.
  • Decode the CUSUM lines (SH, SL, \(k\), \(h\)), fill in a CUSUM table by hand, and spot the sheet's sign typo.
  • Tell specification limits from control limits, compute \(C_p\) and \(C_{pk}\), and decide "minimally capable" and "six-sigma quality".

In plain English

Suppose the process mean creeps up by one standard deviation of X̄. Each point is now a bit high, but rarely above the 3σ line, so the plain chart can take dozens of subgroups to react. Two fixes look at patterns across several points instead of one point at a time. The Western Electric rules say "too many points in a row are high." The CUSUM keeps a running total of how far points sit above (or below) the center, so small, persistent deviations add up until they cross a threshold.

Process capability is a different question. A process can be perfectly stable and still make parts that are out of spec, because it is stable around the wrong value or with too much spread. Control charts ask "is the process behaving like itself?" Capability asks "is itself good enough?"

The Western Electric rules

First you need zone lines. On any chart, \(\sigma\) means the standard deviation of the plotted statistic. For the X̄ chart that is \(\hat\sigma = \overline S/(c_4\sqrt n)\) from Module 9. Draw lines at the center line plus and minus 1σ, 2σ and 3σ. The 3σ lines are the usual control limits. If a chart only shows CL and UCL, get σ from \(\sigma = (\mathrm{UCL} - \mathrm{CL})/3\).

Quality Control · The Western Electric Rules box

\[ \text{zone lines: } \mathrm{CL} \pm 1\sigma,\ \mathrm{CL} \pm 2\sigma,\ \mathrm{CL} \pm 3\sigma \]

"Any one of these four patterns is evidence that the process is out of control."

Rule 1
"Any point plotting outside the 3σ control limits." One point beyond UCL or LCL. This is the plain control chart test.
Rule 2
"Two out of three consecutive points plotting above the upper 2σ limit, or two out of three consecutive points plotting below the lower 2σ limit." Look at the last 3 points; if at least 2 of them are beyond 2σ on the SAME side, it fires.
Rule 3
"Four out of five consecutive points plotting above the upper 1σ limit, or four out of five below the lower 1σ limit." Look at the last 5 points; at least 4 beyond 1σ on the same side.
Rule 4
"Eight consecutive points plotting on the same side of the center line." The last 8 points are all above, or all below, the CL.
\(\sigma\)
the SD of the plotted statistic (for an X̄ chart, the SD of a subgroup mean), the same σ that put the control limits at 3σ.

Each rule describes something unlikely for an in-control process. For example, a single in-control point is beyond +2σ with probability about 0.023, so two of three beyond +2σ is rare. Using all four rules makes the chart react much faster to small shifts, at the price of a few more false alarms.

Saying WHEN the process went out of control

The instructor's rule, written on the lecture example: report the "first point to complete any such sequence." Not the first point that looks odd, and not the most extreme point.

Method: first out-of-control sample by the Western Electric rules

  1. Get the zone lines. CL and σ (given, or \(\sigma = (\mathrm{UCL} - \mathrm{CL})/3\)). Write down CL ± σ, ± 2σ, ± 3σ.
  2. Convert each point to a z-score if it helps: \(z_i = (\text{point}_i - \mathrm{CL})/\sigma\). Then the lines are simply ±1, ±2, ±3.
  3. Walk forward in time. At each sample \(i\), look only at windows that END at \(i\): the last 1 point (rule 1), the last 3 (rule 2), the last 5 (rule 3), the last 8 (rule 4).
  4. Same side only. A point above +2σ and a point below −2σ do not combine. Points exactly on a line are not "beyond" it.
  5. Stop at the first \(i\) where any rule fires. That \(i\) is the answer. Say which rule did it.

Heads up: the window must be complete

A window ending at sample \(i\) needs \(i\) to be at least 3, 5 or 8. In the lecture example, points 1 to 4 were all already below −1σ, yet the instructor said rule 3 fires at point 5: you need five points before "four out of five" can be judged. Likewise "two out of three" can first fire at sample 3, and rule 4 at sample 8. Later in a series this is automatic.

Worked example · Lecture 8F (moisture X̄ chart)

An X̄ chart of moisture content has 32 subgroups. CL \(= \overline{\overline X} = 2.650\) and \(\hat\sigma = 0.1165\) (the SD of X̄), so the lines are: −3σ = 2.300, −2σ = 2.417, −1σ = 2.533, +1σ = 2.767, +2σ = 2.883, +3σ = 3.000. The first five subgroup means are 2.308, 2.498, 2.395, 2.35, 2.50 (values after the first two are read off the plot). By the Western Electric rules, is the process out of control, and at which group is it first detected?

  1. z-scores. \((2.308 - 2.650)/0.1165 = -2.94\); then −1.30, −2.19, −2.57, −1.29.
  2. Rule 1. Point 1 at −2.94 is inside −3. No point beyond 3σ until point 12 (3.05 > 3.000).
  3. Rule 2. Window 1 to 3: points 1 (−2.94) and 3 (−2.19) are both below −2σ. Two of three on the same side, completed at point 3.
  4. Rule 3. Window 1 to 5: all five are below −1σ. Completed at point 5 (the first full window).
  5. Rule 4. Points 10 to 17 are all above the CL, completed at point 17.
  6. Earliest. min(12, 3, 5, 17) = 3.

Answer: out of control, first at the 3rd datum (rule 2). The instructor boxed "OoC at 3rd datum".

Worked example · 8F worksheet

An X̄ chart of 32 samples shows the lines 3σ = 4.725, 2σ = 3.987, 1σ = 3.249, \(\overline{\overline X}\) = 2.511, −1σ = 1.773, −2σ = 1.035, −3σ = 0.297. The first eight points read off the chart are about 2.67, 3.87, 2.62, 3.58, 3.38, 2.58, 3.40, 3.52 (later points include a drop to about 1.30 at sample 16 and a low of about 0.57 at sample 25). By the Western Electric rules, at what sample would you first declare the process out of control?

  1. σ. \(3.249 - 2.511 = 0.738\). z-scores of samples 1 to 8: 0.22, 1.84, 0.15, 1.45, 1.18, 0.09, 1.20, 1.37.
  2. Rule 1. Nothing above 4.725 or below 0.297, ever (the low point 0.57 is still inside).
  3. Rule 2. Sample 2 (3.87) is close to the 2σ line but below 3.987. No pair of points beyond 2σ. Never fires.
  4. Rule 3. Points above 3.249: samples 2, 4, 5, 7, 8. Window 1 to 5: 3 of 5. Window 2 to 6: 3. Window 3 to 7: 3. Window 4 to 8: samples 4, 5, 7, 8 = 4 of 5. Fires at 8.
  5. Rule 4. Samples 1 to 8 are all above 2.511. Fires at 8.

Answer: sample 8 (rules 3 and 4 both complete there). Answering 16 or 25 is wrong: neither crosses 3σ, and the patterns fire much earlier.

Check yourself · HW7 Q2 (c)

Same data as the Module 9 check (20 subgroups of \(n = 5\), \(\overline{\overline X} = 2.505\), \(\overline s = 0.057\)). The first ten means are 2.49, 2.45, 2.51, 2.53, 2.49, 2.44, 2.44, 2.42, 2.42, 2.47. Given the Western Electric rules, what is the earliest point at which you would deem the process out of control?

Hint

\(\hat\sigma = 0.057/(0.94\sqrt5) = 0.02712\). Lines: −1σ = 2.4779, −2σ = 2.4508, −3σ = 2.4236.

Full solution
  1. z-scores. −0.55, −2.03, 0.18, 0.92, −0.55, −2.40, −2.40, −3.13, −3.13, −1.29.
  2. Rule 1. Sample 8 (−3.13) is the first beyond 3σ. Fires at 8.
  3. Rule 2. Below −2σ: samples 2, 6, 7. Window 2 to 4 has only sample 2. Window 5 to 7 has samples 6 and 7. Fires at 7.
  4. Rule 3. Below −1σ: samples 2, 6, 7, 8, 9. Window 5 to 9 has 6, 7, 8, 9. Fires at 9.
  5. Rule 4. Samples 5 to 10 are below the CL (6 in a row), then sample 11 (2.54) is above. Never 8 in a row in the data.
  6. Earliest. Sample 7, by rule 2. One sample earlier than the plain chart (sample 8).

Answer: out of control at sample 7 (rule 2).

Check yourself · Original practice (rule drill in z-scores)

Each sequence lists points as z-scores (distance from the CL in σ units), starting at sample 1. For each, which rule fires first, and at which sample? Say "none" if no rule fires.

(A) 0.4, 2.3, 1.1, 2.5
(B) −1.2, −0.5, −1.4, −1.1, −1.3
(C) 0.2, 0.5, 0.9, 0.3, 0.7, 0.1, 0.6, 0.4
(D) 1.5, −2.4, 0.3, 2.6, 0.8, 3.2
(E) 2.2, 0.5, −0.3, 2.4, 0.6, −0.2, 0.4
(F) 1.2, 1.5, 0.4, 1.3, 1.1, 2.1, 2.2, 3.1

Full solution
  1. (A) Window 2 to 4 is 2.3, 1.1, 2.5: two above +2. Rule 2 at sample 4.
  2. (B) Window 1 to 5: −1.2, −1.4, −1.1, −1.3 are below −1 (four of five). Rule 3 at sample 5.
  3. (C) Eight points, all above the CL, none beyond 1. Rule 4 at sample 8.
  4. (D) Samples 2 (−2.4) and 4 (+2.6) are beyond 2σ on opposite sides, so window 2 to 4 does not count. Sample 6 (3.2) is beyond 3σ: rule 1 at sample 6. (Window 4 to 6 also has 2.6 and 3.2, so rule 2 completes at 6 too.)
  5. (E) Samples 1 and 4 are above +2, but no window of 3 consecutive points contains both. Nothing else fires. None.
  6. (F) Above +1: samples 1, 2, 4, 5 → four of five in window 1 to 5. Rule 3 at sample 5. Rule 2 would fire at 7 and rules 1 and 4 at 8, but 5 comes first.

Answer: A: rule 2 at 4. B: rule 3 at 5. C: rule 4 at 8. D: rule 1 (and 2) at 6. E: none. F: rule 3 at 5.

Average run length (ARL)

The ARL is the average number of subgroups you inspect until the chart flags one. Let \(Y\) be the number of subgroups inspected up to and including the first flagged one. Each subgroup is an independent trial that is flagged with probability \(P\), so \(Y\) is geometric, and the mean of a geometric is one over the success probability.

\[ \mathrm{ARL} = E(Y) = \frac{1}{P(\text{one point is flagged})} \]

Not on the sheet. Memorize it. A long ARL is what you want when the process is in control (rare false alarms). A short ARL is what you want after a real shift (fast detection).

Worked example · Lecture 8F (in-control ARL and ARL after a 1σ shift)

(a) A process is in control, with \(\overline X_i\) normal with mean \(\mu\) and SD \(\sigma\) (the SD of X̄). On average, how many samples until a point falls beyond the 3σ limits? (b) Now the true mean has shifted to \(\mu' = \mu + \sigma\), but the limits stay at \(\mu \pm 3\sigma\). What is the ARL now?

  1. (a) P(flag). \(P(\overline X < \mu - 3\sigma \text{ or } \overline X > \mu + 3\sigma) = 2 \times 0.00135 = 0.0027\). (The standard normal c.d.f. at 3 is 0.99865.)
  2. (a) ARL. \(1/0.0027 = 370.4\). Every flag here is a false alarm: about one every 370 subgroups. You would investigate, find nothing, and move on.
  3. (b) Re-express the limits around the new mean. LCL \(= \mu - 3\sigma = \mu' - 4\sigma\). UCL \(= \mu + 3\sigma = \mu' + 2\sigma\).
  4. (b) P(flag). \(P(\overline X < \mu' - 4\sigma) \approx 3\times10^{-5}\), small enough to ignore. \(P(\overline X > \mu' + 2\sigma) = 1 - \Phi(2) \approx 0.0227\) (the standard normal c.d.f. at 2 is 0.9773).
  5. (b) ARL. \(1/0.0227 \approx 44.05\). Using the unrounded 0.02275 gives 43.96. Either way, about 44.

Answer: (a) ARL = 370.4. (b) ARL ≈ 44. As the instructor wrote: it "can still take a long time to detect an OoC process!" That is the motivation for the Western Electric rules and the CUSUM.

How the ARL changes for other shifts of the mean (δ in units of σ of X̄). These come from the same calculation; the exam may ask about the direction, not the numbers.

Shift δ0 (in control)0.511.523
ARL370.415544156.32

Heads up: what the exam expects on ARL

The instructor's practice-question pointers say: know what the ARL is and how it would change with specified changes in the process mean, but don't expect quantitative ARL calculations. So know the definition, \(\mathrm{ARL} = 1/P\), the value 370.4, and that a bigger shift means a shorter ARL. The 44 calculation above is there so you understand why.

Check yourself · HW7 Q3

A process is declared out of control if any point falls outside the 3σ limits on an X̄ chart. The process is in control. (a) What is the probability that a false alarm occurs within the next 50 samples? Treat each subgroup as a trial that "succeeds" (a false alarm) with probability \(p = P(\overline X < \mu - 3\sigma \text{ or } \overline X > \mu + 3\sigma)\), and let \(Y \sim \mathrm{Binom}(50, p)\) count the false alarms. (b) What if you examine 100 samples?

Hint

\(P(Y \ge 1) = 1 - P(Y = 0)\), and the binomial p.m.f. at 0 is \((1-p)^{k}\).

Full solution
  1. p. \(p = 0.0027\).
  2. (a) \(P(Y = 0) = \binom{50}{0}p^0(1-p)^{50} = 0.9973^{50} = 0.8736\). So \(P(Y \ge 1) = 1 - 0.8736 = 0.1264\).
  3. (b) \(0.9973^{100} = 0.7631\), so \(P(Y \ge 1) = 0.2369\).
  4. Interpret. Doubling the number of samples almost doubles the chance of at least one false alarm (not exactly, since \(1 - (1-p)^k\) bends over as \(k\) grows). Even a perfectly good process will eventually throw a false alarm.

Answer: (a) about 0.126; (b) about 0.237.

The CUSUM chart

CUSUM stands for cumulative sum. Instead of judging each point alone, you keep two running totals: SH collects evidence that the mean has gone UP, and SL collects evidence that it has gone DOWN.

Quality Control · CUSUM chart (three lines)

\[ \begin{aligned} \mathrm{SH}_i &= \max(0,\ \overline X_i - \mu - k\sigma + \mathrm{SH}_{i-1}) \\ \mathrm{SL}_i &= \min(0,\ \overline X_i - \mu + k\sigma + \mathrm{SL}_{i-1}) \\ \mathrm{SH}_0 &= \mathrm{SL}_0 = 0 \end{aligned} \]
\[ \text{out of control if } \mathrm{SH}_i > h\sigma \text{ or } \mathrm{SL}_i < -h\sigma \quad (h = 4) \]

"SH: take the last SH, add how far this mean is above the center minus a slack of k sigma, and never go below zero. SL: the mirror image, never above zero. Alarm when either one gets h sigma away from zero."

\(\overline X_i\)
the mean of subgroup \(i\).
\(\mu\)
the in-control process mean, estimated by \(\hat\mu = \overline{\overline X}\).
\(\sigma\)
the SD of a subgroup MEAN, the same as on the X̄ chart: \(\hat\sigma = \overline S/(c_4\sqrt n)\). The lecture writes it as \(A_3\overline S/3\), which is the same thing.
\(k\)
the slack (also called the reference value), in units of σ. \(k = 1/2\) by convention. This is NOT printed on the sheet: remember it. Deviations smaller than \(k\sigma\) are treated as noise and get absorbed.
\(h\)
the decision threshold, in units of σ. The sheet says \(h = 4\) (the lecture says 4 or 5 by convention). The alarm lines are at \(\pm h\sigma = \pm 4\sigma\).
\(\max(0,\cdot)\), \(\min(0,\cdot)\)
reset to 0 when the evidence points the other way. SH is never negative and SL is never positive.
\(+k\sigma\) in SL
the sign flips. SH subtracts \(k\sigma\); SL ADDS \(k\sigma\). The instructor circled this "+" in the lecture.

Heads up: sign typo on your sheet

The sheet prints "process out-of-control if \(\mathrm{SH}_i > h\sigma\) or \(\mathrm{SL}_i < h\sigma\)". That cannot be right: SL is always 0 or negative, so "\(\mathrm{SL}_i < h\sigma\)" would be true at every sample. The lecture writes the correct version: \(\mathrm{SL}_i < -h\sigma\).

Heads up: yet another k

This \(k\) is the CUSUM slack, 1/2. It has nothing to do with the \(k\) of the binomial or p chart (items per sample) or of chi-square (total count).

Why CUSUM catches small, persistent shifts

Look at the increment SH adds each time: \(\overline X_i - \mu - k\sigma\). When the process is in control, \(\overline X_i - \mu\) averages 0, so the increment averages \(-k\sigma = -0.5\sigma\). SH keeps getting pulled back to 0. Now suppose the mean shifts up by 1σ. The increment averages \(+1\sigma - 0.5\sigma = +0.5\sigma\) every sample, and those add up. Starting from 0, SH needs to climb past \(4\sigma\), which takes roughly 8 samples on average. Compare that with the ARL of about 44 for the plain X̄ chart. The CUSUM remembers; the X̄ chart forgets each point as soon as it is plotted.

Method: a CUSUM table by hand

  1. Get \(\mu\), \(\sigma\), \(k\sigma\), \(h\sigma\). \(\hat\mu = \overline{\overline X}\), \(\hat\sigma = \overline S/(c_4\sqrt n)\), \(k\sigma = \hat\sigma/2\), \(h\sigma = 4\hat\sigma\).
  2. Make columns: \(i\), \(\overline X_i\), \(\overline X_i - \mu - k\sigma\), \(\mathrm{SH}_i\), \(\overline X_i - \mu + k\sigma\), \(\mathrm{SL}_i\). Start with \(\mathrm{SH}_0 = \mathrm{SL}_0 = 0\).
  3. Each row: \(\mathrm{SH}_i\) = previous SH plus the first increment, but replace a negative result by 0. \(\mathrm{SL}_i\) = previous SL plus the second increment, but replace a positive result by 0.
  4. Check after every row: is \(\mathrm{SH}_i > h\sigma\) or \(\mathrm{SL}_i < -h\sigma\)? The first row where this happens is when the process is declared out of control. Strict inequality: landing exactly on \(h\sigma\) does not count.

Worked example · Lecture 8F (moisture CUSUM)

For the moisture data, \(\overline{\overline X} = 2.650\), \(\overline S = 0.245\) and \(A_3 = 1.427\) (so \(n = 5\)). The first subgroup means are 2.308 and 2.498. Find \(\mathrm{SH}_1, \mathrm{SH}_2, \mathrm{SL}_1, \mathrm{SL}_2\). Then, with the third mean at about 2.395 (read off the plot), decide when the CUSUM first flags.

  1. σ, kσ, hσ. \(\hat\sigma = A_3\overline S/3 = 1.427 \times 0.245/3 = 0.1165\). (Check: \(0.245/(0.94\sqrt5) = 0.1166\), the same to rounding.) \(k\sigma = 0.0583\). \(h\sigma = 4 \times 0.1165 = 0.466\).
  2. \(\mathrm{SH}_1\). \(\max(0,\ 2.308 - 2.650 - 0.0583 + 0) = \max(0, -0.400) = 0\).
  3. \(\mathrm{SL}_1\). \(\min(0,\ 2.308 - 2.650 + 0.0583 + 0) = \min(0, -0.284) = -0.284\).
  4. \(\mathrm{SH}_2\). \(\max(0,\ 2.498 - 2.650 - 0.0583 + 0) = \max(0, -0.210) = 0\).
  5. \(\mathrm{SL}_2\). \(\min(0,\ 2.498 - 2.650 + 0.0583 - 0.284) = -0.378\).
  6. \(\mathrm{SL}_3\). \(\min(0,\ 2.395 - 2.650 + 0.0583 - 0.378) \approx -0.574\). Is it below \(-0.466\)? Yes.

Answer: \(\mathrm{SH}_1 = \mathrm{SH}_2 = 0\), \(\mathrm{SL}_1 = -0.284\), \(\mathrm{SL}_2 = -0.378\). The CUSUM declares the process out of control at sample 3, the same sample the Western Electric rules gave.

Worked example · Original practice (a full CUSUM table)

An X̄ chart has \(\hat\mu = 10.0\) and \(\hat\sigma = 0.2\) (the SD of X̄). Use \(k = 1/2\) and \(h = 4\). The first 12 subgroup means are 10.1, 9.8, 10.0, 9.9, 10.3, 10.15, 10.1, 10.38, 10.15, 10.35, 10.35, 10.25. Run the CUSUM. When is the process first declared out of control? Would the plain X̄ chart have caught it?

  1. Constants. \(k\sigma = 0.1\), \(h\sigma = 0.8\). Alarm if SH > 0.8 or SL < −0.8.
  2. Fill the table.
    \(i\)\(\overline X_i\)\(\overline X_i - 10.1\)\(\mathrm{SH}_i\)\(\overline X_i - 9.9\)\(\mathrm{SL}_i\)
    110.1000.20
    29.8−0.30−0.1−0.1
    310.0−0.100.10
    49.9−0.2000
    510.30.20.20.40
    610.150.050.250.250
    710.100.250.20
    810.380.280.530.480
    910.150.050.580.250
    1010.350.250.830.450
    1110.350.251.080.450
    1210.250.151.230.350
    The third column is \(\overline X_i - \mu - k\sigma = \overline X_i - 10.1\). The fifth is \(\overline X_i - \mu + k\sigma = \overline X_i - 9.9\).
  3. Read a few rows aloud. Row 2: SL = min(0, −0.1 + 0) = −0.1. Row 3: SL = min(0, 0.1 − 0.1) = 0. Row 5: SH = max(0, 0.2 + 0) = 0.2. Row 10: SH = 0.58 + 0.25 = 0.83.
  4. First alarm. \(\mathrm{SH}_{10} = 0.83 > 0.8\). SL never gets near −0.8.
  5. Compare. The largest mean is 10.38, which is 1.9σ above 10.0, so the X̄ chart (UCL = 10.6) never flags. The Western Electric rules first fire at sample 12 (rules 3 and 4).

Answer: out of control at sample 10 by the CUSUM (SH upward). The plain chart misses the shift entirely, and the Western Electric rules catch it two samples later.

Check yourself · Original practice (CUSUM, low side)

\(\hat\mu = 50\), \(\hat\sigma = 2\) (SD of X̄), \(k = 1/2\), \(h = 4\). The subgroup means are 49, 47, 48, 46, 47, 45. Compute SH and SL for each, and say when the process is first declared out of control.

Hint

\(k\sigma = 1\) and \(h\sigma = 8\). The SL increment is \(\overline X_i - 50 + 1 = \overline X_i - 49\).

Full solution
  1. SH. Every \(\overline X_i - 51\) is negative, so SH stays 0 throughout.
  2. SL increments \(\overline X_i - 49\): 0, −2, −1, −3, −2, −4.
  3. SL running. 0, −2, −3, −6, −8, −12.
  4. Check. \(\mathrm{SL}_5 = -8\) is not strictly below −8. \(\mathrm{SL}_6 = -12 < -8\).

Answer: SH = 0 throughout; SL = 0, −2, −3, −6, −8, −12. Out of control at sample 6.

Process capability: \(C_p\) and \(C_{pk}\)

Process capability is the ability of an IN-CONTROL process to meet design specifications: each item's measurement must land between a lower specification limit (LSL) and an upper specification limit (USL). Only ask about capability once the control charts say the process is in control; an unstable process has no single \(\mu\) and \(\sigma\) to judge.

Spec limits are not control limits

Specification limits (LSL, USL)Control limits (LCL, UCL)
Who sets themEngineering, the designer or the customerComputed from the process's own data
Apply toEach individual itemThe plotted statistic (a subgroup mean, SD, proportion or count)
Question answeredIs this part acceptable?Is the process behaving like itself?
Used in\(C_p\), \(C_{pk}\)X̄, S, p, c charts, Western Electric, CUSUM
Change them byRedesigning the productChanging the process (its mean or spread)

Never put LCL or UCL into a \(C_{pk}\) formula, and never use LSL or USL to decide whether a point is out of control. A process can be in control and still not capable, and a process can produce in-spec parts while being out of control.

Quality Control · (Actual) process capability

\[ C_{pk} = \min\left(\frac{\mu - \mathrm{LSL}}{3\sigma},\ \frac{\mathrm{USL} - \mu}{3\sigma}\right) \]

"C-p-k is the smaller of: the distance from the mean down to the lower spec, and up to the upper spec, each measured in units of three sigma."

\(C_{pk}\)
the actual, or realized, capability: how well the process does where it sits now, off-center or not.
\(\mu\)
the process mean, estimated by \(\overline{\overline X}\).
\(\sigma\)
the SD of ONE ITEM, estimated by \(\hat\sigma = \overline S/c_4\). There is no \(\sqrt n\): the specs apply to individual items, not to subgroup means. The lecture says "our statistic is X, not X̄, so not dividing by √n."
LSL, USL
the lower and upper specification limits, given by the problem.
\(\min\)
the side closer to the mean is the side that will produce bad parts first, so it decides.
Threshold
\(C_{pk} > 1\): the process is "minimally capable" (the mean is more than 3σ from the nearer spec limit).

Quality Control · (Potential) process capability

\[ C_p = \frac{\mathrm{USL} - \mathrm{LSL}}{6\sigma} \]

"C-p is the width of the spec window divided by six sigma."

\(C_p\)
the potential capability: what \(C_{pk}\) would be if the process were perfectly centered between the spec limits. It ignores where the mean is.
\(6\sigma\)
the natural width of the process (±3σ around the mean). \(\sigma = \overline S/c_4\), as above.
Threshold
\(C_p > 2\): "six sigma" quality. A correctly centered process would have the spec midpoint more than 6σ from each spec limit. This course uses no 1.5σ shift.

Two facts that come up as conceptual questions. \(C_{pk} \le C_p\) always. They are equal exactly when the mean sits at the spec midpoint \((\mathrm{LSL} + \mathrm{USL})/2\). So to raise \(C_{pk}\) you can center the process (up to \(C_p\)), and beyond that only reducing \(\sigma\) helps.

Heads up: the six-sigma boundary

The lecture defines six sigma as \(C_p > 2\), but in the battery example it says a process with \(C_p\) exactly 2 "would" meet it. Exact equality is unlikely on the exam. If it happens, say \(C_p = 2\) sits right at the six-sigma boundary.

Method: capability questions

  1. Read off LSL, USL (the specs) and \(\overline{\overline X}\). Ignore any control limits you were given.
  2. Get \(\hat\sigma = \overline S/c_4\) using \(n\) for \(c_4\). If the problem hands you \(\hat\sigma\) or \(\overline S/c_4\) directly, use it.
  3. \(C_{pk}\): compute both distances in units of \(3\hat\sigma\), take the smaller. Compare with 1.
  4. \(C_p\): spec width over \(6\hat\sigma\). Compare with 2.
  5. Conclude in words: "minimally capable / not minimally capable" and "meets / does not meet six-sigma quality".

Worked example · 9M worksheet

Specifications say the item should measure between 2 and 3. The process is monitored with an X̄ chart, with \(\hat\sigma = 0.1\) and \(\overline{\overline X} = 2.4\). Is the process at least minimally capable? Give \(C_{pk}\).

  1. Identify. LSL = 2, USL = 3, \(\hat\mu = 2.4\), \(\hat\sigma = 0.1\) (taken as the SD of individual items, as capability needs).
  2. Lower side. \((2.4 - 2)/(3 \times 0.1) = 0.4/0.3 = 1.333\).
  3. Upper side. \((3 - 2.4)/0.3 = 0.6/0.3 = 2.0\).
  4. \(C_{pk}\). \(\min(1.333, 2.0) = 1.333\).
  5. Conclude. \(1.333 > 1\): minimally capable. (Bonus: \(C_p = 1/(6 \times 0.1) = 1.667 < 2\), so not six sigma. \(C_{pk} < C_p\) because 2.4 is off the midpoint 2.5.)

Answer: \(C_{pk} \approx 1.33\). Yes, minimally capable.

Worked example · Lecture 9M (\(C_{pk}\) with \(c_4\))

Specs say an item must measure between 9 and 11. The process is monitored with an X̄ chart with \(n = 6\), \(\overline{\overline X} = 9.7\) and \(\overline S = 0.3\). Is it minimally capable?

  1. \(c_4\) for \(n = 6\). \(\Gamma(3)\sqrt{2/5}/\Gamma(5/2) = 2 \times 0.6325/1.3293 = 0.9515\). (The instructor circled \(n = 6\): you need it for \(c_4\).)
  2. \(\hat\sigma\) for items. \(0.3/0.9515 = 0.3153\). No \(\sqrt n\). So \(3\hat\sigma = 0.9458\).
  3. Both sides. \((9.7 - 9)/0.9458 = 0.740\). \((11 - 9.7)/0.9458 = 1.374\).
  4. \(C_{pk}\). \(\min(0.740, 1.374) = 0.740\). (Neat form: \(\tfrac{7}{9}c_4\).)

Answer: \(C_{pk} \approx 0.740 < 1\). Not minimally capable. The mean sits too close to the LSL.

Worked example · HW7 Q5

The thickness specification for aluminum sheets is 0.246 to 0.254 mm. An X̄ chart with rational subgroups of size 6 shows the process is in control with \(\overline{\overline X} = 0.248\) and \(\overline S = 0.002\). (a) Compute \(C_{pk}\). (b) Is the process capability acceptable? (c) To what value should the process mean be set to maximize capability?

  1. σ for items. \(c_4(6) = 0.9515\), so \(\hat\sigma = 0.002/0.9515 = 0.002102\) and \(3\hat\sigma = 0.006306\).
  2. (a) Both sides. \((0.248 - 0.246)/0.006306 = 0.317\). \((0.254 - 0.248)/0.006306 = 0.951\). \(C_{pk} = 0.317\).
  3. (b) \(0.317 < 1\), so not even minimally capable. Not acceptable.
  4. (c) \(C_{pk}\) is largest when both sides are equal, at the spec midpoint \((0.246 + 0.254)/2 = 0.250\). Then \(C_{pk} = C_p = 0.008/(6 \times 0.002102) = 0.634\).
  5. Interpret (c). Even perfectly centered, 0.634 < 1. Centering helps, but the spread itself must shrink for this process to become capable.

Answer: (a) \(C_{pk} \approx 0.317\). (b) No, \(C_{pk} < 1\). (c) Set the mean to 0.250 mm (gives \(C_{pk} = C_p \approx 0.634\), still below 1).

Check yourself · Lecture 9M (\(C_p\), battery voltage)

Battery voltage must be between 8.7 and 9.3. The process is in control with \(\overline S/c_4 = 0.075\). What is \(C_p\)? Does the process meet six-sigma quality? If not, what σ would it need?

Full solution
  1. \(C_p\). \((9.3 - 8.7)/(6 \times 0.075) = 0.6/0.45 = 4/3 \approx 1.33\).
  2. Six sigma? \(1.33 < 2\). No.
  3. Needed σ. Solve \(0.6/(6\sigma) = 2\): \(\sigma = 0.6/12 = 0.05\). That is a one-third reduction from 0.075.

Answer: \(C_p = 4/3\); does not meet six-sigma quality. It would (right at the boundary) if σ dropped to 0.05.

Check yourself · Original practice (\(C_p\) and \(C_{pk}\) together)

Specs are 10 ± 0.6. From an X̄ chart with \(n = 5\): \(\overline{\overline X} = 10.2\), \(\overline S = 0.12\). Find \(C_p\) and \(C_{pk}\). Is the process minimally capable? Six sigma?

Full solution
  1. Specs. LSL = 9.4, USL = 10.6.
  2. σ for items. \(c_4(5) = 0.9400\); \(\hat\sigma = 0.12/0.94 = 0.1277\); \(3\hat\sigma = 0.3830\); \(6\hat\sigma = 0.7660\).
  3. \(C_p\). \(1.2/0.766 = 1.567\).
  4. \(C_{pk}\). \(\min((10.2 - 9.4)/0.383,\ (10.6 - 10.2)/0.383) = \min(2.089, 1.044) = 1.044\).

Answer: \(C_p \approx 1.57\), \(C_{pk} \approx 1.04\). Minimally capable (\(C_{pk} > 1\)), not six sigma (\(C_p < 2\)).

Check yourself · Original practice (conceptual)

(a) Every point on a process's X̄ and S charts is inside the control limits. Does that mean its parts meet spec? (b) A student computes \(C_{pk}\) using UCL and LCL in place of USL and LSL. What is wrong? (c) Can \(C_{pk}\) ever exceed \(C_p\)?

Full solution
  1. (a) No. In control means stable; it says nothing about the specs. A stable process centered in the wrong place, or with too much spread, makes out-of-spec parts every day. Check \(C_{pk}\).
  2. (b) Control limits come from the data and describe subgroup statistics. Capability compares the process with the engineering specs for individual items, so it must use LSL and USL (and \(\sigma = \overline S/c_4\), not the X̄ chart's \(\overline S/(c_4\sqrt n)\)).
  3. (c) No. \(C_{pk}\) uses the smaller half-distance, and the smaller of two numbers is at most their average, which is \(C_p\). They are equal only when the process is centered.

Answer: (a) no; (b) wrong limits (and probably the wrong σ); (c) no, \(C_{pk} \le C_p\).

Common mistakes

  • Reporting the most extreme point as "when" the process went out of control. Report the first sample that completes any rule's pattern.
  • Combining points on opposite sides of the CL for rules 2 and 3. Same side only.
  • Firing "4 of 5" at sample 4. The window needs 5 points.
  • Copying the sheet's typo "\(\mathrm{SL}_i < h\sigma\)". It is \(\mathrm{SL}_i < -h\sigma\).
  • Using \(-k\sigma\) in SL. SL adds \(k\sigma\); SH subtracts it.
  • Forgetting the reset: SH can never go below 0, SL never above 0.
  • Using \(\overline S/c_4\) as the CUSUM σ. CUSUM uses the SD of a mean, \(\overline S/(c_4\sqrt n)\). Capability uses \(\overline S/c_4\).
  • Mixing up spec limits and control limits. Specs are set by engineering; control limits come from data.
  • Using 1.33 as the "minimally capable" cutoff or adding a 1.5σ shift to six sigma. In this course: \(C_{pk} > 1\) and \(C_p > 2\).

Quick check

The process is in control with 3σ limits. What is the ARL, and where does it come from?

370.4. ARL = 1/P(flag) = 1/0.0027, because the wait until the first flag is geometric.

If the process mean shifts by a larger amount, does the ARL get longer or shorter?

Shorter. A bigger shift puts more of the X̄ distribution beyond a limit, so P(flag) grows and 1/P shrinks (about 44 for 1σ, about 6 for 2σ, 2 for 3σ).

Points are at z = 2.5, −2.2, 0.4. Does rule 2 fire at the third point?

No. The two points beyond 2σ are on opposite sides. Rule 2 needs two of three beyond 2σ on the same side.

Eight consecutive points sit between the CL and +1σ. Out of control?

Yes, by rule 4. Eight in a row on the same side of the center line, even though none is far out.

What value of k does the CUSUM use, and is it on the sheet?

k = 1/2, and it is not on the sheet. It is the slack: deviations smaller than σ/2 get absorbed.

Why can SL never be positive?

Because of the \(\min(0,\cdot)\). SL only accumulates evidence of a downward shift; any upward evidence resets it to 0.

Which is the "potential" capability, \(C_p\) or \(C_{pk}\)?

\(C_p\). It is what you would get if the process were centered. \(C_{pk}\) is the actual (realized) capability.

\(C_{pk} = 1.2\) and \(C_p = 2.4\). Minimally capable? Six sigma?

Minimally capable, yes (\(C_{pk} > 1\)). Six sigma, yes by the course definition (\(C_p > 2\)), though the process is badly off-center and centering it would raise \(C_{pk}\) to 2.4.

For capability, σ is estimated by \(\overline S/(c_4\sqrt n)\).

False. Capability is about individual items, so \(\hat\sigma = \overline S/c_4\), with no \(\sqrt n\).

If you see this on the exam

  • "By the Western Electric rules, when is the process first out of control?" → get σ = (UCL − CL)/3, convert to z, walk forward, check windows of 1, 3, 5, 8 ending at each point, same side only; answer = first completing point.
  • "What is the ARL?" → \(1/P(\text{flag})\); in control 1/0.0027 = 370.4; after a 1σ shift about 44; bigger shift, shorter ARL.
  • "Probability of a false alarm within N samples" → \(1 - (1 - 0.0027)^N\).
  • "Compute the CUSUM" → \(\sigma = \overline S/(c_4\sqrt n)\), \(k\sigma = \sigma/2\), \(h\sigma = 4\sigma\); SH subtracts kσ and floors at 0; SL adds kσ and caps at 0; alarm when SH > hσ or SL < −hσ.
  • "Why use CUSUM?" → it accumulates small, persistent deviations, so it detects small shifts much faster than a 3σ X̄ chart.
  • "Compute \(C_{pk}\); minimally capable?" → \(\hat\sigma = \overline S/c_4\) (no √n), smaller of the two distances over 3σ, compare with 1.
  • "Six-sigma quality?" → \(C_p = (\mathrm{USL} - \mathrm{LSL})/(6\sigma)\), compare with 2.
  • "Where should the mean be set?" → at the spec midpoint; then \(C_{pk} = C_p\).

Module 11 · Review drill for exam morning · about 30 min

Formula decoder: every line of the sheet as a flashcard

The exam gives you the formula sheet, so the skill being tested is reading it fast and knowing exactly what each symbol is. This drill walks the sheet in the order it is printed, one card per line.

After this drill you can

  • Point at any line of the formula sheet and say what it means in one sentence.
  • Name every symbol in that line and where its number comes from.
  • Say which kind of exam question sends you to that line.

How to use the cards

Put your formula sheet next to the screen. For each card: cover the answer, read the formula, and say out loud what it means, what every symbol is, and when you would use it. Then flip it open and check. If you missed anything, follow the module link at the bottom of the card later, not now. Speed matters more than perfection on the first pass; aim for about 20 seconds a card.

The grey label on each card ("PMF · line 1") tells you where the line sits on the sheet. The left column runs from Probability Mass Functions down to Test Statistics; the right column runs from Confidence Intervals down to the Western Electric box.

Three things on the sheet that will trip you

Sample mean typo. The sheet prints \(\overline X = \sum_{i=1}^n X_i\). The \(1/n\) is missing. Use \(\overline X = \frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n X_i\).

CUSUM typo. The sheet says out of control if \(\mathrm{SL}_i < h\sigma\). SL is never positive, so that would always be true. Use \(\mathrm{SL}_i < -h\sigma\).

The letter k means three different things. In the Binomial block, \(k\) is the number of trials. In the chi-square lines, \(k\) is the TOTAL count (sum of all the data), and in the p-chart \(k\) is the number of items in each sample. In the CUSUM lines, \(k\sigma\) is a slack amount with \(k = 1/2\) by convention. Look at which block you are in.

Probability Mass Functions

PMF · line 1, first part

\[ 0 \le p_X(x) \le 1 \]
What it means

Read it as: every probability a p.m.f. gives is between 0 and 1.

Symbols: \(X\) is a discrete random variable (it takes separate values like 0, 1, 2). \(x\) is one particular value. \(p_X(x) = P(X = x)\) is the probability mass function (p.m.f.).

On the exam: a quick check that a proposed p.m.f. is valid, or that your computed probability is sensible.

Review: Module 1

PMF · line 1, second part

\[ \textstyle\sum_x p_X(x) = 1 \]
What it means

Read it as: the probabilities of all possible values add up to 1.

Symbols: the sum runs over every value \(x\) that \(X\) can take.

On the exam: solving for a missing probability in a table, or checking a p.m.f.

Review: Module 1

PMF · line 1, third part

\[ F_X(x) = \textstyle\sum_{y \le x} p_Y(y) \]
What it means

Read it as: the c.d.f. at \(x\) is the total probability of all values at or below \(x\).

Symbols: \(F_X(x) = P(X \le x)\) is the cumulative distribution function (c.d.f.). \(y\) is a dummy counting variable. The sheet writes \(p_Y(y)\); read it as \(p_X(y)\), the same p.m.f. evaluated at \(y\).

On the exam: a discrete c.d.f. is a staircase that jumps by \(p_X(x)\) at each possible value. Every p-value is a c.d.f. value, so this is where p-values come from for counts.

Review: Module 1, Module 2

PMF · line 2, inverse c.d.f.

\[ F_X^{-1}(q) = \inf\{x : F_X(x) \ge q\} \]
What it means

Read it as: the inverse c.d.f. at \(q\) is the smallest value \(x\) whose c.d.f. reaches at least \(q\).

Symbols: \(q \in [0,1]\) is a quantile level (a probability). "inf" means "the smallest such value". \(F_X^{-1}\) is the inverse c.d.f., also called the quantile function.

On the exam: the "smallest x that reaches q" wording is needed because a staircase c.d.f. usually jumps over \(q\) and never equals it exactly. It gives rejection-region boundaries for discrete statistics (binomial or Poisson sums). Because of the jumps, the real Type I error rate usually cannot equal \(\alpha\) exactly.

Review: Module 2

PMF · line 3

\[ \mathbb{P}(a \le X \le b) = \textstyle\sum_{x \in [a,b]} p_X(x) \]
What it means

Read it as: the probability that \(X\) lands between \(a\) and \(b\) is the sum of the p.m.f. over the values in that range.

Symbols: \(a, b\) are the ends of the range, both included.

On the exam: for discrete variables the endpoints matter: \(P(X \le 3)\) and \(P(X < 3)\) differ. Equivalent c.d.f. form: \(F_X(b) - F_X(a-1)\) for integer values.

Review: Module 1

Probability Density Functions

PDF · line 1, first part

\[ f_X(x) \ge 0 \]
What it means

Read it as: a density is never negative.

Symbols: \(X\) is a continuous random variable. \(f_X(x)\) is its probability density function (p.d.f.). A density value is not a probability and can be bigger than 1.

On the exam: checking a proposed p.d.f., such as \(\theta x^{\theta-1}\) on \([0,1]\) from HW5.

Review: Module 1

PDF · line 1, second part

\[ \textstyle\int_x f_X(x)\,dx = 1 \]
What it means

Read it as: the total area under a density is 1.

Symbols: the integral runs over every value \(X\) can take (its support).

On the exam: finding a missing constant in a p.d.f., or checking that your c.d.f. reaches 1 at the top of the support.

Review: Module 1

PDF · line 1, third part

\[ F_X(x) = \textstyle\int_{y \le x} f_Y(y)\,dy \]
What it means

Read it as: the c.d.f. at \(x\) is the area under the density to the left of \(x\).

Symbols: \(F_X(x) = P(X \le x)\). \(y\) is the integration variable; read \(f_Y(y)\) as the same density \(f_X\) evaluated at \(y\).

On the exam: this is the line you use most. Integrate the given p.d.f. to get the c.d.f. Example: \(\int_0^x \frac1\theta e^{-y/\theta}dy = 1 - e^{-x/\theta}\). Every by-hand p-value, power and CI starts here.

Review: Module 2

PDF · line 2, inverse c.d.f.

\[ F_X^{-1}(q) = x \]
What it means

Read it as: the inverse c.d.f. takes a probability \(q\) and returns the value \(x\) that has fraction \(q\) of the distribution at or below it.

Symbols: \(q \in [0,1]\) is the quantile level. To find \(x\), set \(F_X(x) = q\) and solve for \(x\).

On the exam: rejection-region boundaries. Exponential: \(1 - e^{-x/\theta} = q\) gives \(x = -\theta\ln(1-q)\). HW5 Q2: \(F(x) = x^\theta\) gives \(x = q^{1/\theta}\).

Review: Module 2

PDF · line 3, first part

\[ f_X(x) = \tfrac{d}{dx}F_X(x) \]
What it means

Read it as: the density is the slope (derivative) of the c.d.f.

Symbols: \(f_X\) is the p.d.f., \(F_X\) is the c.d.f.

On the exam: "given this c.d.f., what is the p.d.f.?" (HW6 Q3c: \(\frac{d}{dx}x^\theta = \theta x^{\theta-1}\)). Going the other way, integrate.

Review: Module 1

PDF · line 3, second part

\[ \mathbb{P}(a \le X \le b) = \textstyle\int_a^b f_X(x)\,dx \]
What it means

Read it as: the probability that \(X\) lands between \(a\) and \(b\) is the area under the density between them.

Symbols: \(a, b\) are the ends of the interval. For continuous \(X\), including or excluding the endpoints changes nothing.

On the exam: usually computed as \(F_X(b) - F_X(a)\).

Review: Module 1

Characterizing Distributions

Characterizing · line 1

\[ \mathbb{E}(X) = \textstyle\sum_x x\,p_X(x) \ \text{or}\ \int_x x f_X(x)\,dx \]
What it means

Read it as: the expected value is the probability-weighted average of the possible values.

Symbols: \(\mathbb{E}(X)\), also written \(\mu\), is the mean of the distribution. Use the sum for discrete \(X\) and the integral for continuous \(X\).

On the exam: mostly background. It tells you which way a parameter pushes the data, for example \(\mathbb{E}(X) = \theta\) for the exponential, so bigger \(\theta\) means bigger data.

Review: Module 1

Characterizing · line 2

\[ \mathbb{V}(X) = \sigma^2 = \mathbb{E}(X^2) - (\mathbb{E}(X))^2 \]
What it means

Read it as: the variance is the mean of the square minus the square of the mean.

Symbols: \(\mathbb{V}(X)\) or \(\sigma^2\) is the variance: the average squared distance from the mean. \(\mathbb{E}(X^2)\) is the mean of \(X^2\).
