On the exam: background for standard errors and for control limits like \(\bar c \pm 3\sqrt{\bar c}\) (a Poisson variance equals its mean).

Review: Module 1

Characterizing · line 3

\[ \sigma = \sqrt{\mathbb{V}(X)} \]
What it means

Read it as: the standard deviation is the square root of the variance.

Symbols: \(\sigma\) is the population standard deviation, in the same units as \(X\).

On the exam: \(\sigma\) is the "nuisance parameter" in power questions: bigger \(\sigma\) means lower power. In quality control, \(\sigma\) is estimated from \(\overline S\) and \(c_4\).

Review: Module 1

Characterizing · line 4, first part

\[ \mathbb{E}(aX + b) = a\,\mathbb{E}(X) + b \]
What it means

Read it as: scaling and shifting the data scales and shifts the mean the same way.

Symbols: \(a, b\) are fixed constants.

On the exam: unit changes, and why \(\mathbb{E}(\overline X) = \mu\) (with \(a = 1/n\) applied to a sum).

Review: Module 1

Characterizing · line 4, second part

\[ \mathbb{V}(aX + b) = a^2\,\mathbb{V}(X) \]
What it means

Read it as: scaling multiplies the variance by the square of the scale factor, and shifting does nothing to the variance.

Symbols: \(a, b\) are constants. The shift \(b\) disappears.

On the exam: this is why \(\mathbb{V}(\overline X) = \sigma^2/n\): the \(1/n\) gets squared.

Review: Module 1

Statistics

Sheet typo on the first line

The sheet prints \(\overline X = \sum_{i=1}^n X_i\). That is the sum, not the mean. The corrected line, shown on the card below, is \(\overline X = \frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n X_i\).

Statistics · line 1 (corrected)

\[ \overline X = \tfrac{1}{n}\textstyle\sum_{i=1}^n X_i \]
What it means

Typo fix: the sheet leaves out the \(1/n\). Always divide by \(n\).

Read it as: the sample mean is the sum of the data divided by how many data there are.

Symbols: \(X_1, \dots, X_n\) are the \(n\) data. \(n\) is the sample size. \(\overline X\) is the sample mean (a statistic, so it is random).

On the exam: t statistics, \(\overline D\) in the paired test, subgroup means on an \(\overline X\) chart, and the grand mean \(\overline{\overline X}\).

Review: Module 1

Statistics · line 2

\[ S = \Big[\tfrac{1}{n-1}\textstyle\sum_{i=1}^n (X_i - \overline X)^2\Big]^{1/2} \]
What it means

Read it as: the sample standard deviation is the square root of the sum of squared deviations from the mean, divided by \(n-1\).

Symbols: \(S\) estimates \(\sigma\). \(n - 1\) (not \(n\)) is used in the denominator. \(S^2\) is the sample variance.

On the exam: \(S_X, S_Y\) in Welch's test, \(S_D\) in the paired test, \(S_i\) for each subgroup on an S chart. Watch whether a problem gives \(S\) or \(S^2\).

Review: Module 1, Module 7

Statistics · line 3

\[ Y \text{ is drawn from a sampling distribution with } \mathbb{E}(Y),\ \mathbb{V}(Y) \]
What it means

Read it as: a statistic computed from random data is itself random, and its distribution is called its sampling distribution.

Symbols: \(Y\) is any statistic (for example \(\overline X\), a sum \(X_+\), or \(T\)). \(\mathbb{E}(Y)\) and \(\mathbb{V}(Y)\) are its mean and variance across repeated experiments.

On the exam: every test and CI needs this distribution: "Both require that we know (or can estimate) a sampling distribution!" It is the \(F_Y\) in \(F_Y(y;\theta) - q = 0\).

Review: Module 2

Statistics · line 4

\[ \mathrm{se}(Y) = \sqrt{\mathbb{V}(Y)} \]
What it means

Read it as: the standard error of a statistic is the standard deviation of its sampling distribution.

Symbols: \(\mathrm{se}(Y)\) measures how much \(Y\) would vary from experiment to experiment.

On the exam: the standard error of \(\overline X\) is \(\sigma/\sqrt n\) (or \(S/\sqrt n\)). In Welch's test, \(S_\Delta\) is the standard error of \(\overline X - \overline Y\).

Review: Module 1

Statistics · line 5, first part

\[ E[\overline X] = \mathbb{E}(X) = \mu \]
What it means

Read it as: on average, the sample mean equals the population mean.

Symbols: \(\mu\) is the population mean.

On the exam: this is why the sampling distribution of \(\overline X\) is centered at \(\mu\), and why the \(\overline X\) chart's center line \(\overline{\overline X}\) estimates \(\mu\).

Review: Module 1

Statistics · line 5, second part

\[ \mathbb{V}(\overline X) = \mathbb{V}(X)/n = \sigma^2/n \]
What it means

Read it as: the variance of the sample mean is the data variance divided by \(n\).

Symbols: \(\sigma^2\) is the variance of one datum, \(n\) is the sample size. Taking the square root gives \(\mathrm{se}(\overline X) = \sigma/\sqrt n\).

On the exam: this is the engine of "power goes up with \(n\)": the spread \(\sigma/\sqrt n\) shrinks, so the null and alternative distributions separate. It is also the \(\sqrt n\) in the \(\overline X\) chart limits.

Review: Module 1, Module 4

Binomial

Binomial · header

\[ X \sim \mathrm{Binom}(k, p) \qquad \text{Bernoulli: } k = 1 \]
What it means

Read it as: \(X\) counts the successes in \(k\) independent trials, each with success probability \(p\). A Bernoulli variable is a single trial.

Symbols: \(k\) = number of trials (here \(k\) means trials). \(p\) = probability of success on each trial.

On the exam: counts of defective items (the p chart assumes \(X_i \sim \mathrm{Binom}(k, p)\)), heads in coin flips. A multinomial experiment is this with more than two outcomes per trial.

Review: Module 1

Binomial · line 1

\[ p_X(x) = \tbinom{k}{x}p^x(1-p)^{k-x}, \quad x \in \{0,\dots,k\},\ p \in [0,1] \]
What it means

Read it as: the probability of exactly \(x\) successes is the number of ways to place them, times the probability of any one such sequence.

Symbols: \(\binom{k}{x}\) counts the arrangements. \(p^x(1-p)^{k-x}\) is the probability of one arrangement.

On the exam: small by-hand cases, like \(P(X = 0) = (1-p)^k\). Binomial c.d.f.s with many terms are done by software, so expect the value to be given.

Review: Module 1

Binomial · line 2, first part

\[ \tbinom{k}{x} = k!/[x!(k-x)!] \]
What it means

Read it as: "k choose x" is the number of ways to choose which \(x\) of the \(k\) trials are successes.

Symbols: \(!\) is the factorial: \(4! = 4\cdot3\cdot2\cdot1\), and \(0! = 1\).

On the exam: only inside a binomial p.m.f. calculation.

Review: Module 1

Binomial · line 2, second part

\[ \mathbb{E}(X) = kp \qquad \mathbb{V}(X) = kp(1-p) \]
What it means

Read it as: on average you get \(k\) times \(p\) successes, with variance \(kp(1-p)\).

Symbols: \(k\) trials, success probability \(p\).

On the exam: divide the variance by \(k^2\) to get the variance of a proportion, \(p(1-p)/k\). That is exactly what sits under the square root in the p-chart limits. In the chi-square test, the expected count \(kp_{i,0}\) is this mean.

Review: Module 1, Module 9

Binomial · sufficient statistic

\[ X_+ = \textstyle\sum_{i=1}^n X_i \sim \mathrm{Binom}(nk, p) \]
What it means

Read it as: if you repeat a \(k\)-trial binomial experiment \(n\) times, the total number of successes is binomial with \(nk\) trials.

Symbols: \(n\) = number of repeated experiments (data). \(k\) = trials per experiment. \(X_+\) = total successes, the statistic you test with.

On the exam: "what is the test statistic and its distribution under \(H_0\)?" Example (8M): 10 experiments of 10 flips, 68 heads: \(X_+ = 68 \sim \mathrm{Binom}(100, p)\). For discrete CIs, the lower bound uses \(y_{\rm obs} - 1\).

Review: Module 2, Module 5

Poisson

Poisson · header and p.m.f.

\[ X \sim \mathrm{Pois}(\lambda): \ p_X(x) = \tfrac{\lambda^x}{x!}e^{-\lambda},\ x \in \{0,1,\dots\},\ \lambda > 0 \]
What it means

Read it as: \(X\) counts events (flaws, hurricanes, arrivals) with no fixed upper limit, at average rate \(\lambda\).

Symbols: \(\lambda\) = expected count per unit (per year, per panel). \(x\) = an observed count.

On the exam: recognizing count data (5F worksheet). The c chart assumes flaws per unit are \(\mathrm{Pois}(c)\).

Review: Module 1

Poisson · line 1, end

\[ \mathbb{E}(X) = \mathbb{V}(X) = \lambda \]
What it means

Read it as: for a Poisson count, the mean and the variance are the same number.

Symbols: \(\lambda\) is both.

On the exam: this is why the c-chart limits are \(\bar c \pm 3\sqrt{\bar c}\): the standard deviation is the square root of the mean.

Review: Module 1, Module 9

Poisson · sufficient statistic

\[ X_+ = \textstyle\sum_{i=1}^n X_i \sim \mathrm{Pois}(n\lambda) \]
What it means

Read it as: the total of \(n\) independent Poisson counts is Poisson with mean \(n\lambda\).

Symbols: \(n\) = number of counts observed, \(X_+\) = their sum.

On the exam: 5F worksheet: data summing to 40 with \(n = 10\) and \(H_0: \lambda = 5\) give \(X_+ = 40\) compared against \(\mathrm{Pois}(50)\). For a CI you solve for \(n\lambda\), then divide the bounds by \(n\).

Review: Module 2, Module 5

Normal

Normal · header and p.d.f.

\[ X \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu, \sigma^2): \ f_X(x) = \tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^2}}e^{-(x-\mu)^2/(2\sigma^2)} \]
What it means

Read it as: the bell curve centered at \(\mu\) with spread \(\sigma\). Support: \(x, \mu \in (-\infty, \infty)\), \(\sigma > 0\).

Symbols: \(\mu\) = mean, \(\sigma^2\) = variance (the second argument is the variance, not the SD).

On the exam: the normal c.d.f. has no algebraic form, so normal p-values and power come with the c.d.f. value given. You may still need \(z = (\bar x - \mu_0)/(\sigma/\sqrt n)\). Useful facts: \(P(|Z| > 3) = 0.0027\), \(1 - \Phi(2) \approx 0.023\).

Review: Module 1

Normal · line 2

\[ \mathbb{E}(X) = \mu;\ \mathbb{V}(X) = \sigma^2 \]
What it means

Read it as: the two parameters of a normal are its mean and its variance.

Symbols: \(\mu\), \(\sigma^2\).

On the exam: in a test of \(\mu\), \(\sigma\) is the "other parameter". Larger \(\sigma\) lowers power.

Review: Module 1, Module 4

Normal · sufficient statistic, σ known

\[ \overline X \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu, \sigma^2/n) \]
What it means

Read it as: when \(\sigma\) is known, the sample mean is normal, centered at \(\mu\), with variance \(\sigma^2/n\).

Symbols: \(n\) = sample size. The standard error is \(\sigma/\sqrt n\).

On the exam: the 6M power example (\(n = 25\), \(\sigma = 20\), standard error 4, \(y_{\rm RR} = 146.58\)). Also the basis of the \(\overline X\) chart: limits sit 3 standard errors from the center.

Review: Module 2, Module 4

Normal · sufficient statistic, σ unknown

\[ T = (\overline X - \mu)/(S/\sqrt n) \sim t(n-1) \]
What it means

Read it as: when \(\sigma\) is unknown and you plug in \(S\), the standardized mean follows a t distribution with \(n - 1\) degrees of freedom.

Symbols: \(S\) = sample SD, \(n - 1\) = degrees of freedom. Under \(H_0\) you put \(\mu_0\) in for \(\mu\).

On the exam: one-sample t test (5F: \(T = (148-140)/(20/5) = 2\), \(t(24)\)). The paired t test is this same line applied to the differences. The t has heavier tails than the normal, so p-values are a bit bigger.

Review: Module 2, Module 7

Exponential

Exponential · header and p.d.f.

\[ X \sim \mathrm{Exp}(\theta): \ f_X(x) = \tfrac{1}{\theta}e^{-x/\theta},\ x \ge 0,\ \theta > 0 \]
What it means

Read it as: a waiting time or lifetime with mean \(\theta\).

Symbols: \(\theta\) = the scale parameter, which is also the mean.

On the exam: the star of the by-hand questions. Integrate this to get the c.d.f. (next card), then do p-values, rejection regions, power and CIs with logs.

Review: Module 2, Module 4

Exponential · not printed: c.d.f.

\[ F_X(x;\theta) = 1 - e^{-x/\theta}, \quad x \ge 0 \]
What it means

Not on the sheet. Memorize it or integrate the p.d.f.: \(\int_0^x \frac1\theta e^{-y/\theta}dy = 1 - e^{-x/\theta}\).

Read it as: the probability the lifetime is at most \(x\).

Symbols: upper tail \(1 - F = e^{-x/\theta}\).

On the exam: upper-tailed p-value \(= e^{-x_{\rm obs}/\theta_0}\). 5W worksheet: \(x_{\rm obs} = 3\), \(\theta_0 = 1\) gives \(p = e^{-3} = 0.0498\).

Review: Module 2

Exponential · not printed: inverse c.d.f.

\[ F_X^{-1}(q;\theta) = -\theta\ln(1-q) \]
What it means

Not on the sheet. Solve \(1 - e^{-x/\theta} = q\) for \(x\).

Read it as: the value with fraction \(q\) of lifetimes below it.

On the exam: rejection-region boundaries. \(\theta_0 = 1\), \(\alpha = 0.05\): upper-tailed \(y_{\rm RR} = -\ln 0.05 = 2.996\); lower-tailed \(y_{\rm RR} = -\ln 0.95 = 0.0513\).

Review: Module 2, Module 4

Exponential · line 1, end

\[ \mathbb{E}(X) = \theta \qquad \mathbb{V}(X) = \theta^2 \]
What it means

Read it as: the mean is \(\theta\) and the standard deviation is also \(\theta\).

Symbols: \(\theta\) = scale parameter.

On the exam: it tells you direction: bigger \(\theta\) means bigger data, so \(F_Y(y;\theta)\) goes down as \(\theta\) goes up. That is the direction the q table assumes.

Review: Module 1, Module 5

Exponential · sufficient statistic

\[ \overline X \sim \mathrm{Gamma}(n, \theta/n) \ \text{(shape-scale)} \]
What it means

Read it as: the mean of \(n\) exponential lifetimes follows a gamma distribution with shape \(n\) and scale \(\theta/n\).

Symbols: shape \(n\) = sample size, scale \(\theta/n\).

On the exam: the gamma c.d.f. is not algebraic for \(n > 1\), so by-hand exponential questions use ONE datum (\(n = 1\)), where \(\overline X = X \sim \mathrm{Exp}(\theta)\). With \(n > 1\), expect to name the distribution only.

Review: Module 2

Test Statistics

Test Statistics · KS test

\[ D_n = \max|F_{X_n}(x) - F_X(x)| \]
What it means

Read it as: the KS statistic is the largest vertical gap between the data's empirical c.d.f. and the null c.d.f.

Symbols: \(F_{X_n}(x)\) (also written \(\widehat F_n(x)\)) = empirical c.d.f., the fraction of the \(n\) data at or below \(x\), a staircase with steps of \(1/n\). \(F_X(x)\) = c.d.f. of the fully specified null distribution. \(n\) = sample size.

On the exam: given a table of \(F_{X_n}\) and \(F_X\) values, compute \(|F_{X_n} - F_X|\) in every row and take the largest (HW5 Q4: \(D_n = 0.35\)). Under \(H_0\), \(\sqrt n D_n\) follows the Kolmogorov distribution; critical values and p-values come from software. KS works only for continuous distributions and has low power. To test normality, use Shapiro-Wilk.

Review: Module 6

Test Statistics · Mann-Whitney U

\[ U_i = n_1n_2 + n_i(n_i+1)/2 - R_i \]
What it means

Read it as: for each group, start from \(n_1n_2\), add \(n_i(n_i+1)/2\), and subtract that group's rank sum.

Symbols: \(n_1, n_2\) = group sizes. \(i \in \{1, 2\}\) picks the group. \(R_i\) = sum of group \(i\)'s ranks in the pooled data.

On the exam: by hand: pool, rank, sum ranks, plug in. Checks: \(R_1 + R_2 = N(N+1)/2\) with \(N = n_1 + n_2\), and \(U_1 + U_2 = n_1n_2\). \(H_0\): both samples come from the same distribution. Works with discrete data; \(U\) is approximately normal for large samples.

Review: Module 6

Test Statistics · Mann-Whitney, line 2

\[ \text{Test statistic is smaller of } U_1 \text{ and } U_2 \]
What it means

Read it as: report \(U = \min(U_1, U_2)\).

Symbols: \(U_1, U_2\) from the previous card.

On the exam: a small \(U\) (far below \(n_1n_2/2\)) means one group's values sit mostly below the other's, which is evidence against \(H_0\).

Review: Module 6

Test Statistics · Mann-Whitney, line 3

\[ R_i:\ \text{sum of all ranks in group } i \]
What it means

Read it as: after ranking everything together, add up the ranks that belong to group \(i\).

Symbols: \(R_1\), \(R_2\).

On the exam: ties are not covered in class. If you meet one, give each tied value the average of the ranks they share, and say so.

Review: Module 6

Test Statistics · Mann-Whitney, line 4

\[ \text{range of ranks: 1 for smallest datum, } n_1 + n_2 \text{ for largest} \]
What it means

Read it as: rank the pooled data from 1 (smallest) up to \(n_1 + n_2\) (largest).

Symbols: \(n_1 + n_2\) = total number of data.

On the exam: rank smallest = 1. Ranking in the other direction swaps \(U_1\) and \(U_2\) but does not change \(\min(U_1, U_2)\).

Review: Module 6

Test Statistics · unpaired t-test

\[ T = (\overline X - \overline Y)/S_\Delta \sim t(\nu) \]
What it means

Read it as: Welch's statistic is the difference in sample means divided by its standard error, and it follows a t distribution with \(\nu\) degrees of freedom.

Symbols: \(\overline X, \overline Y\) = means of two independent groups. \(S_\Delta\) = standard error of the difference. \(\nu\) = Welch degrees of freedom (usually not a whole number).

On the exam: two separate groups of different units. \(H_0: \mu_X = \mu_Y\). Assumptions: every datum independent of all others, and the data in each group normal. Welch does NOT assume equal variances. 7M worksheet: \(T = (0.5 - 1.1)/3 = -0.2\).

Review: Module 7

Test Statistics · unpaired, S_Δ line

\[ S_\Delta = \sqrt{S_X^2/n_X + S_Y^2/n_Y} \]
What it means

Read it as: add each group's squared standard error, then take the square root.

Symbols: \(S_X, S_Y\) = sample SDs; \(n_X, n_Y\) = group sizes.

On the exam: square the SDs first. If the problem gives variances \(S^2\), do not square again.

Review: Module 7

Test Statistics · unpaired, ν line

\[ \nu = \frac{S_\Delta^4}{S_X^4/(n_X^2(n_X-1)) + S_Y^4/(n_Y^2(n_Y-1))} \]
What it means

Read it as: the Welch degrees of freedom are \(S_\Delta^4\) divided by the sum of each group's \(S^4/(n^2(n-1))\).

Symbols: \(S_\Delta^4 = (S_\Delta^2)^2\). Note \(n_X^2\) is squared.

On the exam: 7M example: \(\nu = 6.942\). Easy form: with \(a = S_X^2/n_X\) and \(b = S_Y^2/n_Y\), \(\nu = (a+b)^2/[a^2/(n_X-1) + b^2/(n_Y-1)]\).

Review: Module 7

Test Statistics · paired t-test

\[ T = \overline D/(S_D/\sqrt n) \sim t(n-1) \]
What it means

Read it as: a one-sample t test on the differences: mean difference over its standard error, with \(n - 1\) degrees of freedom.

Symbols: \(\overline D\) = mean of the differences, \(S_D\) = SD of the differences, \(n\) = number of PAIRS.

On the exam: use it when the same unit is measured twice (before and after, or under both treatments). The design decides, not the numbers. \(H_0: \mu_D = 0\). 7M example: \(D = \{0.3, 0.9, 0.4, 0.9, 0.5\}\), \(T = 4.743\), \(t(4)\).

Review: Module 7

Test Statistics · paired, D_i

\[ D_i = X_i - Y_i \]
What it means

Read it as: for each unit, subtract its second measurement from its first.

Symbols: \(X_i, Y_i\) = the two measurements on unit \(i\).

On the exam: compute every \(D_i\) first, then \(\overline D\) and \(S_D\) from the \(D\)'s alone. Keep the sign.

Review: Module 7

Test Statistics · chi-square G.o.F.

\[ X^2 = \sum_{i=1}^m\Big[\frac{(X_i - kp_{i,0})^2}{kp_{i,0}}\Big] \sim \chi^2(m-1) \]
What it means

Read it as: for each category, (observed minus expected) squared over expected; add them up; compare with a chi-square with \(m - 1\) degrees of freedom.

Symbols: \(m\) = number of categories. \(X_i\) = observed count in category \(i\). \(k\) = TOTAL count (sum of all \(X_i\)), not the number of categories. \(p_{i,0}\) = null probability of category \(i\). \(kp_{i,0}\) = expected count.

On the exam: a multinomial experiment with specified probabilities. It approximates the exact multinomial test and is valid only if every expected count is at least 5. The p-value is always upper-tailed. HW6 Q1: \(X^2 = 54/8 = 6.75\), df 3.

Review: Module 8

Test Statistics · chi-square independence/homogeneity

\[ X^2 = \sum_{i=1}^r\sum_{j=1}^c\Big[\frac{(X_{ij} - E_{ij})^2}{E_{ij}}\Big] \sim \chi^2((r-1)(c-1)) \]
What it means

Read it as: the same observed-minus-expected sum, over every cell of a contingency table, with \((r-1)(c-1)\) degrees of freedom.

Symbols: \(r, c\) = number of rows and columns. \(X_{ij}\) = observed count in row \(i\), column \(j\). \(E_{ij}\) = expected count.

On the exam: same math for both. Homogeneity: group sizes fixed by design (one margin fixed), each subject asked one question. Independence: one sample, each subject answers two questions, no margin fixed. For a 2×2 table with one margin fixed, use Barnard's exact test.

Review: Module 8

Test Statistics · E_ij line, first part

\[ E_{ij} = r_ic_j/k \]
What it means

Read it as: expected count in a cell = row total times column total divided by grand total.

Symbols: \(r_i\) = row-\(i\) total, \(c_j\) = column-\(j\) total, \(k\) = grand total of the whole table.

On the exam: compute every cell. Check: the expected counts in each row add to that row's total. HW6 Q2: \(E_{11} = 64 \cdot 35/93 = 24.086\).

Review: Module 8

Test Statistics · E_ij line, second part

\[ r_i,\ c_j:\ \text{sum of data in row } i,\ \text{column } j \]
What it means

Read it as: \(r_i\) and \(c_j\) are the margin totals of the table.

Symbols: "margins" = the totals written along the right side and bottom.

On the exam: a margin "fixed by design" (chosen before data collection) is the clue for homogeneity, and for Barnard if the table is 2×2.

Review: Module 8

Confidence Intervals

Confidence Intervals · line 1

\[ [\widehat\theta_L, \widehat\theta_U]:\ \mathbb{P}(\widehat\theta_L \le \theta \le \widehat\theta_U) = 1 - \alpha \]
What it means

Read it as: the interval is built so that, over many repeated experiments, a fraction \(1 - \alpha\) of the intervals contain the true \(\theta\).

Symbols: \(\widehat\theta_L, \widehat\theta_U\) = lower and upper bounds (random, computed from data). \(\theta\) = fixed, unknown parameter.

On the exam: interpretation. Correct: "95% of intervals built this way contain \(\theta\)." Wrong: "there is a 95% chance \(\theta\) is in this interval." A computed interval either contains \(\theta\) or it does not.

Review: Module 5

Confidence Intervals · line 2

\[ \text{One-sided bounds: } [\widehat\theta_L, \infty) \text{ or } (-\infty, \widehat\theta_U] \]
What it means

Read it as: a one-sided interval gives only a lower bound or only an upper bound.

Symbols: the other end is open to infinity (or to the edge of the parameter space, like 0 for \(\theta > 0\)).

On the exam: "how large might \(\theta\) be?" is a one-sided upper bound. It uses \(q = \alpha\), not \(\alpha/2\).

Review: Module 5

Confidence Intervals · line 3

\[ \text{Confidence coefficient: } 1 - \alpha \]
What it means

Read it as: the stated long-run capture rate of the interval procedure.

Symbols: \(\alpha\) set by you; \(\alpha = 0.05\) means a "95% interval".

On the exam: coverage is the ACTUAL long-run fraction of intervals that contain \(\theta\). It equals \(1 - \alpha\) exactly only for an exact, continuous sampling distribution. For discrete ones (binomial, Poisson) it is not exact.

Review: Module 5

Confidence Intervals · line 4

\[ \text{Solve } F_Y(y_{\rm obs};\theta) - q = 0 \text{ for } \theta \]
What it means

Read it as: hold the observed statistic fixed, and find the parameter value that puts \(y_{\rm obs}\) at quantile \(q\) of the sampling distribution.

Symbols: \(Y\) = statistic, \(y_{\rm obs}\) = its observed value, \(\theta\) = the unknown you solve for, \(q\) from the table on the next card.

On the exam: exponential, one datum: \(1 - e^{-y_{\rm obs}/\theta} = q\) gives \(\theta = -y_{\rm obs}/\ln(1-q)\). 7F: \(x_{\rm obs} = 4\), 95%: \([1.08, 157.99]\). Not on the sheet: for discrete statistics, the lower-bound equation uses \(y_{\rm obs} - 1\).

Review: Module 5

Confidence Intervals · q table

\[ \begin{array}{l|c|c} \text{Interval} & q \text{ lower} & q \text{ upper} \\ \hline \text{one-sided lower} & 1-\alpha & - \\ \text{one-sided upper} & - & \alpha \\ \text{two-sided} & 1-\alpha/2 & \alpha/2 \end{array} \]
What it means

Read it as: the lower bound always uses the big \(q\), the upper bound the small \(q\).

Why: at the lower bound \(\theta\) is so small that \(y_{\rm obs}\) looks unusually large, so it sits high in the c.d.f. (\(F = 1 - \alpha/2\)). At the upper bound \(\theta\) is so large that \(y_{\rm obs}\) looks unusually small (\(F = \alpha/2\)). This assumes bigger \(\theta\) means bigger data, which holds for the exam (the instructor said no reversing is needed there).

On the exam: 95% two-sided: \(q = 0.975\) (lower), \(0.025\) (upper). 90% one-sided upper: \(q = 0.10\) (7F worksheet: \(\widehat\theta_U = -1/\ln 0.9 = 9.49\)).

Review: Module 5

Hypothesis Testing

Hypothesis Testing · line 1

\[ \text{Type I error: } \alpha = \mathbb{P}_{\theta_0}(\text{reject null}) \]
What it means

Read it as: the level \(\alpha\) is the probability of rejecting \(H_0\) when \(H_0\) is actually true.

Symbols: \(\theta_0\) = the null value; the subscript means "computed assuming \(\theta = \theta_0\)". \(\alpha\) is chosen before the data, typically 0.05.

On the exam: a definition you must be able to write. In quality control a false alarm is a Type I error (probability 0.0027 for 3σ limits).

Review: Module 3

Hypothesis Testing · line 2

\[ \text{Type II error: } \beta = \mathbb{P}_{\theta'}(\text{fail to reject null};\ \alpha) \]
What it means

Read it as: \(\beta\) is the probability of failing to reject \(H_0\) when the true value is a specific alternative \(\theta'\), for a test run at level \(\alpha\).

Symbols: \(\theta'\) = one specific alternative value. Power at \(\theta'\) \(= 1 - \beta\).

On the exam: lowering \(\alpha\) raises \(\beta\) (for fixed \(n\)). In quality control a failure to detect is a Type II error.

Review: Module 3, Module 4

Hypothesis Testing · lines 3 and 4

Rejection region: set of values of test statistic for which we would reject the null; defined assuming the null hypothesis is true

What it means

Read it as: the rejection region is the set of test-statistic values that lead to "reject \(H_0\)", and it is fixed using the null distribution before any data are seen.

Symbols: its edge is \(y_{\rm RR}\).

On the exam: \(y_{\rm RR}\) never depends on \(y_{\rm obs}\). Rejecting because \(y_{\rm obs}\) is in the region and rejecting because \(p < \alpha\) always agree.

Review: Module 3, Module 4

Hypothesis Testing · line 5

\[ \text{Solve } F_Y(y_{\rm RR};\theta_0) - q = 0 \text{ for } y_{\rm RR} \]
What it means

Read it as: hold the parameter at its null value and find the statistic value at quantile \(q\). That value is the rejection-region boundary.

Symbols: \(\theta_0\) = null value, \(y_{\rm RR}\) = boundary, \(q = \alpha\), \(1 - \alpha\), or \(\alpha/2\) and \(1-\alpha/2\) (from the power table).

On the exam: the same equation as the CI line, with a different unknown. Tests fix \(\theta\) and solve for \(y\). CIs fix \(y\) and solve for \(\theta\).

Review: Module 2, Module 4

Hypothesis Testing · p-value table

\[ \begin{array}{l|l} \text{Test} & p\text{-value} \\ \hline \text{lower} & F_Y(y_{\rm obs};\theta_0) \\ \text{upper} & 1 - F_Y(y_{\rm obs};\theta_0) \\ \text{two} & 2\min[F_Y(y_{\rm obs};\theta_0),\, 1 - F_Y(y_{\rm obs};\theta_0)] \end{array} \]
What it means

Read it as: the p-value is the null probability of a result as extreme as or more extreme than \(y_{\rm obs}\): the left tail for a lower-tailed test, the right tail for upper, twice the smaller tail for two-tailed.

Symbols: \(F_Y(\cdot;\theta_0)\) = c.d.f. of the statistic under \(H_0\).

On the exam: pick the row from \(H_a\) (\(<\), \(>\), \(\ne\)). If \(p < \alpha\), reject \(H_0\); otherwise fail to reject. 5F: one-tailed \(p = 0.028\) rejects; two-tailed \(0.056\) does not. Two-tailed tests are more conservative.

Review: Module 4

Hypothesis Testing · power table

\[ \begin{array}{l|l|l} \text{Test} & \text{RR boundary} & \text{Power} \\ \hline \text{lower} & y_{\rm RR} = F_Y^{-1}(\alpha;\theta_0) & F_Y(y_{\rm RR};\theta) \\ \text{upper} & y_{\rm RR} = F_Y^{-1}(1-\alpha;\theta_0) & 1 - F_Y(y_{\rm RR};\theta) \\ \text{two} & y_{\rm RR,lo} = F_Y^{-1}(\alpha/2;\theta_0) & F_Y(y_{\rm RR,lo};\theta)\,+ \\ & y_{\rm RR,hi} = F_Y^{-1}(1-\alpha/2;\theta_0) & 1 - F_Y(y_{\rm RR,hi};\theta) \end{array} \]
What it means

Read it as: step 1, middle column: use \(\alpha\) and \(\theta_0\) to find the boundary. Step 2, right column: use that boundary and the true \(\theta\) to find the probability of landing in the rejection region.

Symbols: \(\theta_0\) = null value, \(\theta\) = the true or alternative value you compute power at.

On the exam: power is the probability of rejecting \(H_0\) at a given \(\theta\). It equals \(\alpha\) at \(\theta = \theta_0\). It rises with \(n\), with distance \(|\theta - \theta_0|\), and with \(\alpha\); it falls as \(\sigma\) rises.

Review: Module 4

Power table · lower-tailed row

\[ y_{\rm RR} = F_Y^{-1}(\alpha;\theta_0), \quad \text{power} = F_Y(y_{\rm RR};\theta) \]
What it means

Read it as: the boundary cuts off the bottom \(\alpha\) of the null distribution; power is the area below that same boundary under the true distribution.

Symbols: \(H_a: \theta < \theta_0\).

On the exam: exponential, \(\theta_0 = 1\), \(\alpha = 0.05\): \(y_{\rm RR} = -\ln 0.95 = 0.0513\). Power at \(\theta = 0.5\): \(1 - e^{-0.0513/0.5} = 0.0975\).

Review: Module 4

Power table · upper-tailed row

\[ y_{\rm RR} = F_Y^{-1}(1-\alpha;\theta_0), \quad \text{power} = 1 - F_Y(y_{\rm RR};\theta) \]
What it means

Read it as: the boundary cuts off the top \(\alpha\) of the null distribution; power is the area above it under the true distribution.

Symbols: \(H_a: \theta > \theta_0\).

On the exam: exponential, \(\theta_0 = 1\): \(y_{\rm RR} = 2.996\); power at \(\theta = 2\) is \(e^{-2.996/2} = 0.224\). 6M normal example: \(y_{\rm RR} = 146.58\), power at \(\mu = 144\) is 0.259. HW5 Q2: \(x_{\rm RR} = 0.95\), power at \(\theta = 2\) is \(1 - 0.95^2 = 0.0975\).

Review: Module 4

Power table · two-tailed row

\[ \begin{aligned} &y_{\rm RR,lo} = F_Y^{-1}(\alpha/2;\theta_0),\ y_{\rm RR,hi} = F_Y^{-1}(1-\alpha/2;\theta_0) \\ &\text{power} = F_Y(y_{\rm RR,lo};\theta) + 1 - F_Y(y_{\rm RR,hi};\theta) \end{aligned} \]
What it means

Read it as: split \(\alpha\) into two tails of \(\alpha/2\); power is the true probability of landing in either tail.

Symbols: \(H_a: \theta \ne \theta_0\).

On the exam: exponential, \(\theta_0 = 1\), \(\alpha = 0.05\): boundaries \(0.0253\) and \(3.689\). Power at \(\theta = 2\): \(0.0126 + 0.1581 = 0.171\), lower than the one-sided 0.224 because each tail is thinner.

Review: Module 4

Quality Control

Typo and two hidden conventions in this block

The CUSUM rule should read \(\mathrm{SL}_i < -h\sigma\) (the card below shows the corrected line). The CUSUM \(k\) is \(1/2\) by convention and is not printed. In the \(\overline X\) chart and CUSUM, \(\sigma\) is the SD of a subgroup MEAN, \(\overline S/(c_4\sqrt n)\). In \(C_p\) and \(C_{pk}\), \(\sigma\) is the SD of an individual item, \(\overline S/c_4\).

Quality Control · c₄

\[ c_4 = \Gamma(n/2)\sqrt{2/(n-1)}\,/\,\Gamma((n-1)/2) \]
What it means

Read it as: \(c_4\) is a correction factor: on average \(S\) underestimates \(\sigma\), with \(\mathbb{E}(S) = c_4\sigma\), so \(\overline S/c_4\) is unbiased.

Symbols: \(n\) = subgroup size (data per subgroup, not the number of subgroups). \(\Gamma\) = gamma function (next cards).

On the exam: compute it by hand from the Gamma lines. Values: \(n = 2\): 0.7979; 3: 0.8862; 4: 0.9213; 5: 0.9400; 6: 0.9515.

Review: Module 9

Quality Control · Γ line 1

\[ \text{If } n \text{ integer, } \Gamma(n) = (n-1)! \]
What it means

Read it as: at whole numbers, the gamma function is a factorial shifted by one.

Symbols: \(\Gamma(1) = 0! = 1\), \(\Gamma(2) = 1\), \(\Gamma(3) = 2\), \(\Gamma(4) = 6\).

On the exam: plugging into \(c_4\). Here "\(n\)" is just the input to \(\Gamma\), which may be \(n/2\) or \((n-1)/2\).

Review: Module 9

Quality Control · Γ line 2

\[ \Gamma(1/2) = \sqrt\pi \quad \Gamma(3/2) = \sqrt\pi/2 \quad \Gamma(5/2) = 3\sqrt\pi/4 \]
What it means

Read it as: the gamma function at the half-integers you need.

Symbols: \(\sqrt\pi = 1.7725\). Beyond these, use \(\Gamma(x+1) = x\Gamma(x)\), so \(\Gamma(7/2) = 15\sqrt\pi/8\).

On the exam: \(n = 4\): \(c_4 = \Gamma(2)\sqrt{2/3}/\Gamma(3/2) = 0.8165/0.8862 = 0.9213\).

Review: Module 9

Quality Control · X̄-chart

\[ (\mathrm{LCL}, \mathrm{UCL}) = \widehat\mu \pm 3\widehat\sigma = \overline{\overline X} \pm 3\overline S/(c_4\sqrt n) \]
What it means

Read it as: the control limits for subgroup means sit 3 estimated standard errors above and below the grand mean.

Symbols: \(\overline{\overline X}\) = grand mean (average of the subgroup means), the center line. \(\overline S\) = average of the subgroup SDs. \(n\) = subgroup size. Here \(\widehat\sigma = \overline S/(c_4\sqrt n)\) is the SD of \(\overline X\).

On the exam: 8W worksheet: \(\overline{\overline X} = 9\), \(\overline S = 4/3\), \(n = 3\): \((6.39, 11.61)\). 3σ limits mean a false-alarm rate \(\alpha = 0.0027\).

Review: Module 9

Quality Control · S-chart

\[ (\mathrm{LCL}, \mathrm{UCL}) = (B_3\overline S,\ B_4\overline S) \]
What it means

Read it as: the S chart's limits are the average subgroup SD multiplied by two constants.

Symbols: \(\overline S\) = center line. \(B_3, B_4\) from the next card.

On the exam: for \(n \le 5\), \(B_3 = 0\), so LCL = 0. A point below the LCL means unusually small variation: good, find the cause and maybe copy it.

Review: Module 9

Quality Control · B₃, B₄

\[ \begin{aligned} B_3 &= \max\big(0,\ 1 - (3/c_4)\sqrt{1 - c_4^2}\big) \\ B_4 &= 1 + (3/c_4)\sqrt{1 - c_4^2} \end{aligned} \]
What it means

Read it as: 1 minus or plus three times the relative standard error of \(S\); the lower one is floored at 0.

Symbols: \(c_4\) for your subgroup size.

On the exam: compute \((3/c_4)\sqrt{1 - c_4^2}\) once and use it twice. \(n = 3\): 1.568, so \(B_3 = 0\), \(B_4 = 2.568\). \(n = 4\): 1.266, so \(B_3 = 0\), \(B_4 = 2.266\).

Review: Module 9

Quality Control · p-chart

\[ (\mathrm{LCL}, \mathrm{UCL}) = \overline p \pm 3\sqrt{\overline p(1 - \overline p)/k} \]
What it means

Read it as: limits for the proportion defective sit 3 binomial standard errors around the average proportion.

Symbols: \(k\) = number of items inspected in EACH sample (here \(k\) means sample size). \(m\) = number of samples. \(\overline p\) = total defectives \(/(mk)\).

On the exam: 9M: \(m = 30\), \(k = 500\), 763 defectives: \(\overline p = 0.051\), limits \((0.021, 0.080)\). Attribute data (defective or not). If the LCL comes out negative, report 0, since a proportion cannot be negative.

Review: Module 9

Quality Control · c-chart

\[ (\mathrm{LCL}, \mathrm{UCL}) = \overline c \pm 3\sqrt{\overline c} \]
What it means

Read it as: limits for a count of flaws per unit sit 3 Poisson standard deviations around the average count.

Symbols: \(\overline c\) = total flaws divided by the number of units \(m\).

On the exam: 9M: 491 flaws on 40 units: \(\overline c = 12.275\), limits \((1.764, 22.786)\). Use the c chart for counts of flaws, the p chart for proportions of defective items.

Review: Module 9

Quality Control · CUSUM, SH line

\[ \mathrm{SH}_i = \max(0,\ \overline X_i - \mu - k\sigma + \mathrm{SH}_{i-1}),\ \ \mathrm{SH}_0 = 0 \]
What it means

Read it as: the high-side sum adds up how far each subgroup mean sits above \(\mu + k\sigma\), and resets to 0 whenever it would go negative.

Symbols: \(\overline X_i\) = subgroup mean \(i\). \(\mu\) estimated by \(\overline{\overline X}\). \(\sigma\) = SD of \(\overline X\), \(\overline S/(c_4\sqrt n)\). \(k = 1/2\) by convention (slack, here \(k\) means a multiple of \(\sigma\)).

On the exam: fill a table row by row. It catches small sustained upward shifts that never cross the 3σ limits.

Review: Module 10

Quality Control · CUSUM, SL line

\[ \mathrm{SL}_i = \min(0,\ \overline X_i - \mu + k\sigma + \mathrm{SL}_{i-1}),\ \ \mathrm{SL}_0 = 0 \]
What it means

Read it as: the low-side sum adds up how far each mean sits below \(\mu - k\sigma\), and resets to 0 whenever it would go positive.

Symbols: note the PLUS \(k\sigma\) here (the instructor circled it). SL is always \(\le 0\).

On the exam: 8F: \(\overline X_1 = 2.308\), \(\mu = 2.650\), \(k\sigma = 0.0583\): \(\mathrm{SL}_1 = -0.284\), \(\mathrm{SL}_2 = -0.378\).

Review: Module 10

Quality Control · CUSUM rule (corrected)

\[ \text{out of control if } \mathrm{SH}_i > h\sigma \text{ or } \mathrm{SL}_i < -h\sigma \quad (h = 4) \]
What it means

Typo fix: the sheet prints \(\mathrm{SL}_i < h\sigma\). Since SL is never positive, that would flag every point. The lecture writes \(-h\sigma\).

Read it as: flag the first subgroup where either running sum gets more than \(h\) standard errors away from 0.

Symbols: \(h = 4\) on the sheet (the lecture says 4 or 5). \(\sigma\) = SD of \(\overline X\).

On the exam: answer "out of control at subgroup \(i\)" for the first \(i\) that crosses.

Review: Module 10

Quality Control · actual capability

\[ C_{pk} = \min\Big(\frac{\mu - \mathrm{LSL}}{3\sigma},\ \frac{\mathrm{USL} - \mu}{3\sigma}\Big) \]
What it means

Read it as: the realized capability is the distance from the mean to the NEARER spec limit, measured in units of \(3\sigma\).

Symbols: LSL, USL = lower and upper specification limits (set by the customer, for individual items). \(\mu\) estimated by \(\overline{\overline X}\). \(\sigma\) = SD of an individual item, \(\overline S/c_4\) (no \(\sqrt n\)).

On the exam: \(C_{pk} > 1\) means minimally capable. 9M: specs 9 to 11, \(\overline{\overline X} = 9.7\), \(\overline S = 0.3\), \(n = 6\): \(C_{pk} = 0.740\), not minimally capable. In control does not mean capable.

Review: Module 10

Quality Control · potential capability

\[ C_p = \frac{\mathrm{USL} - \mathrm{LSL}}{6\sigma} \]
What it means

Read it as: the potential capability is the spec width divided by the process width \(6\sigma\): what \(C_{pk}\) would be if the mean sat exactly in the middle of the specs.

Symbols: same \(\sigma\) as \(C_{pk}\) (per item).

On the exam: \(C_p > 2\) means six-sigma quality. Always \(C_{pk} \le C_p\), equal only when centered. 9M: specs 8.7 to 9.3, \(\sigma = 0.075\): \(C_p = 4/3\), not six sigma.

Review: Module 10

The Western Electric Rules

Western Electric box · whole box

Any one of the following conditions is evidence that a process is out of control:

1. Any point plotting outside the 3σ control limits.

2. Two out of three consecutive points plotting above the upper 2σ limit, or two out of three consecutive points plotting below the lower 2σ limit.

3. Four out of five consecutive points plotting above the upper 1σ limit, or four out of five consecutive points plotting below the lower 1σ limit.

4. Eight consecutive points plotting on the same side of the center line.

What it means

Read it as: four pattern checks; if any one fires, the process is out of control.

Symbols: σ here is the SD of the plotted statistic (for an \(\overline X\) chart, \(\overline S/(c_4\sqrt n)\)). The 3σ lines are the control limits; the 1σ and 2σ lines split the chart into zones.

On the exam: "when is it first out of control?" Walk through the points in time order. For each rule, find the point that COMPLETES the pattern. The answer is the earliest of those (8F: rule 2 completes at point 3, so out of control at point 3).

Review: Module 10

Western Electric box · rule 1

Any point plotting outside the 3σ control limits.

What it means

Read it as: one point beyond LCL or UCL is enough.

On the exam: this is the plain control-chart rule. With 3σ limits an in-control process trips it with probability 0.0027 per point, so the in-control ARL is about 370.

Review: Module 10

Western Electric box · rule 2

Two out of three consecutive points plotting above the upper 2σ limit, or two out of three consecutive points plotting below the lower 2σ limit.

What it means

Read it as: in any window of 3 consecutive points, 2 are beyond the 2σ line on the SAME side.

On the exam: one point above \(+2\sigma\) and one below \(-2\sigma\) do not count together. The signal is called at the point that completes the window.

Review: Module 10

Western Electric box · rule 3

Four out of five consecutive points plotting above the upper 1σ limit, or four out of five consecutive points plotting below the lower 1σ limit.

What it means

Read it as: in any window of 5 consecutive points, 4 are beyond the 1σ line on the same side.

On the exam: the instructor calls it at the 5th point of the window, even if 4 qualifying points already appeared (8F example: points 1 to 4 were all low, called at point 5).

Review: Module 10

Western Electric box · rule 4

Eight consecutive points plotting on the same side of the center line.

What it means

Read it as: eight in a row above the center line, or eight in a row below it.

On the exam: count the run; the 8th point is the signal. This rule catches small shifts that never reach the outer zones.

Review: Module 10

If you see this on the exam

  • "Find the p-value" → p-value table, row chosen by \(H_a\); exponential c.d.f. \(1 - e^{-x/\theta}\) (not printed).
  • "Rejection-region boundary" or "power" → power table: middle column with \(\theta_0\), right column with the true \(\theta\).
  • "Confidence interval" → CI block: solve \(F_Y(y_{\rm obs};\theta) = q\) for \(\theta\); big \(q\) gives the lower bound.
  • "Table of e.c.d.f. and null c.d.f. values" → KS line: largest \(|F_{X_n} - F_X|\).
  • "Two groups, rank" → Mann-Whitney lines; "two groups, means" → unpaired t (Welch); "same unit twice" → paired t.
  • "Counts in categories with claimed probabilities" → chi-square G.o.F., \(k\) = total count. "Table of counts" → \(E_{ij} = r_ic_j/k\).
  • "Subgroups of size n with means and SDs" → \(c_4\), \(\overline X\) chart, S chart. "Proportion defective" → p chart. "Flaws per unit" → c chart.
  • "Running sums" → CUSUM with \(k = 1/2\), flag at \(\pm h\sigma\) (fix the SL sign). "Specs LSL, USL" → \(C_{pk}\) (> 1 minimally capable), \(C_p\) (> 2 six sigma), per-item \(\sigma = \overline S/c_4\).

Module 12 · Practice exam · about 50 min

A full practice exam, then the answers

Twenty-one questions, 100 points, every bullet of the study guide. A little more than half the points are conceptual, matching what the instructor said to expect.

Exam conditions

Set a timer for 50 minutes. Use only the formula sheet, paper, a pencil and a calculator. Do not open any solution until the timer ends. Then grade yourself with the point values and the scoring note at the bottom.

Where these questions come from

The instructor's Exam 2 "practice questions" handout is a list of pointers to textbook exercises. It contains no past exam questions, so every question below is original. They copy the style of HW5 to HW7, which the instructor said are similar to exam questions, and follow the handout's hints: KS questions look like the homework, Barnard's test is the 2×2 test with one margin fixed, ARL questions are qualitative, and the exam needs no "reversed" q table.

Part A · Hypothesis testing (22 pts)

6 pts

Q1. Write a one-sentence definition of each: (a) the p-value; (b) the level of a test, \(\alpha\) (the Type I error rate); (c) the power of a test.

Solution
  1. (a) p-value. The probability, assuming \(H_0\) is true, of observing a test statistic value as extreme as or more extreme than the one actually observed.
  2. (b) Level \(\alpha\). The probability of rejecting \(H_0\) when \(H_0\) is actually true, \(\alpha = \mathbb{P}_{\theta_0}(\text{reject null})\). You choose it before seeing data, typically 0.05.
  3. (c) Power. The probability of rejecting \(H_0\), as a function of the true parameter value \(\theta\). At a specific alternative \(\theta'\) it equals \(1 - \beta\); at \(\theta = \theta_0\) it equals \(\alpha\).

Grading: 2 pts each. Lose 1 pt in (a) for leaving out "assuming \(H_0\) is true" or "as or more extreme", and in (b) for leaving out "when \(H_0\) is true".

Review: Module 3

3 pts

Q2. An engineer tests \(H_0: \mu = 50\) MPa against \(H_a: \mu > 50\) MPa for the mean yield strength of a new alloy, at \(\alpha = 0.05\).

(a) The p-value is 0.031. State the conclusion in one sentence, in context.

(b) On a second batch a teammate gets p = 0.21 and writes: "We accept \(H_0\), so the mean strength is proven to be 50 MPa." Rewrite the sentence correctly.

Solution
  1. (a) Since \(p = 0.031 < \alpha = 0.05\), we reject \(H_0\) and conclude there is evidence that the mean yield strength is greater than 50 MPa.
  2. (b) Since \(p = 0.21 \ge 0.05\), we fail to reject \(H_0\) and conclude that a mean strength of 50 MPa is plausible. The only two conclusions are "reject" and "fail to reject". A test never "accepts" \(H_0\) and never proves anything.

Grading: (a) 1 pt; (b) 2 pts (1 for "fail to reject", 1 for removing "proven").

Review: Module 3

4 pts

Q3. You run an upper-tailed test of \(H_0: \mu = \mu_0\) against \(H_a: \mu > \mu_0\) on normal data with known \(\sigma\), using the statistic \(\overline X\) at a fixed level \(\alpha\). For a fixed alternative \(\mu' > \mu_0\), does the power increase, decrease, or stay the same when:

(a) the sample size goes from \(n = 25\) to \(n = 100\)? (b) \(\mu'\) moves farther above \(\mu_0\)? (c) \(\sigma\) doubles? (d) What is the power if the true mean equals \(\mu_0\)?

Solution
  1. (a) Increases. \(\overline X \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu, \sigma^2/n)\), so its spread \(\sigma/\sqrt n\) shrinks. The null and alternative sampling distributions overlap less, so more of the alternative falls past \(y_{\rm RR}\).
  2. (b) Increases. The boundary \(y_{\rm RR} = F^{-1}(1-\alpha;\mu_0)\) does not move, and the alternative distribution slides further past it.
  3. (c) Decreases. Bigger \(\sigma\) widens both sampling distributions, so they are harder to tell apart. (In the 6M weights example, power at \(\mu = 144\) fell from 0.259 to 0.126 when \(\sigma\) went from 20 to 40.)
  4. (d) \(\alpha\). When \(\theta = \theta_0\), the probability of rejecting is the Type I error rate, whatever \(n\) is.

1 pt each.

Review: Module 4

9 pts

Q4. The lifetime \(X\) (in thousands of hours) of a pump seal is exponential with c.d.f. \(F_X(x;\theta) = 1 - e^{-x/\theta}\) for \(x \ge 0\). You test ONE seal and observe \(x_{\rm obs} = 7\). Test \(H_0: \theta = 2\) against \(H_a: \theta > 2\) at \(\alpha = 0.05\).

(a) [3] Compute the p-value and state your conclusion.

(b) [2] Suppose the test had been planned as two-tailed (\(H_a: \theta \ne 2\)) before collecting data. What would the p-value and conclusion be?

(c) [2] Find the rejection-region boundary \(y_{\rm RR}\) for the upper-tailed test.

(d) [2] Find the power of the upper-tailed test if the true value is \(\theta = 6\). What is \(\beta\) there?

Solution
  1. (a) One datum, so \(Y = X\) and \(y_{\rm obs} = 7\). Upper-tailed row of the p-value table: \(p = 1 - F_Y(7; 2) = 1 - (1 - e^{-7/2}) = e^{-3.5} = 0.0302\). Since \(0.0302 < 0.05\), we reject \(H_0\) and conclude there is evidence that the mean seal lifetime exceeds 2 thousand hours.
  2. (b) Two-tailed row: \(F_Y(7;2) = 0.9698\) and \(1 - F_Y(7;2) = 0.0302\). \(p = 2 \times \min(0.9698, 0.0302) = 0.0604\). Since \(0.0604 > 0.05\), we fail to reject \(H_0\); \(\theta = 2\) is plausible. Same data, different conclusion: two-tailed tests are more conservative. You cannot switch tails after seeing the p-value (that is p-hacking).
  3. (c) Upper-tailed: \(y_{\rm RR} = F_Y^{-1}(1 - \alpha;\theta_0)\). Solve \(1 - e^{-y/2} = 0.95\): \(e^{-y/2} = 0.05\), so \(y_{\rm RR} = -2\ln(0.05) = 2\ln 20 = 5.99\). Reject if \(x \ge 5.99\). Check: \(7 > 5.99\), which agrees with (a).
  4. (d) Power \(= 1 - F_Y(y_{\rm RR};\theta) = e^{-5.99/6} = e^{-0.9986} = 0.368\). (Exactly, \(0.05^{2/6} = 0.05^{1/3}\).) \(\beta = 1 - 0.368 = 0.632\). If the true mean lifetime is 6, a one-seal test rejects \(H_0\) only about 37% of the time.

(a) p = 0.0302, reject. (b) p = 0.0604, fail to reject. (c) \(y_{\rm RR} = 5.99\). (d) power 0.368, \(\beta = 0.632\).

Review: Module 4

Part B · Distribution tests (13 pts)

4 pts

Q5. You test whether \(n = 5\) data come from an exponential distribution with \(\theta = 2\), using a one-sample KS test. The table gives the data, the empirical c.d.f. \(F_{X_n}(x)\) at each datum, and the null c.d.f. \(F_X(x) = 1 - e^{-x/2}\).

\(x\)0.30.81.12.95.0
\(F_{X_n}(x)\)0.20.40.60.81.0
\(F_X(x)\)0.1390.3300.4230.7650.918

(a) [3] Compute the observed KS statistic \(D_n\). (b) [1] Under \(H_0\), what distribution does \(\sqrt n D_n\) follow, and is its p-value computed by hand?

Solution
  1. (a) Compute \(|F_{X_n}(x) - F_X(x)|\) in each column and take the largest.
\(x\)0.30.81.12.95.0
\(|F_{X_n} - F_X|\)0.0610.0700.1770.0350.082
  1. Answer (a). \(D_n = 0.177\), at \(x = 1.1\). (Careful version: just before each jump the e.c.d.f. is one step lower, \((i-1)/n\). Those gaps are 0.139, 0.130, 0.023, 0.165, 0.118, all smaller than 0.177, so \(D_n = 0.177\) either way.)
  2. (b) \(\sqrt n D_n\) follows the Kolmogorov distribution. The p-value comes from software, not by hand.

\(D_n = 0.177\); Kolmogorov distribution; p-value by software.

Review: Module 6

3 pts

Q6. (a) Before running a t test, you want to check whether 12 measurements are plausibly normal. Should you use the KS test or the Shapiro-Wilk test? Why?

(b) A KS test of normality gives p = 0.42. A classmate writes "the data are normal." What is wrong with that?

(c) Of the KS, Shapiro-Wilk and Mann-Whitney U tests, which can properly be used with discrete data?

Solution
  1. (a) Shapiro-Wilk. It is built specifically to test normality and is more powerful for that job. KS is a weak test (low power: a low probability of rejecting a false null).
  2. (b) A large p-value means we fail to reject \(H_0\); normality is plausible, nothing is proven. And because KS has low power, failing to reject is especially weak evidence.
  3. (c) Only Mann-Whitney U. KS is limited to continuous distributions and handles ties badly; Shapiro-Wilk tests a continuous (normal) distribution.

1 pt each.

Review: Module 6

6 pts

Q7. Wear depth (μm) after a test cycle was measured on parts with an old coating (group A) and a new coating (group B). The parts are all different.

Group A (\(n_1 = 4\)): 3.1, 4.6, 2.8, 5.2    Group B (\(n_2 = 5\)): 1.9, 2.4, 3.5, 2.2, 1.7

(a) [1] State \(H_0\) and \(H_a\) for a Mann-Whitney U test. (b) [4] Compute \(R_A\), \(R_B\), \(U_A\), \(U_B\) and the test statistic \(U\). (c) [1] For large samples, what is the approximate distribution of \(U\)?

Solution
  1. (a) \(H_0\): the two samples come from the same distribution. \(H_a\): they do not (one group tends to have larger values).
  2. Rank the pooled data, 1 for the smallest up to \(n_1 + n_2 = 9\) for the largest:
Value1.71.92.22.42.83.13.54.65.2
GroupBBBBAABAA
Rank123456789
  1. Rank sums. \(R_A = 5 + 6 + 8 + 9 = 28\). \(R_B = 1 + 2 + 3 + 4 + 7 = 17\). Check: \(28 + 17 = 45 = 9 \cdot 10/2\).
  2. U values. \(n_1n_2 = 20\). \(U_A = 20 + 4 \cdot 5/2 - 28 = 20 + 10 - 28 = 2\). \(U_B = 20 + 5 \cdot 6/2 - 17 = 20 + 15 - 17 = 18\). Check: \(2 + 18 = 20 = n_1n_2\).
  3. Statistic. \(U = \min(2, 18) = 2\). Very small compared with \(n_1n_2/2 = 10\): the groups barely overlap (only 2 of the 20 A-B pairs have the A value below the B value).
  4. (c) Approximately normal (Gaussian) for large samples.

\(R_A = 28\), \(R_B = 17\), \(U_A = 2\), \(U_B = 18\), \(U = 2\).

Review: Module 6

Part C · Two-sample t tests (6 pts)

6 pts

Q8. (a) [2] For each study, should you use a paired or an unpaired t test? (i) Six welders each weld one joint with rod type A and one joint with rod type B, and the joint strengths are compared. (ii) Four shafts from supplier X and five shafts from supplier Y are measured for diameter.

(b) [3] For study (ii): \(\bar x = 12.0\), \(s_X = 2.0\), \(n_X = 4\); \(\bar y = 10.5\), \(s_Y = 1.0\), \(n_Y = 5\). Compute \(S_\Delta\), the Welch statistic \(T\), and \(\nu\).

(c) [1] Under \(H_0: \mu_X = \mu_Y\), what distribution does \(T\) follow? List the test's assumptions.

Solution
  1. (a)(i) Paired. Each welder produces both measurements, so the two strengths for one welder are related (not independent). Work with \(D_i\) = A minus B for each welder: \(T = \overline D/(S_D/\sqrt6) \sim t(5)\).
  2. (a)(ii) Unpaired (Welch). Different shafts from two independent groups; no shaft has a natural partner. The design decides, not the numbers.
  3. (b) \(S_\Delta\). \(S_X^2/n_X = 4/4 = 1\) and \(S_Y^2/n_Y = 1/5 = 0.2\). \(S_\Delta = \sqrt{1.2} = 1.095\).
  4. T. \(T = (12.0 - 10.5)/1.095 = 1.369\).
  5. \(\nu\). \(S_\Delta^4 = 1.2^2 = 1.44\). Denominator: \(S_X^4/(n_X^2(n_X-1)) = 16/(16 \cdot 3) = 0.3333\) and \(S_Y^4/(n_Y^2(n_Y-1)) = 1/(25 \cdot 4) = 0.01\). \(\nu = 1.44/0.3433 = 4.19\).
  6. (c) \(T \sim t(\nu)\) with \(\nu = 4.19\). Assumptions: every datum is independent of all others, and the data in each group are normal. Welch does not assume equal variances. (For reference, the two-tailed p-value is about 0.24, so you would fail to reject.)

(i) paired, (ii) unpaired; \(S_\Delta = 1.095\), \(T = 1.369\), \(\nu = 4.19\); \(T \sim t(4.19)\).

Review: Module 7

Part D · Categorical data (17 pts)

3 pts

Q9. (a) [2] Which of these are multinomial experiments? (i) Roll a die 60 times and count how often each face appears. (ii) Measure the diameter of 30 bearings. (iii) Inspect exactly 200 circuit boards and classify each as pass, rework or scrap. (iv) Keep testing parts until the 5th failure, recording the failure type each time.

(b) [1] The chi-square goodness-of-fit test approximates which exact test, and when is the approximation considered valid?

Solution
  1. (i) Yes. \(k = 60\) trials fixed in advance, \(m = 6\) outcomes, independent trials, constant probabilities.
  2. (ii) No. Continuous measurements, no categories.
  3. (iii) Yes. \(k = 200\) fixed, \(m = 3\) categories.
  4. (iv) No. The number of trials is not fixed in advance; it depends on when the 5th failure happens.
  5. (b) The exact multinomial test. The chi-square version is valid when every expected count \(kp_{i,0}\) is at least 5.

(i) and (iii). Exact multinomial test; all expected counts ≥ 5.

Review: Module 8

5 pts

Q10. A supplier claims its defects are 50% cosmetic, 30% dimensional and 20% electrical. You classify 80 defects and find 34 cosmetic, 30 dimensional and 16 electrical.

(a) [3] Compute the expected counts, the chi-square goodness-of-fit statistic \(X^2\), and its degrees of freedom. (b) [2] The p-value is 0.301. State your conclusion at \(\alpha = 0.05\).

Solution
  1. Identify. \(k = 80\) (the TOTAL count), \(m = 3\) categories, \(p_0 = (0.5, 0.3, 0.2)\).
  2. Expected counts. \(kp_{i,0} = 40, 24, 16\). All are at least 5, so the approximation is valid.
  3. Statistic. \(X^2 = \frac{(34-40)^2}{40} + \frac{(30-24)^2}{24} + \frac{(16-16)^2}{16} = 0.9 + 1.5 + 0 = 2.4\).
  4. df. \(m - 1 = 2\), so \(X^2 \sim \chi^2(2)\) under \(H_0\).
  5. (b) Since \(0.301 > 0.05\), we fail to reject \(H_0\) and conclude that the claimed defect mix is plausible.

Expected 40, 24, 16; \(X^2 = 2.4\), df = 2; fail to reject.

Review: Module 8

6 pts

Q11. A plant randomly selects 150 employees and asks each one two questions: which department they work in and which shift they prefer.

DayNightSwingTotal
Assembly40302090
Testing20301060
Total606030150

(a) [3] Compute every expected count and the chi-square statistic \(X^2\). (b) [1] Degrees of freedom? (c) [1] Is this a test of independence or of homogeneity? Why? (d) [1] The p-value is 0.125. State the conclusion at \(\alpha = 0.05\).

Solution
  1. Expected counts \(E_{ij} = r_ic_j/k\), with \(k = 150\): Assembly row \(90 \cdot 60/150 = 36\), 36, \(90 \cdot 30/150 = 18\). Testing row \(60 \cdot 60/150 = 24\), 24, 12. All at least 5.
CellObservedExpected\((O-E)^2/E\)
Assembly, Day403616/36 = 0.444
Assembly, Night303636/36 = 1.000
Assembly, Swing20184/18 = 0.222
Testing, Day202416/24 = 0.667
Testing, Night302436/24 = 1.500
Testing, Swing10124/12 = 0.333
  1. (a) \(X^2 = 0.444 + 1.000 + 0.222 + 0.667 + 1.500 + 0.333 = 4.17\).
  2. (b) \((r-1)(c-1) = (2-1)(3-1) = 2\).
  3. (c) Independence. One sample of 150, each person answers two questions, and neither the row totals nor the column totals were fixed in advance. If the plant had instead chosen, say, 75 Assembly and 75 Testing workers and asked only about shifts, it would be homogeneity.
  4. (d) Since \(0.125 > 0.05\), we fail to reject \(H_0\) and conclude it is plausible that department and shift preference are independent.

\(X^2 = 4.17\), df = 2, independence, fail to reject.

Review: Module 8

3 pts

Q12. Which test fits each design? (a) 50 parts from line 1 and 50 parts from line 2 are chosen in advance and each is classified pass or fail. (b) The same, but with three lines of 50 parts each. (c) Why is Fisher's exact test not the right exact test for (a)?

Solution
  1. (a) Barnard's exact test. A 2×2 table (2 lines × pass/fail) with one margin, the line sample sizes, fixed by design. It is exact, with no approximation. (A chi-square test of homogeneity would approximate it.)
  2. (b) Chi-square test of homogeneity. The table is 3×2, and Barnard's test is only for 2×2 tables. Group sizes are fixed, so homogeneity.
  3. (c) Fisher's test assumes BOTH margins are fixed in advance. Here only the line totals are fixed; the pass and fail totals are random.

1 pt each.

Review: Module 8

Part E · Confidence intervals (10 pts)

6 pts

Q13. You observe one datum \(x_{\rm obs} = 5\) from an exponential distribution with c.d.f. \(F_X(x;\theta) = 1 - e^{-x/\theta}\).

(a) [4] Construct a 90% two-sided confidence interval for \(\theta\). (b) [1] Construct a 95% one-sided lower bound for \(\theta\). (c) [1] Using (a), would a two-tailed test of \(H_0: \theta = 1\) at \(\alpha = 0.10\) reject?

Solution
  1. Identify. One datum, so \(Y = X\), \(y_{\rm obs} = 5\). 90% two-sided means \(\alpha = 0.10\): \(q = 1 - \alpha/2 = 0.95\) for the lower bound and \(q = \alpha/2 = 0.05\) for the upper bound.
  2. Lower bound. \(1 - e^{-5/\theta} = 0.95\), so \(e^{-5/\theta} = 0.05\), \(-5/\theta = \ln 0.05\), \(\widehat\theta_L = -5/\ln 0.05 = 5/\ln 20 = 1.67\).
  3. Upper bound. \(1 - e^{-5/\theta} = 0.05\), so \(e^{-5/\theta} = 0.95\), \(\widehat\theta_U = -5/\ln 0.95 = 97.5\).
  4. (a) The 90% CI is \([1.67, 97.5]\). One datum cannot pin \(\theta\) down, so the interval is wide.
  5. (b) One-sided lower: \(q = 1 - \alpha = 0.95\). That is the same equation as the lower bound in (a), so \(\widehat\theta_L = 1.67\); the bound is \([1.67, \infty)\).
  6. (c) Yes. \(\theta = 1\) lies outside \([1.67, 97.5]\), and a two-tailed level-0.10 test rejects exactly the \(\theta_0\) values outside the 90% interval. (Check: \(p = 2e^{-5} = 0.013 < 0.10\).)

(a) \([1.67, 97.5]\); (b) \([1.67, \infty)\); (c) reject.

Review: Module 5

4 pts

Q14. From a sample of bottles, a 95% confidence interval for the mean fill volume \(\mu\) is [498.2, 501.6] mL. True or false?

(a) There is a 95% probability that \(\mu\) is between 498.2 and 501.6 mL. (b) If the sampling were repeated many times and an interval built the same way each time, about 95% of those intervals would contain \(\mu\). (c) About 95% of individual bottles contain between 498.2 and 501.6 mL. (d) A two-tailed test of \(H_0: \mu = 500\) at \(\alpha = 0.05\) on the same data would fail to reject.

Solution
  1. (a) False. \(\mu\) is a fixed number. This computed interval either contains it or it does not. The 95% describes the procedure.
  2. (b) True. This is the correct interpretation (the confidence coefficient as a long-run capture rate).
  3. (c) False. The interval is about the mean \(\mu\), not about individual bottles.
  4. (d) True. 500 is inside the 95% interval, and a two-tailed level-0.05 test fails to reject exactly the values inside it.

F, T, F, T (1 pt each).

Review: Module 5

Part F · Quality control (32 pts)

3 pts

Q15. (a) [1] What is a rational subgroup? (b) [2] For each process, say whether the data are variables or attributes, and name the control chart(s) to use: (i) shaft diameter, 5 shafts measured every hour; (ii) number of scratches on each painted panel; (iii) number of defective chips among 400 tested each shift.

Solution
  1. (a) A small batch of items sampled together at one time (either items made close to the sampling time, or items drawn at random from production since the last sample). Each subgroup gives one plotted point.
  2. (i) Variables (continuous measurements): \(\overline X\) chart and S chart.
  3. (ii) Attributes (counts of flaws per unit): c chart.
  4. (iii) Attributes (proportion defective in a sample of \(k = 400\)): p chart.

1 pt for (a), 2 pts for (b) (lose 1 per wrong chart).

Review: Module 9

6 pts

Q16. A process is monitored with \(m = 20\) rational subgroups of size \(n = 4\). The summaries are \(\sum_{i=1}^{20}\overline X_i = 401.0\) and \(\sum_{i=1}^{20} S_i = 24.0\).

(a) [2] Compute \(c_4\) using the Gamma values on the formula sheet. (b) [2] Compute the 3σ limits for the \(\overline X\) chart. (c) [2] Compute the 3σ limits for the S chart.

Solution
  1. (a) \(c_4 = \Gamma(4/2)\sqrt{2/3}\,/\,\Gamma(3/2) = \Gamma(2) \cdot 0.8165 / (\sqrt\pi/2)\). \(\Gamma(2) = 1! = 1\) and \(\sqrt\pi/2 = 0.8862\), so \(c_4 = 0.8165/0.8862 = 0.9213\).
  2. Averages. \(\overline{\overline X} = 401.0/20 = 20.05\). \(\overline S = 24.0/20 = 1.2\).
  3. (b) Half-width \(3\overline S/(c_4\sqrt n) = 3(1.2)/(0.9213 \cdot 2) = 3.6/1.8426 = 1.954\). Limits \(20.05 \pm 1.954\): (18.10, 22.00).
  4. (c) \(\sqrt{1 - c_4^2} = \sqrt{1 - 0.8488} = 0.3888\). \((3/c_4)(0.3888) = 3.2563 \times 0.3888 = 1.266\). \(B_3 = \max(0, 1 - 1.266) = 0\), \(B_4 = 2.266\). Limits \((0 \times 1.2,\ 2.266 \times 1.2)\) = (0, 2.72).

\(c_4 = 0.9213\); \(\overline X\) chart (18.10, 22.00); S chart (0, 2.72).

Review: Module 9

5 pts

Q17. (a) [3] Each shift, 500 wafers are inspected; over 30 shifts, 600 wafers were defective in total. Compute the 3σ limits of the p chart. What does \(k\) stand for in the p-chart formula here? (b) [2] Over 25 painted panels, 400 flaws were counted in total. Compute the c-chart limits. A new panel has 31 flaws. Is the process in control?

Solution
  1. (a) \(k = 500\) = number of items inspected in each sample (not the number of samples, and not a total). \(\overline p = 600/(30 \times 500) = 0.04\). Half-width \(3\sqrt{0.04 \times 0.96/500} = 3\sqrt{0.0000768} = 3(0.008764) = 0.0263\). Limits \(0.04 \pm 0.0263\): (0.0137, 0.0663).
  2. (b) \(\overline c = 400/25 = 16\). Half-width \(3\sqrt{16} = 12\). Limits (4, 28). 31 > 28, so the new panel plots above the UCL: out of control (Western Electric rule 1).

p chart (0.0137, 0.0663), \(k\) = items per sample; c chart (4, 28); 31 flaws is out of control.

Q18. An \(\overline X\) chart has center line 50 and \(\sigma = 2\) (the SD of \(\overline X\)), so the 1σ lines are 48 and 52, the 2σ lines 46 and 54, and the 3σ limits 44 and 56. The first 16 subgroup means are:

Subgroup910111213141516
\(\overline X\)52.554.551.855.056.853.051.050.4

(a) [4] Using the Western Electric rules, at which subgroup is the process first declared out of control, and by which rule? (b) [1] If only the 3σ limits were used, when would it first be flagged?

Solution
  1. Convert to σ units (\(z = (\overline X - 50)/2\)): 0.3, 2.3, −0.4, 0.5, 1.2, 0.7, 1.4, 1.55, 1.25, 2.25, 0.9, 2.5, 3.4, 1.5, 0.5, 0.2.

  4. Rule 3 (4 of 5 above 52): window 5 to 9 holds 52.4, 51.4, 52.8, 53.1, 52.5; four of them are above 52. Fires at 9. (Earlier windows have at most 3.)
  5. Rule 4 (8 in a row above 50): subgroup 3 is below 50; subgroups 4 through 11 are all above. Fires at 11.
  6. (a) Earliest is subgroup 9, by rule 3. Subgroup 2 is a single point beyond 2σ, which is not a signal by itself.
  7. (b) Subgroup 13, the first point outside 44 to 56.

(a) Subgroup 9, rule 3. (b) Subgroup 13.

5 pts

Q19. A CUSUM chart uses \(\widehat\mu = 10.0\) and \(\widehat\sigma = 0.4\) (the SD of \(\overline X\)), with \(k = 1/2\) and \(h = 4\). The first five subgroup means are 10.3, 10.6, 10.1, 10.9, 10.8.

(a) [4] Compute \(\mathrm{SH}_i\) and \(\mathrm{SL}_i\) for \(i = 1\) to 5. At which subgroup is the process first out of control? (b) [1] Would an \(\overline X\) chart with 3σ limits have flagged any of these five points?

Solution
  1. Constants. \(k\sigma = 0.5 \times 0.4 = 0.2\). \(h\sigma = 4 \times 0.4 = 1.6\). Flag if \(\mathrm{SH}_i > 1.6\) or \(\mathrm{SL}_i < -1.6\) (the sheet's "\(\mathrm{SL}_i < h\sigma\)" is a typo for \(-h\sigma\)).
  2. Recursions. \(\mathrm{SH}_i = \max(0, \overline X_i - 10.2 + \mathrm{SH}_{i-1})\); \(\mathrm{SL}_i = \min(0, \overline X_i - 9.8 + \mathrm{SL}_{i-1})\).
\(i\)\(\overline X_i\)\(\overline X_i - 10.2\)\(\mathrm{SH}_i\)\(\overline X_i - 9.8\)\(\mathrm{SL}_i\)
110.30.10.10.50
210.60.40.50.80
310.1−0.10.40.30
410.90.71.11.10
510.80.61.71.00
  1. (a) \(\mathrm{SH}_5 = 1.7 > 1.6\): out of control at subgroup 5. SL stays at 0 because every mean is above \(\mu - k\sigma = 9.8\).
  2. (b) No. The 3σ limits are \(10.0 \pm 1.2 = (8.8, 11.2)\) and every point is inside. The CUSUM adds up a small, sustained upward shift that no single point reveals.

SH: 0.1, 0.5, 0.4, 1.1, 1.7; SL: all 0; out of control at subgroup 5; the \(\overline X\) chart flags nothing.

Review: Module 10

3 pts

Q20. (a) Define the average run length (ARL). (b) For an in-control process with 3σ limits, the ARL is about 370. What is every signal in that situation, and which hypothesis-testing error does it correspond to? (c) If the process mean shifts up by 1σ, does the ARL increase or decrease? What do we call it when the chart misses the shift?

Solution
  1. (a) The average number of subgroups plotted until (and including) the first out-of-control signal. It is \(1/P(\text{signal})\) for a single point.
  2. (b) Every signal is a false alarm: an in-control process declared out of control. That is a Type I error, with probability 0.0027 per point, so \(1/0.0027 \approx 370\).
  3. (c) It decreases (to about 44 for a 1σ shift; bigger shifts give smaller ARLs), but it can still take many subgroups. Missing a real shift is a failure to detect, a Type II error.

1 pt each.

Review: Module 10

5 pts

Q21. A part must measure between 4.0 and 5.0 mm. An in-control \(\overline X\) chart with subgroups of size \(n = 5\) gives \(\overline{\overline X} = 4.62\) and \(\overline S = 0.094\). (Use \(c_4 = 0.9400\) for \(n = 5\).)

(a) [1] Estimate \(\sigma\) for individual parts. (b) [2] Compute \(C_p\) and \(C_{pk}\). (c) [1] Is the process minimally capable? Is it of six-sigma quality? (d) [1] Without reducing variation, what single change would raise \(C_{pk}\), and to what value?

Solution
  1. (a) For capability, \(\sigma\) is the SD of ONE part: \(\widehat\sigma = \overline S/c_4 = 0.094/0.94 = 0.100\) mm. No \(\sqrt n\) here.
  2. (b) \(C_p = (5.0 - 4.0)/(6 \times 0.1) = 1/0.6 = 1.67\). \(C_{pk} = \min\big((4.62 - 4.0)/0.3,\ (5.0 - 4.62)/0.3\big) = \min(2.07, 1.27) = 1.27\). The mean sits closer to the USL, so that side sets \(C_{pk}\).
  3. (c) \(C_{pk} = 1.27 > 1\): minimally capable. \(C_p = 1.67 < 2\): not six-sigma quality.
  4. (d) Re-center the mean at the spec midpoint, 4.50 mm. Then \(C_{pk} = C_p = 1.67\).

\(\widehat\sigma = 0.100\); \(C_p = 1.67\), \(C_{pk} = 1.27\); minimally capable, not six sigma; center at 4.50 to get \(C_{pk} = 1.67\).

  • 75 to 89: reread the "Common mistakes" box and redo the check problems in each module where you lost 3 or more points.
  • Below 75: go back to the modules in the table for every question you missed, starting with the ones worth the most points. Then retake this exam without looking at the solutions.

If you see this on the exam

  • "Define p-value / level / power" → write the three definitions from Q1 word for word; include "assuming \(H_0\) is true" and "as or more extreme".
  • "State your conclusion" → "reject \(H_0\) and conclude there is evidence that [\(H_a\) in context]" or "fail to reject \(H_0\); [\(\theta_0\) in context] is plausible". Never "accept", never "prove".
  • One exponential datum → p-value \(e^{-x/\theta_0}\) (upper); \(y_{\rm RR} = -\theta_0\ln(1-q)\); power from the same boundary at the true \(\theta\); CI bound \(\theta = -y_{\rm obs}/\ln(1-q)\).
  • "Same unit measured twice" → paired; "two separate groups" → Welch. Chi-square: \(k\) = total count; check expected counts ≥ 5.
  • "2×2 table, one margin fixed" → Barnard. "One sample, two questions" → independence. "Group sizes fixed" → homogeneity.
  • Control limits → \(c_4\) from the Gamma lines; \(\sigma\) of a MEAN for charts and CUSUM, \(\sigma\) of an ITEM for \(C_p\), \(C_{pk}\).
  • "When is it first out of control?" → check all four Western Electric rules in time order; report the earliest completing point.
